the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work is a very good one. for the people who work in ports, this port directive is only causing problems. the european commission is opening up the possibility of self-regulation. this will allow skilled professionals who know how to move cargo safely and carefully to be replaced by low-paid crews from outside europe. even if the member states were to be exempted from this obligation, the directive would still be detrimental to ports where the port docks, quays and adjacent business premises are owned by the public authorities. the mandatory periodic tendering of operators may result in the people working there losing their jobs at the end of such a period. only in private ports will continuity be possible. at least, as long as they don't go bankrupt or get bought out by a competitor. this proposal has met with massive resistance. it was not until 2003 that it was finally rejected by this parliament at third reading. then, just before she left in 2004, former commissioner de palacio left the repetition of this proposal as a time bomb. a major motivation behind this may have been that there are stakeholders outside the ports who want to make shipping even cheaper. this is surprising because the tariffs in europe are lower than in asia or america and also because they represent only a negligible part of the production costs for industry. the official main argument of de palacio was to promote shipping between spain and italy, as an alternative to the large flow of trucks along the road along the southern coast of france. even without a port directive, this short sea transport has already shown a strong growth in recent years. in addition, the effect on german, dutch, belgian and french ports on the north sea and the channel with their large common hinterland would be chaos. as early as 1998, this parliament considered that a possible port directive should focus on the transparency of investments paid for with taxpayers' money and on the extent to which the charges charged cover the costs. in the two successive proposals from the european commission, this has not happened. nevertheless, until a week ago, the christian democrats and the liberals, that is to say half of this parliament, were in favour of this proposal. now, it seems that almost no one believes in it anymore. in the mood for tomorrow, we can finally defuse this time bomb.

president, commissioner, the sugar reform as it is now being proposed will have very negative consequences for poor developing countries and acp countries which are now losing access to the market or are getting a lower price for their products. any agricultural reform which is bad for developing countries is bad reform in my view. the reform is also detrimental to the incomes of thousands of farmers and sugar workers, without any proper social plan to counter it. that makes it a worthless reform. finally, this reform is not profitable for the european taxpayer, because it is budgetary neutral. these three facts together make it possible to speak of a disastrous reform. the european sugar policy must certainly be reformed, but not in a way that benefits only the large food producers.
mr president, the sugar reform is first and foremost a social problem facing farmers and workers in europe and the third world. social justice means opening up the european market on a global scale. this reform is inevitable and painful. but if you're gonna go through the fire, you better jump. the commissioner made a giant leap, the council made a slightly smaller leap and our parliament would like to make a slightly smaller leap. but the workers and farmers are worried and demand strong social guarantees. that's right. the restructuring fund is therefore indispensable. our pressure has resulted in a larger fund, a larger restructuring fund, and that is important. we'd like more. because workers want more than retraining, they want new jobs when their factory closes. bioethanol is a way out, provided its production is energy efficient. research into this will require more money. only in this way can we have a social future for the sector - a sustainable future - and only in this way can the sector accept this reform.
the application of the aarhus convention to the european institutions is a major step forward, as it contributes to the necessary transparency of european regulation. this will increase public participation in the preparation of environmental plans and programmes. this regulation strengthens democracy in the field of the environment. the aarhus provisions on access to information, participation in decision-making and access to justice in environmental matters will apply throughout the union. it is no longer only the member states who will have to comply with these provisions, but also the community institutions. access to justice, as provided for in the aarhus convention, implies that the public should have the opportunity to bring environmental decisions of the institutions of the european union before the european court of justice. at present, citizens with an environmental interest or environmental ngos have no chance of bringing an action before the court, because they are rarely directly and individually affected (the conditions for access to justice laid down in the ec treaty). this is often easier for economic actors to prove. of course it is important that ngos have the same access to justice, although there are some criticisms to be made of the way this has been framed by the european commission and the environment committee (in particular amendments 22 and 25). the amendments also impose strict conditions on ngos that can apply for internal review, in order to prevent many hundreds of ngos from having to go to court with minor complaints. but even with these conditions - which are here now and which the rapporteur proposes in particular - there will be many ngos who will be able to make such a request. it is not desirable that every environmental decision of the european institutions should be brought before the court of justice, because it creates legal uncertainty and will only add to the enormous workload of the european judiciary. on the other hand, where there are serious and justified doubts about a particular environmental decision, there should be the possibility of challenging it. in my group there are two ways of thinking: there is a section of my group which strongly supports amendments nos 22 and 25, because they believe that it will remove inequality, that it will give a better effect to the aarhus convention and that it will be in line with the ec treaty. another part of the group thinks the opposite, namely that inequality is being created because environmental ngos would be given a privileged position and that it would also be at least at odds with the treaty. i can only regret that there is no middle way to give ngos a voice when they really need it without the risk of frequently raising minor objections. i think it would be better if the treaty simply increased the possibility of access to justice (the constitutional treaty would have done that, but unfortunately that is no longer the case).
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. is it really necessary to establish standards for bathing water at european level? is this not a typical example of a matter which member states can do themselves? i know that my colleagues from the dutch social democrats, who are not here today, have even mentioned the shameful swimming pool directive in a report. it is not a directive on swimming pools, much less a directive on bathing water, but on the water of beaches and lakes. the question whether european action in this area would add value has been raised. we have rightly discussed this in this parliament at first reading and the european parliament did not share that doubt at the time. but more importantly, the member states, and above all the member states, felt that a directive was needed. if they think so, then there should be one, but i think it should be a serious directive and not one which is there but which is mainly about creating exceptions. so, after more than 12 years of discussion, a difficult compromise between the council and parliament has now been reached and i believe that it will be transposed into legislation tomorrow. the new directive will ensure cleaner bathing water on europe's beaches and lakes, better information for swimmers and less red tape for local authorities. across the european union, more than 80% of bathing water sites already comply with the higher standards, and in some member states, more than 90% of bathing water sites do so. currently, about 12 percent of swimmers suffer from nausea, stomach flu, rash, constipation, or an eye or ear infection each year. the new directive thus raises health standards, which will result in an estimated 8% of swimmers falling ill in 2015. from 2015, the swimmer will also be informed of the current bathing water quality at the bathing site itself, using symbols that will be identical throughout europe. now, as a swimming consumer, you can only see the quality of the previous year, and that with some effort and only on the internet. i'm excited to be able to see the water quality on the spot, because i don't know about you, but i rarely take my laptop to the beach. instead of 19 parameters, member states will have to measure 2 parameters and that is an example of how much less bureaucracy will be applied in measuring water. the european parliament and governments have long been at odds over the question of how strict the standards should be and how high the costs to the member states should be. we have reached a compromise which we can all be satisfied with and i think the british presidency deserves a compliment in that respect, but certainly the european commission and you, commissioner, have played an important role in bringing about the compromise and i would also like to thank many of the shadow rapporteurs for their cooperation in this. the bathing water is not just cleaner, it's much cleaner, the costs are controllable, and there's deregulation. i am, and i will conclude with this, mr president, looking forward to the commission's comments on the forthcoming article by mr wiedenmann in the scientific journal environmental health perspectives, in which he says in complicated english: concentration responsive effects with no observed adverse effect levels, abbreviated noaels. i can't exactly define the term itself, but perhaps you can. i am curious, however, whether the commission will include this study in its evaluation report in 2008, because there are quite a few pertinent comments on the directive as we now take it. i just think we can't really bring it into the timeline anymore. i shall be very pleased if the directive is actually adopted tomorrow. i think it's a success for all of us, including me, who's not exactly a keen swimmer. i only go in the water when i really have to. i will now, after 2015, at least enter the water with a little more peace of mind. i hope, commissioner, that i shall be happy to do so in your member state.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the european union. thanks to his efforts, we can adopt the revision of the bathing water directive tomorrow. i would also like to thank the european commission for its cooperation, because without the ambitious proposal it has made, it would not have been possible to achieve the tightening of the criteria. i can tell you, mr president, that i will therefore be voting in favour of the outcome of the conciliation tomorrow, even though i am not entirely satisfied with the result achieved. the category of 'acceptable' that has been created is nothing more than a shame sheet for the lack of serious tightening of standards. the separation between fresh and salt water is also based on very limited information, and this all leads to a much lower level of protection for swimmers than would otherwise be possible. i would like to draw the attention of the commission and the council to the article which has also been mentioned by jules maaten and which will be published next month. the article makes clear what standards we need to have to really protect swimmers. i hope, commissioner dimas, that you will use this information in the next review, which i hope will not take too long.
mr schüssel, first of all, welcome. the austrian presidency will be marked by two major themes: the failed constitutional process and the services directive. i can be very simple about the constitution. he's dead, you know. that is what the dutch people think and that is what the french people think. two member states have rejected the constitution and that is why it is out of date. the sooner the european union admits this, the sooner we can begin the necessary reforms: reducing eu interference in matters of national responsibility, ending money laundering and fraud in europe and genuinely democratising the eu. the services directive. austria wants a compromise between the different camps. but if a compromise is sought between multinationals on the one hand and trade unions on the other, it will always mean a deterioration of working conditions in europe. moreover, no compromise can be reached on the core of the proposal, the principle of the country of origin. my party and my group reject competition on terms of employment or social dumping as the future of europe. so we reject the proposed directive. europe did not want a constitution; europe does not want this services directive. the austrian presidency could make history by being the first to really listen to what the people want. but if we choose to force a constitution and this services directive down people's throats, this will only strengthen the resistance to european cooperation.
the president, chancellor, you can be sure that after a presidency marked by stagnation, we have all too high expectations of the austrian presidency of the council. it is up to you, chancellor, to draw up a report on the future of europe, based on the report to be produced during your term of office. the european parliament has been a very active participant in the process of european integration. it is also about europe's future prosperity, growth and employment. the council must set priorities so that the lisbon strategy can be put into practice. this can also strengthen globalisation has not been met, nor have measures been taken to re-establish the european social model in a society which is changing, a society which is becoming silver, a society which is becoming greener, a society in which borders are no longer borders. chancellor, may your presidency be more than just pretty words. let us work together to reinterpret our european social model and then let us certainly also for christian democrats the foundation of a society based on people. and i admit that in my own circle, but also in the socialist circle, free market thinking is gaining ground and that in writing. the majority of the european parliament is putting the functioning of the european union at risk by throwing the council's common position on the financial perspective for 2007-2013 into the trash. we disagree with that. firstly, the member states collect the contributions for the european union. parliament is putting on too big a pants when it wants to force member states to make more money available to the eu. secondly, in the light of the principle of subsidiarity, there are items in the european budget for which much less or no money should be spent: the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the programme. furthermore, eu propaganda spending and subsidies to 'pro-european' think tanks serve no useful purpose. thirdly, cohesion and structural funds policy should focus on the less-favoured regions in those member states whose gdp is less than 80% of the eu average. the logical consequence of this is that structural support policy is shifting from the southern to the eastern member states. the european parliament has to be more resourceful in the interests of citizens and limit the eu to its core tasks. in short: doing more with less!
in writing. to be very clear: i do not reject the european council's common position. however, the european parliament, as one arm of the legislative and budgetary authority, should be able to play its full parliamentary role in the definition of policies, their reform and their budget. i therefore support the willingness of this house to enter into constructive negotiations with the council.
in writing. although the sp is satisfied with the reduction in the eu budget, and in particular with the improvement in the dutch payment position, we are still very critical of the way the remaining money is spent. the skewed distribution of agricultural subsidies, the wasteful diversion of funds and the promotion of prestige projects have not yet been eliminated. the fact that the budgetary agreement is now the one that is hurting the really poor member states is intolerable.
in writing. today the european parliament adopted a resolution on europe's multiannual budget. i welcome the compromise reached by the council in december 2005 and see it as a good starting point for further negotiations between the european parliament and the council. the successful efforts of the dutch government through minister zalm van financiën remain intact as far as i am concerned. the council's decision to give the netherlands a rebate on its 1 billion contribution to the eu is outside the competence of parliament and will therefore not be part of the negotiations. i believe that europe needs to invest more in knowledge, innovation and entrepreneurship. i do not see that reflected enough in the present compromise. i also believe that the european parliament, like any national parliament, must take its responsibility in the adoption of the budget. the negotiations which the european parliament opens with the council are similar to the debate which the house of commons has with the cabinet on the occasion of prince's day.
in writing. the subject of this resolution, the quality of bathing water, is important, but should in my opinion, from the viewpoint of subsidiarity, be dealt with at the national level. there is no added value in regulating this subject at the european level. for this reason i vote against this resolution.
in writing. in a society based on democratic values, i believe that every party should be granted equal rights. it therefore includes equal, non-discriminatory access to justice for citizens, businesses and ngos. article 230 (4) of the treaty already provides for this and is therefore sufficient. that is why i thought i should vote against amendments nos 19, 22, 24 and 25.
in writing. port services must be covered by a legislative framework taking into account their specificity. a community framework would allow equitable access to port activities. furthermore an integrated policy is necessary to facilitate the development of european ports. it would also be the opportune moment for due consideration of the role of the managing body of the port as well as the operating methods of the port. in addition, very clear clarification on state aid rules is essential in order to guarantee transparent and fair competition between european ports. decisions on a case-by-case basis, which would lead to a 'gouvernement des juges', where the two legislative bodies of the european union are left aside, are not acceptable. the european commission's proposal was not sufficiently complete to gain support. following the rejection of this proposal, and for all reasons mentioned, i urge the european commission to enter into a vast consultation process with all parties concerned, also giving voice to those parties neglected until now and representing the demand side, and to propose an ambitious white paper on european seaports policy in its entirety, and this at the earliest possible opportunity.
in writing. i voted against the directive because we could only vote for or against the original commission proposal. the proposal for a new port directive is lacking in many respects. a substantial package of amendments has been drawn up by the rapporteur, mr jarzembowski, but unfortunately there was insufficient support for them. we must continue the debate on a european port policy in the broad sense, which is why i have asked the commissioner to come up with a discussion paper, which hopefully will be a good one.
in writing. the dutch delegation of the ind/dem group will support the uen group resolution on homophobia. the dutch delegation of the ind/dem group will withdraw its support for the resolutions of the ppe-de, alde, greens/efa and gue/ngl and the joint motion for a resolution on homophobia in the european union. the dutch delegation can agree with the uen group resolution because only this resolution makes it clear that policy measures in the field of discrimination are the responsibility of the member states. the principle of subsidiarity should be applied here. we also reject calls for homophobia and violence against people on the grounds of their sexual orientation. we believe that policies aimed at preventing discrimination on grounds of religion, race and sexual orientation are a matter for the member states. the resolutions of the various political groups go beyond this principle by explicitly addressing the political situation in a number of member states.
the tone of the report by mr duff and mr voggenhuber reminds me very much of the debate in the house a year ago. the european parliament then embraced the constitution with great fanfare, and nothing seemed to stand in the way of a successful ratification by all 25 member states. i am disappointed to see that the ep has not learned the lesson of the double no. this house is not thinking about the future of europe, but about a strategy to get the twice rejected constitution ratified. all this, of course, under the guise of considerable measures to reassure public opinion. in the face of these transparent attempts to ignore the express wishes of the french and dutch electorate, i would nevertheless like to express my appreciation for some of the amendments tabled by the two rapporteurs. at least they are not obsessively adhering to the current constitution. moreover, they seem to have understood the 'no' in france and the netherlands better than my colleagues from the large groups and they show a greater willingness to give it real thought. i have heard my colleague, mr voggenhuber, speak of nationalism. i think his intellectual capacity is so high that he does not understand all the opponents of this constitution, because i really have nothing to do with it.
president, the constitution is dead, long live the treaty. it is true that the constitutional treaty was rejected because of the context more than because of the text, but it is also true that the text of the constitutional treaty proved too weak to resolve the problems of the context. there were insufficient or no bold and appealing ideas. why do we not give the citizens real influence and let them, for example, directly elect the president of the european commission or create the possibility of a european corrective referendum? so, not 25 or 27 small referendums, but a real european referendum, or why not create a real european political space, a european res publica. but then about that context. the citizens of my country, the netherlands, and i suspect most other member states, do not want to talk about the institutions, thank you very much. what they want is a europe that is competitive, that creates jobs, that takes action against terrorism and crossborder crime, that has a proper foreign policy, that is simply doing its job. if that fails, it is like trying to revive a treaty revision, pulling a dead horse and talking in vain. we need a new treaty to make europe more democratic and more efficient, but we are in no hurry. the many excellent ideas put forward by our rapporteurs include extending the period of reflection until the end of 2007, keeping several options open, and also, in general, an approach on how we should conduct the debate which i think is excellent. but what we don't need is a document called a constitution that has scared people off, instead of engaging them; a rose smells just as good if you call it something else. the fact that the name and the text are being obstinately and obstinately adhered to is therefore an insurmountable problem for my party. if the solution is to put forward an improved text - and i really hope we succeed in doing so, because it is important - then i am in favour of putting it before all european citizens in a referendum at the same time as the next european elections.
president. - there are two very different forms of neighbourhood policy. the first is based on our own self-interest, on attempts to make the environment serve economic and military desires developed within the european union. we are satisfied with neighbouring countries that supply us with oil and gas, that keep refugees out of our territory and that subject their own people to dictatorial measures designed to prevent our interests from being harmed. in such a model, neighbourhood policy is also a means of keeping those areas with a lower standard of living, with less democracy and with fewer human rights permanently out of the rich fortress of europe. these countries are only called upon to provide cheap labour for a limited period of time. such a neighborhood would be very similar to the way the united states has dealt with latin america for two centuries. there is also a very different kind of neighbourhood policy. it is based on the assumption that we have done a lot of things better in this part of europe than in other countries and that we have something to offer others. this includes the promotion of democracy and human rights, including in belarus, tunisia, algeria and the still occupied palestinian territories. it also means that we are contributing to economic development, which should bring these countries to a level of prosperity similar to ours. in some cases, notably for the western balkans, but possibly also for ukraine, belarus, moldova and turkey, it may also represent a transition phase towards full membership of the european union. my group rejects the first form of neighbourhood policy entirely, but can wholeheartedly support the second model. fortunately, the second variant is the dominant one in mr tannock's report, and most of the amendments also favour democracy, human rights and solidarity over economic and military advantage. if the council and the commission follow this line, our neighbourhood policy is going in the right direction.
in his interesting report mr tannock rightly draws attention to the current situation in the eu's neighbour belarus. in particular, in paragraph 56 it urges all the european institutions to strengthen support for civil society and the country's political opposition. in this context, commissioner, i would like to ask what progress has been made with the brussels plans to establish a delegation in minsk. the commission's proposal is to be accompanied by a report on the implementation of the european union's external relations policy. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the establishment of a european union agency for fundamental rights. it is also a prerequisite for the eventual establishment of an active neighbourhood with belarus. i fear that the present authoritarian regime of president lukashenko has little regard for this. it probably wants a toothless european delegation on its own territory as an international credential. commissioner, does this attitude of minsk prevent the european delegation from coming to the belarusian capital? the rapporteur, mr tannock, is equally keen to see a political settlement of the long-standing transnistrian issue. it therefore calls on all parties concerned to do so. unfortunately for moldova, two of these parties - russia and ukraine - issued a joint statement on the transnistrian conflict just a month ago - in mid-december 2005 - without mentioning chişinău. president putin and president yushchenko did not inform the united states, the european union or romania of this. the eu has also been active in the fight against terrorism and the fight against organised crime. the repetition of an earlier move by yevgeni primakov in 1997, which received no international support and was explicitly rejected by moldova. in passing, putin and yushchenko now jointly claim responsibility for 'peace, stability and the well-being of the people of moldova, for russians and ukrainians on both sides of the dniester'. that simply implies a russian-ukrainian condominium. at the same time, on 15 december 2005, kiev and moscow accepted each other's solutions to the transnistrian issue as "complementary". the commission has already made a number of proposals, including the one on the right, which i think is the sealing of a state division of moldova, the international legitimization of russia's military presence in transnistria and the establishment of a 'democratic neutral state of moldova'. commissioner, how do you interpret this bilateral agreement between putin and yevchenko on moldova's future in the context of the european neighbourhood policy? thank you. i look forward to the answer to my two questions.
on behalf of the gue/ngl group. mr president, rapporteur onesta has tried to strike a balance between indignation at the actions of some members and guarantees of freedom of expression. the positive aspect is that members who are punished do not lose their right to vote and can appeal. i think, however, that this proposal may prove counterproductive. it is an invitation to members of parliament to make the news by calling for criminal measures against themselves and thereby playing the role of martyrs to the public. it also tempts fearful presidents to be unnecessarily harsh with people they do not consider their friends. moreover, any incident will lead to calls to rule out any unforeseen annoyances by even stricter rules. disruptive debates and the visible display of dissent are part of normal parliamentary practice. the european parliament is making a fool of itself if it is excluded from a number of meetings or even suspended as a member. the media then report less on the content of the debate and decision-making and more on the lack of tolerance. that is the wrong signal to the voters, and therefore it would be better if such a regulation were limited to preventing and punishing physical violence.
on behalf of the ind/dem group president, good news from iraq?! that sounds almost politically out of line. however, i would argue against that on the basis of my own recent experience, and especially if i hear any noises in here. in the middle of november last year i had the privilege of participating as a member of the european parliament in a major international conference on the democratization of the middle east, which was also addressed by mr mcmillan-scott. the scene was arbil, the capital of the autonomous kurdistan region in northern iraq. in complete peace, in complete security and openness, scientists from europe discussed with kurdish government and parliament members the prospects and problems of the emergence of democratic states based on the rule of law in the wider region. so, fortunately, iraq is far from being just the depressing scene of relentlessly gruesome terrorist attacks. it is precisely in iraqi kurdistan that the european union has a unique opportunity today to make a substantial contribution to the urgent task of overall reconstruction of the two-stream country. i would like to ask the commissioner if she shares this view and, if so, what initiatives the european commission is already taking or is preparing to take in northern iraq. at the same time, i address this house: kurdish colleagues in iraq look forward to contacts with the european parliament. democratisation of the middle east? let us not confine ourselves to recommendations on paper, but give them personal content through concrete political relations!
the european parliament has a duty to ensure that the european union is able to take action in the fight against racism and xenophobia. in developing countries, the percentage is often even higher, as mr bowis has already said. when we talk about disabled people in developing countries, we're talking about people who are both physically and mentally disabled, both blind people and children with war trauma, so hundreds of millions of people. in developing countries, there are often no facilities and disabled people often live in isolation. unesco estimates that 98% of children with disabilities in those countries are not in school. the numbers speak for themselves. special policy attention is needed for this group. the millennium development goals are also particularly difficult to achieve if we leave such a large proportion of the population behind. the disabled do not automatically benefit from poverty reduction programmes, and there is no mainstreaming in this area. the problems were clear, mr president, and they are clear, and the question is what we do about them. as mentioned, in 2003 the commission prepared a guidance note for all delegations and services. this gave disabled people a place in development policy. my question to the commission is: - and the commission has already said how it is being dealt with, namely mainly through political dialogue - is there anything more to be said about the results? are there more specific measures targeted at the disabled? have practical solutions been found to certain problems, for example in the field of education? education is the key to combating poverty and social exclusion, which affect the majority of the disabled. are disabled people themselves involved in the formulation and implementation of programmes? in the netherlands this is being done and the results are very good. finally, what is the commission doing to exchange and pool knowledge in this field?
in writing. chechnya was conquered by the russian empire in the 19th century, its population was temporarily deported by stalin, and more recently, under yeltsin and putin, it has been murdered and bombed. the commitment made by russia after the first war in the 1990s, through lebed, the peace negotiator, to allow a democratic choice for independence after a transitional period, has never been fulfilled. the chechens must therefore no longer be expected to consider themselves part of russia. no people wants to submit to its most brutal murderers and oppressors. the resistance will therefore continue indefinitely, and i fear its suppression by a military superpower. this resolution is relatively mild about the intimidation and the falsification of the elections held in the meantime, which were supposed to give a majority to moscow's puppets. the text also reaffirms the territorial integrity of russia, whereas the retention of this territory in russia has been based on pure violence for a century and a half. in the present hopeless situation, this resolution, which rightly points out the many abuses, is better than remaining silent about the misdeeds of our great energy supplier. that's why i'm voting for it.
president. - i have a brief explanation of vote on the voggenhuber/duff report. this is a good report. the main thrust is that europe must move forward, and this is important, because even those who voted no in the referendums were not satisfied with the europe we have today. the report states that support among the population is essential and that there are several options open, and that is also important, because the referendums are a fact, the negative referendums in france and the netherlands and the positive referendums in spain and luxembourg. i am very pleased that the current text also includes amendment no 18, which emphasizes that the concerns of citizens in countries such as france and the netherlands must be addressed. this is a clean sweep. i would like to thank the rapporteur for his report, which is a very good report, because the main thrust of it is that we must move forward, that we must have the courage to break out of the impasse, but that we do so with a number of open options; the commission has already taken the necessary steps to ensure that the european parliament is able to express its views on the matter. that seems to me to be a sound line and that is why we, as the cda delegation, have wholeheartedly supported this step forward.
in writing. the pause for reflection on the european constitution has so far been characterised by silence rather than debate. with this resolution parliament is making a worthy attempt to smooth the debate. but the resolution also expresses the hope that, as a result of the pause, the current text of the constitution can be preserved. that is, in my view, illusory and undesirable. this does not justify the rejection of the constitution by the french and dutch electorate. unfortunately, parliament has ignored the warnings of its rapporteurs, mr duff and mr voggenhuber, on this point. that is why i abstained on the final vote on the resolution. the time for reflection must be used to agree on the most democratic possible procedure for a new revision of the treaty. the french and dutch 'no' to the constitution was certainly not a 'yes' to the present europe.

in writing. the constitution rejected by the electorate in the netherlands and france was a two-part piece. most voters are in favour of the opening up of council meetings, a subsidiarity test for national parliaments or the weak call for a popular initiative in article 47. they could have been introduced long ago, even without a constitution. much more controversial were the economic and military policy choices that this text would cement, such as free and undistorted competition as the eu's main objective. the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the situation in the community. in a couples' sale, good points were held hostage by bad ones. the two rapporteurs want to maintain this link and believe that the delay in approval is only temporary. they even want to stop admitting new member states until that constitution is finally adopted. my party, the socialist party, is the largest party in the netherlands to have successfully campaigned against this constitutional text. after the rejection by the electorate, we have put forward in the dutch parliament proposals for publicity, subsidiarity and popular initiative, as mr brok has recommended here on behalf of the afet committee. i had proposed the same alternative in amendment no 6 which was rejected. a missed opportunity!
author. president, the last two presidents of peru, fujimori and toledo, have taken on the role of miracle worker as saviors of the country. in this way they have each convinced the electorate once and then disappointed them. fujimori has even taken office openly against parliamentary democracy and the wishes of the electorate. that in itself is a good reason to punish him and not give him another chance at leadership. much less convincing is the effort to get rid of him because of his japanese heritage. this is reminiscent of the method used earlier against former zambian president nkomo, who was not allowed to run again after a previous defeat on the grounds of his ancestry. fujimori fled to japan after his crimes and tried to return through chile. apparently he had the illusion that the masses in peru wanted a dictator as their leader and that they would want to put him back in power. now, all over latin america, people are electing a better kind of president. the miscalculation of fujimori provides an excellent opportunity to make it clear that former presidents can also be punished for their abuse of power. we rightly insist on a trial in peru.
author. mr president, a court in the cambodian capital phnom penh last month sentenced in absentia opposition leader sam rainsy to a year and a half in prison for insulting prime minister hun sen and prince norodom ranariddh and also ordered mr sam to pay fines of 20 million riel, or over 4,000 euros, for allegedly denigrating his political rivals. the cambodian government also arrested two human rights activists earlier this month: kem sokha, chairman of the cambodian center for human rights and yeng virak, director of the community legal education center. they were arrested in connection with the display of a banner at the meeting on the occasion of international human rights day on 10 december. after meeting with christopher hill, an american diplomat, the prime minister agreed to release on bail kem sokha and pa nguon teang on january 17, but the charges of libel against them and a number of others who have been released are not being dropped. the conviction of sam rainsy and the arrest of the human rights activists are just a few cases in a whole series of libel and indictments against public figures in the last two years. the lifting of parliamentary immunity and the conviction of cheam channy and, by default, chea poch. it is a return, mr president, to the days when hun sen led a one-party state. the developments signal a new setback and a reversal in the democratization process in south-east asia. dissenting opinions and statements should be challenged through public debate rather than court cases, and the arrest of dissenting voices in cambodia is a serious threat to freedom of expression and political pluralism in cambodia. we must oppose this new attempt by the current authorities to eliminate the opposition and turn cambodia into a glorified dictatorship.
author. - mr president, cambodia was a peaceful neighbour of vietnam when outside intervention began in the early 1970s. in vietnam, a war was raging over the reunification of the northern and southern sectors of this former french colony, where america wanted to keep the south as its own sphere of influence. the suspicion that the vietnamese guerrilla movement was using supply routes to connect the north and south, which ran through inhospitable parts of cambodia, was the reason for american intervention and the bringing to power of a friendly government. since then, relations in the once peaceful cambodia have become highly polarized. the hard-won state power must not be compromised in any way. there is a tradition of parties not wanting to form coalitions with others and not wanting to allow opposition. first there was an american-friendly regime, then the pro-chinese terror regime of pol pot and then, with vietnamese help, the hun sen government. since then, elections have produced results that have made it almost impossible to form governments with broad support. different parties do not want to cooperate with each other, but only want to govern alone. this attitude has now led to the arrest and imprisonment of opposition members of parliament. journalists, human rights activists and trade unionists are also arrested after being accused of defamation. the police and the judiciary are thus made into instruments of political struggle. i do not yet have the impression that the opposition is much more democratic than the ruling party. all political forces in cambodia will have to learn to stop this kind of behaviour. the outside world has so far failed to help cambodia develop a tolerant democracy. i remember that the defeated, bloodthirsty regime of pol pot retained international recognition for a long time. the only reason for this was that the new government of hun sen, which had freed the country from these gangs of murderers, was suspected of having friendly relations with the neighbouring country of vietnam. future signals to cambodia will have to be very different. europe must not take sides on the basis of considerations incomprehensible to the cambodians, but must consistently stand up for human rights and democracy, regardless of who is in power.

president. - as an observer of the european parliament at the palestinian elections just a week ago, one giant election banner caught my attention. there, in ramallah, i saw ayatollah khomeini pictured with hamas leaders yassin and rantisi. the political message is clear: no reconciliation, no peace with the jewish state, but its erasure from the page of history. wise words from khomeini, so judges the current iranian president ahmadinejad. add to that the utterly unbelievable peaceful nuclear aspirations of the mullah regime in tehran and the test for the western world, eu and us, is signed. the historian dan diner rightly commented on the palestinian parliamentary elections with the words "with hamas in ramallah, iran has moved much closer to israel". many western-minded iranians, as i read from tehran these days, regard this development as the gradual triumph of barbarism over civilization. i sincerely hope that the european institutions do not share this resignation, but stand firmly for the right of israel to exist and thus for our own civilization.
we have listened attentively to mr solana's speech, mr president, and particularly to the beginning of his explanation of what happened in january. i hope that this will not be a pattern for the rest of the year and that we will have to see every month that new elements are being added to the foreign policy agenda. i myself found his main point to be the remarks he made, particularly at the beginning, on the situation in europe following the problems with gas supplies to ukraine and other countries. and i think that energy policy has been put much higher on our agenda because of what happened in early january. i draw a number of conclusions from these recent events. firstly, i think that we as consumers may not have been properly organised. our power supply is vulnerable, it seems. too vulnerable, because the consequences of an unexpected energy shortage can be catastrophic. secondly, i note that some member states are more vulnerable than others, and that is a bit contrary to the idea of solidarity that we know within the european union. and there are certainly vulnerable countries in our immediate vicinity, as has been shown, and the need for mutual solidarity, including with our neighbours, is therefore clear. it is also a technical matter, of course, and we can invest in better and more diversified lines of communication to ourselves. the third point i want to make here, and this is the most important, is that energy resources and their availability are being used as a political weapon, in this case against ukraine, but also against moldova and, in fact, also against georgia. we have warned of this in the past. the russians have threatened to do that before and i think we have to be very tough on russia on that point; i would like to say that we have to be careful not to become so dependent on russia that we are not able to say anything to russia at some point, to keep our mouths shut because we have become too dependent. finally, a final comment on this year's agenda. that agenda is covering itself. the european union cannot shirk its responsibility. i would ask the council and the commission in particular to consider how it is that after such successes as in georgia, serbia and ukraine, things are now threatening to go the other way. did we make mistakes? i think that should also be a subject of analysis.
president. - mr president, for some people, including in europe, cuba is paradise. in the eyes of others, it's hell. the european parliament has been a very active participant in the process of european integration. for almost half a century, the same movement has been in power with the same leader. the inhabitants are not free to travel abroad, nor to accept any prize awarded to them. opposition parties have little chance of organising, and even if they do, they do not have the chance to determine in elections how large or small the proportion of the population that supports them is. as in the great neighbouring country, the united states, there are death sentences, which are negative points that can and should be criticised. with all this criticism of cuba, i resist the idea that this is a dictatorship. this country has high expectations and has been an example for the rest of latin america for years in the fields of education, health care and other public services and the protection of the most vulnerable groups of the population. in terms of democracy and human rights, it was certainly no worse than the rest of what latin america was then. it is still the inspiration for the modernization supported by a majority of the voters in venezuela, brazil, bolivia, chile, argentina and uruguay. europe has never followed the north american isolation of cuba, and rightly so. much of what is wrong with cuba is being promoted by that isolation. if we want to improve cuba, we need an open relationship.
mr president, for the first time in 2005, castro and roque spoke personally about cuba after castro. in the meantime, the cuban people are tired of the struggle to stay afloat, and the small groups of cubans who dare to set up their own political or social activities are factually denied a life. europe must speak with one voice in havana. clearly, against the serious violations of human rights, clearly, in favour of a dialogue with the authorities on political and economic reforms which must prevent the loss of what was gained in health care and education in the first years of the revolution. but it also ends the totalitarian regime and the blockade of economic reforms. only then can cubans, through their own initiatives, agriculture and small markets, look forward to better times, without violence and not from miami. europe must now think about offering cuba a social, economic and democratic perspective after castro. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. we must be firm in our commitment to human rights, firm in our commitment to a dialogue rich in new perspectives. it is up to castro and roque to show their courage and enter into dialogue. i was there in january, and i send you my warm greetings on behalf of the "damas de blanco" and they are very happy with the prize we have given them.
the resolution we are discussing tonight speaks for itself. it is a direct consequence of the 2003 discharge resolution by mr terry wynn. it is important that we have better control over eu funds. we all know that the court of auditors has not issued a positive das, a positive certificate of reliability, for the last 11 years. the commission is to be commended for having adopted almost entirely the recommendations of parliament. that's a good thing. it is a pity that the member states think differently and think much more negatively about it. that is why this evening is mainly a debate with the council. i have to tell you frankly that i am not very convinced by the answers, by mr winkler's remarks this evening. in fact, what we are calling for in this resolution is not so different from what is already happening in some cases. we are returning to the previous statement in which we wanted the highest political authority to sign it. we also accept lower-level statements in this resolution; if we look at the current practice of agricultural expenditure, there are already certification bodies which independently monitor the paying agencies and carry out independent audits. it is a mystery to me why the member states can accept it for agricultural expenditure but cannot accept it for other budgetary expenditure such as structural expenditure. the debate tonight is not without obligation. i assume that the council is also aware of the böge resolution on the financial perspective. we are not only asking for more money, after what the council has decided, we are also asking for better financial control and that better financial control is going in exactly the direction of the resolution which we hope to adopt tomorrow.
i would like to thank mr jacques toubon for his efforts and his approach, but i would also like to thank the commission and commissioner verheugen for the way in which the commission has put forward a proposal to improve regulation and deregulation in this case. it is therefore a little strange to us that parliament should now be making a lot of exceptions; we, and i come from a country, the netherlands, which rejected the constitutional treaty in a referendum, believe that europe should not interfere with the size of coffee, with the size of milk, because we have an internal market for that and we have mutual recognition of sizes and products. we are therefore also very unhappy with the additional exceptions or products which are now being included in order to be eligible for harmonisation. we shall therefore vote against them, and if they cannot be voted down and we cannot stick to the commission's proposal, then we as liberals will vote against the whole proposal, which we would actually regret, because in the end this could be an example; i hope, mr president, that we are not faced with this now and that i have to go back to my country to say, in the next referendum on a constitutional treaty, yes, indeed, we as a parliament are meddling with details, which you have to do in the street. the legislation, mr president, already clearly indicates to the consumer what a unit of measure costs per litre or per kilogram. the price already indicates that, so i think we should not underestimate the european citizen, we should not treat them as children or as inferior citizens. i think we have to have faith in the individual, that they can make their own choices, we have to give those choices. secondly, mr president, i would regret that when i visit countries in europe as a tourist or in other ways, i cannot enjoy the contemporary measures that prevail there, for example the pint in the united kingdom. i regret, mr president, that we have not opted for better regulation and deregulation as proposed by the commission. we therefore also regret the proposals for exceptions.
on behalf of the pse group. – mr president, we are right to be concerned about the european union’s communication problems and we are right, as the title of this white paper puts it, to be debating europe and to be involving people. the diagnosis is clear and the white paper is detailed in its prescriptions for dealing with the problem. i wish to thank the commissioner warmly for the efforts she has invested in taking us this far in addressing this crucial problem. we will be discussing many of the group proposals in detail later. i would like to use this opportunity to make some general remarks about communications in general. in communications less is more. the essence of good communication is to put a strong message in simple language. we need to explain to the people what we are doing and why it matters to them. once they know that their interests are affected by our decisions, they will take care of the public sphere themselves. the white paper seeks investment in mechanisms, procedures and new technology to facilitate the channels of communication. that is a good thing, but it does not address the quality of what is to be sent down those channels of communication. it talks about connecting the citizens and about public-sphere debating. that is acceptable to us. however, before people do any of this, they have to care. only then can they be empowered. we have to show them why they should care about what the european union is doing. the responsibility in dealing with those problems lies with us all – all the institutions – and not only with the press room downstairs. partnership is, therefore, also an important concept put forward in the white paper. as i said, it would be good to invest in new websites and our own tv channels, but we already have the world’s largest group of accredited correspondents on our doorstep: 1300 journalists who are hungry to report good-quality stories to audiences all over the world. if we produced timely and newsworthy stories for journalists, then the headlines, websites, new tv channels and public-sphere debates will follow. however, the reality is that most of our communication efforts are foundering because the eu fails on the basic issues. the eu offers one-size-fits-all news announcements instead of making more strenuous efforts to find new messages to capture the interest of specific media groups in our different countries and regions. quite rightly, the white paper places some emphasis on opinion polling and the need to understand european public opinion. but if it uses this resource correctly, why does the commission persist in coming forward with such deeply unpopular proposals like the port services directive that we dealt with a month ago? the white paper talks about the need to listen to the people, and rightly so. here was a case of the commission not even listening to its own parliament, never mind the people. on that point, it is not enough to say that europe needs to listen to the people: it also needs to act on what they say. what is the incentive for people to talk to us in brussels if they have no assurance that what they say will matter or change what we are thinking of doing? i welcome this white paper because it is kick-starting a much-needed debate. this house should welcome the commissioner’s important contribution. we in the european parliament must play our part in ensuring that the european union develops with the support of its citizens and that it serves their interests in the best possible way.
in the past, we could have regulated the protection of road safety and road transport workers differently from one member state to another. the struggle of interest groups for an ever-increasing improvement of this protection was directed at the national legislators. today there is much more international freight traffic over distances of hundreds and sometimes thousands of kilometres. this makes the differences between national regulations a detrimental factor in the competition. companies that have their employees work long hours in a row seem to have a strong advantage. they often fail to take into account that this advantage is nullified by accidents in which lives, vehicles and cargo are damaged or lost altogether. centralization of decision-making is often not in the interests of organisations seeking to protect labour, the environment and safety. it is particularly strengthening the position of those who, against public opinion, want to emphasize business interests, entrepreneurial freedom and endless economic growth. we see this in the case of driving times and rest periods. workers may be forced to work more hours than just driving hours. the commission has also proposed that the minimum working time for the express services be reduced from 12 hours to 11 hours. the monitoring of compliance remains limited. a better arrangement would have been possible by now. it will have to be brought back on the agenda in the near future. the commission has also taken note of the fact that the council has not yet adopted a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens. in the short term, this compromise will be a small improvement, especially since it will also apply to lorries from countries outside the european union and also thanks to the better controllable digital tachograph. that is a reason to be positive, despite serious objections.
mr. president, we have come a long way. my colleague anne jensen has said all that is necessary on this subject. the aim was to update, clarify and, above all, simplify the existing rules on driving and rest periods. all this is in the interests of fair competition between road hauliers, road safety and the social interests of road hauliers. however, earlier texts seemed to have missed this goal entirely. it was not sufficiently recognised that the crux of the matter was not so much the rules on driving and rest times but rather the enforcement of those rules and the different interpretations of them. i have no doubt that the commission will be able to do so. his commitment and involvement was great and i want to thank him for this. the fact that the agreement we are voting on today is considered to be somewhat acceptable by the liberals too is, and i am not often asked to say this, largely due to the council's perseverance in the negotiations. so i am clearly not in agreement with mr piecyk. the usefulness and necessity of some of the provisions of the present agreement may still be called into question. the possibility of splitting up the daily rest period - 3 plus 9 - is extremely limited in my view, and the weekly rest period of 45 hours every two weeks certainly does not contribute to the much needed flexibility in road transport. i am not sure that such rigid provisions would in any way contribute to road safety or better working conditions. the present agreement is therefore not perfect, but acceptable. at least the sector now knows what it's up against. the daily rest period has, thank god, been limited to 11 hours. the direct link to the working time control has been removed. the shortened weekly rest period can be spent in the vehicle and then, finally, there is clarity on the date of entry of the digital tachograph. in short, not perfect but acceptable. i shall therefore vote in favour of the motion.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rules on the protection of workers from harmful substances are respected. in the course of these 20 years, the legislation has acquired a unique position as the basis for the transport patterns of companies. therefore, where possible, adverse effects of changes should be limited. during the negotiations, a successful outcome has been uncertain for a long time. i think the current agreement is defensible. the main points of advantage for me are, on the one hand, that a number of elements have not been changed. i am referring in particular to the 11 hours of daily rest and the disregard of directive 2002/15/ec. on the other hand, the final date of introduction of the digital tachograph is also a profit. this will remove much uncertainty about the use of this instrument and hopefully allow more efficient monitoring. the strengthening of the control rules will contribute to the better functioning of this regulation. the same interpretation and application of the rules in each country is essential for the proper functioning of the internal market. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the establishment of a european agency for the evaluation of medicinal products for human use. all in all, a tighter regime will be established. this by definition means that flexibility in implementation is reduced. i am convinced, however, that the agreements now in force will contribute positively to a healthy and stable sector. this is in the interests of all concerned. finally, mr president, i would like to thank all those involved, and in particular the rapporteur, for their commitment and perseverance.
the agreement on driving and rest periods agreed by the council and parliament last december is acceptable to us, mr president, even if it does not deserve a beauty award. the result of the conciliation was initially unacceptable to the rapporteur, but from my point of view it was an improvement on the result of the vote in the european parliament's plenary. a workable break system - 11 hours instead of 12 - and a driver who can decide where he wants to sleep instead of the european parliament dictating it. president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0173/97). it is really too detailed here and there and, above all, the possibilities for multi-day bus transport are too limited. i would like to ask commissioner barrot what he thinks of this point, because it is going to create real practical problems. finally, there is certainty about the date of introduction of the digital tachograph, and i welcome the very active attitude that commissioner barrot is again exhibiting here. no pardon after may 2006, as you said. commissioner, we will stick to that, because we cannot allow those who have successfully introduced the new digital tachograph to be subjected to more stringent controls than truck combinations which do not have the digital tachograph. so i do hope you're actually going to perform.

in writing. there are serious shortcomings in cuba with regard to fundamental freedoms. the oral question and the resolution, which are both adequate, clearly show that. there is one matter i would like to raise prominently: the position of the house churches. new legislation, contained in directive 43 and resolution 46, requires all operating home communities to register with the authorities. registration applications often lead to extremely complicated negotiations with the public authorities. for example, detailed information about the parishioners and the pastors should be passed on. this new legislation has already led to the closure of several house churches. in 1992, the cuban government amended the constitution. the country's self-characterization changed from an atheist to a secular state. that was a first step in the right direction. the new legislation seems to reflect a trend towards new restrictions. while the cuban constitution recognizes the right of citizens to freedom of religion, de facto restrictions are increasingly in force. why are christian churches, including those that are registered, so closely watched, controlled and even infiltrated? so there is no real freedom of religion in cuba. i would also ask the council and the commission to address this issue in their discussions with the cuban authorities.
i hope that this week the european parliament will take a major new step towards a sustainable european agriculture. the european union will have to respect the five freedoms of the animal and enshrine them in laws. they are freedom from thirst, hunger and malnutrition, freedom from lack of adequate comfort, freedom from pain, injury and disease, freedom to display normal behavior and freedom from fear and stress. this directive meets those freedoms and it is high time. first of all, it is the european consumer and citizen who demands respect for animals and who is also willing to pay a reasonable price for it in an increasing number of countries. it is a good thing that the commission launched the animal welfare action plan three weeks ago. this type of agreement and rules must be european, otherwise member states will compete with each other at the expense of animal welfare. the animal welfare concerns of european consumers are currently focused on the welfare of broilers. this was due to reports of overcrowding in the stables and breeding methods that were aimed at increasing growth rates but not yet at strong bone structure and strengthening of the heart and lungs. too many animals suffer from injuries to legs and chest from poor litter. the committee on agriculture has sent a clear signal to the commission and the council with my report and the amendments to it, in favour of animal welfare, quality requirements, but also a certain flexibility towards the poultry farmer. i also wanted to set out in my report two principles which were not yet included in the directive. firstly, genetic selection, the breeding of animals for certain characteristics, must never result in a reduction or a threat to the potential welfare of an animal species. this principle should be a cornerstone of any animal welfare directive. secondly, socioeconomic considerations must not take precedence over animal welfare. it is logical to make demands on the quality of the housing on the basis of this principle. these are requirements which the good poultry farmers have long met, because they reduce mortality rates and therefore increase yields. animal welfare is in the economic interest of the poultry farmer. mr kyprianou has already mentioned this. the directive also provides for professional and daily checks on health and welfare, which is an important form of prevention of animal diseases, especially today. nevertheless, poultry need more space than is often given. the maximum quantity of poultry per square metre in the few days just before departure for the slaughterhouse should be reduced to 38 kilograms per square metre and to 34 kilograms in 2013, in accordance with the amendment which i tabled with my colleague elizabeth jeggle. many eu member states are already below or around that threshold. amendments which seek to impose an even lower density are not supported. while experts say that animal welfare risks are greater above a density of 30 kilograms per square meter, that is by no means an absolute limit beyond which disasters would occur. above 30 kilos per square foot, it all depends on good management, and that's exactly what this house can count on. that is why the rules must also be flexible with regard to farmers. quality requirements include penalties, but these must be proportionate and encourage better management. there is no point in punishing harder or longer than is strictly necessary. opponents of this directive say that global competition makes this kind of animal welfare standards impossible, because it would impose stricter requirements within europe than outside. that's actually incorrect. the biggest poultry rival, brazil, already complies with this directive. even if europe were to stop poor animal welfare at the border, brazilian meat can still enter without any effort. the european sector will therefore have to focus either on cost savings and thus scale up, or on producing high quality fresh meat close to the european consumer. this is already happening and is being encouraged by this directive. that does not mean that the commission must raise animal welfare issues within the world trade organisation and more vigorously than it does now. i have asked the commission about this. what possibilities does the gatt convention offer for animal welfare? article 20 of the directive states that restrictions on imports are permissible on grounds of public health, animal and plant health and moral grounds. we must continue along this path. europe needs concrete results to restore the confidence of its citizens in the eu. sustainable development is one of the areas where the eu can deliver, animal welfare is part of that and that is why this directive needs to be put in place quickly. (applause from the left)
i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr berman, for his report. it is his first report and, with apologies to the interpreters, i do not think he has got away with it with a jantje van leiden. i have to say that i do not know anyone in this house who is against animal welfare, everyone is for it. i have my doubts about this proposal, because i do not think it is a perfect proposal yet. firstly, it sets the same standards throughout the european union and i would imagine that in a cold climate like finland or in a very different climate like italy very different standards would have to apply as regards the number of animals in a given area. the practice could be that it's different now. the commission does not take this into account in any way and, as the previous speaker has said, we have not yet seen an economic analysis. it is very easy to say that socioeconomic priorities should never take precedence over animal welfare. okay, but there are always people who have to live off this, and what exactly are the consequences? does the commission have any idea of that? the most important point is, of course, the following. it has been mentioned by many others, including the commissioner himself, but i am not entirely convinced by his answer. it is not very useful, if we want to emphasize the so-called non-trade aspects, to draw up a new proposal in the middle of discussions in the world trade organization without being sure that the importers are also complying with it. why does the commission not start talking to the supermarkets and other major importers about a code of conduct as soon as possible? where is the logic in telling european producers that they must meet such and such standards, while supermarkets are allowed to import all kinds of goods from the rest of the world without any control by the commission? we only check for salmonella, or whatever, but we never check for animal welfare. that is what the commission should be doing and that is completely lacking in this proposal.
i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. i would, however, like to advocate that the interests of the chickens should be given a little more prominence when it comes to the density of the workforce. i would therefore ask all members of the european parliament, when they go down the elevator tomorrow before the vote and stand stiffly and awkwardly pressed against their colleagues, to imagine that there are ten more people crammed in there and to think that they have this feeling for one minute, but that an industrial chicken has to endure this miserable life. i would ask you to take that picture with you when you press the button tomorrow. tomorrow we have the opportunity to take an important step towards a more animal-friendly europe. let us seize this opportunity, for the sake of animal welfare, but also for our own humanity.
i would also like to congratulate the rapporteur on the thoroughness with which he has carried out his work. let me be clear, the cda is in favour of animal welfare and considers this an important point. we also welcome the new action programme of our commissioner. this does not mean that, although it is clear that the european citizen does not have a high opinion of animal welfare in europe - you wonder how he comes to that opinion, since europe does have the strictest standards in the world - it is nevertheless clear that as a consumer he often behaves very differently. that's the problem. that means that we can introduce stricter standards in europe, but at the same time we can open the tap if we do nothing about imports. you can say that we do not make that mistake in the world trade organization and that europe is trying, but there must be more to it. we can make arrangements with regard to imports, the quantities imported. we can also make arrangements regarding labelling. we can also agree that in europe we will at least work for those who have special projects, such as the people behind the farm chicken in the netherlands and the "label rouge" in france. it should be possible to support them with additional incentives so that consumers also buy these products. that is better for the cda, because otherwise we would again have european legislation, which the consumer as a citizen might applaud, but at the moment when he is in the supermarket he shows a different buying behaviour. it is essential that what the citizen wants is also translated into consumer behaviour. that is why we are very critical of this report. we will vote for amendment no 42, which at least does something about imports, and we will also support everything in this report that relates to better identification by labelling of meat. but as long as there is no guarantee that we can impose the same requirements on imports, as long as we are thus putting the european farmer back at a disadvantage and making it more difficult for animal welfare in europe again, it will be very difficult for us to vote in favour of this report.

i am speaking on behalf of the ppe-de group. president, commissioner piebalgs, we know that heating and cooling account for almost half of the energy consumed in the european union, and yet nothing has been done to make energy use more efficient. this directive is the final piece of the european energy policy. the report by mrs rothe, which is an initiative of parliament, is therefore timely. the ppe-de group in the european parliament is calling for a doubling of the current share of renewable energy sources in energy consumption for heating and cooling by 2020. this energy can often be generated on site, and the techniques have been greatly improved. this applies to solar, wind, water, biomass and geothermal energy. europe is leading the way in technological knowledge, but we are lagging behind in implementation. europe must be a leader in product innovation and commercialisation. if this development in the sector continues, many jobs will also be created. take a german example: between 1998 and 2002, this has doubled employment in the energy sector to around 125 000 jobs. in order to implement this policy, member states should propose achievable national targets and ensure an energy mix within the limits of their own capabilities. the ppe group is against over-regulation, against binding targets at the moment. the commission's proposal is ambitious, yes, but above all realistic, and that is why we have chosen to formulate effective national objectives and achievable targets. finally, i would like to ask commissioner piebalgs to make better use of the possibilities for financial support from the structural funds in the period 2007-13. the guidelines provide for possibilities, but it is up to the member states to decide whether they use them. we must also ensure that this priority is given to this policy of incentives, with the existing resources of the union and the member states. i'd like to give you this heartfelt cry.

the president of the council. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your kind words. not only after following this debate, but also after the work of the last weeks and months, i am of your opinion and charlie mccreevy's opinion that we now have a better directive before us, which i hope will meet with a very large majority at the first reading. i am pleased to speak, by chance or thanks to the wisdom of the president, as a compatriot of my two predecessors, mr karas and mr swoboda, not only because they are compatriots, but because they have both been heavily involved in this compromise in recent weeks. i would like to thank both of them, after having already thanked mrs gebhardt, and of course malcolm harbour, on behalf of many others. i am sure that you, mr harbour, will be able to wear the presidential tie in the coming weeks and months, because this directive will continue to be a good directive and will pave the way for greater freedom to provide services. it is and has been one of the most controversial dossiers -
