which is unfortunate, but unfortunately the trend has been such - it is also a symbol of the question of whether this europe is able to take us forward towards more growth and employment. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware of the fact that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. the services directive is one of them. so it's an important symbol in many ways, and we're on the right track. in the last few months, i have had the impression that all stakeholders, including the social partners, and i refer to the secretary-general of the european trade union confederation, have said that this is a good compromise to follow. some also said that today was a demonstration for a better directive, that all the responsible stakeholders want this services directive to succeed, because we need it. if we want to give life to the lisbon strategy, if we want to send a signal to people that we are working for growth and jobs, then we need this directive on services. our presidency will continue to work hard in the weeks ahead. we will take parliament's views and position, we will discuss them at the level of the social partners on 9 march, at the competition council on 13 march and at the european council on 23 and 24 march. i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in the negotiations, but i am saying that it should be involved in the negotiations, and i am not saying that it should be involved in the negotiations. i promise you, and i also promise the european parliament, that we will take this position of the european parliament very seriously and we will be guided by it to a great extent. i am in favour of the limitation of the directive, as agreed by the two major political groups. this is in line with the environmental and labour standards in force in the member states. it is important that the directive contributes to reducing the administrative burden for service providers operating in other member states. it seems that the country of origin principle will disappear. i can agree with that. i would like to ask the council and the commission whether they can guarantee that the member states can subject services to medical ethical requirements. another problematic subject which should be left out of the directive is drinking water. the same applies to social care and services. the recipients of these services are often not in a position to choose a provider. this may compromise the care of people with disabilities. the directive does not deal with the working conditions of temporary agency workers, as they are regulated in the temporary agency directive. there is therefore no reason to exclude the temporary employment sector from the scope of the directive. the directive must be put into practice. the quality of enforcement will be decisive. mr president, the prosperity and well-being of the citizens of the european union are based on the well-known freedoms: the free movement of goods, persons, capital and services. the promotion of the free movement of services is also part of the lisbon process: it is necessary to achieve growth and jobs. the proposal we had on the table was a quick-fire one, which, while it may have a lethal effect, can also have positive effects. it seemed to be a positive development for the new member states, but i tell you that the social unrest it would create would be a blow to us and that the directive would not only lead to social unrest but also to the loss of jobs. mr president, there are still a number of problems. i call the detachment. what will the commission do in practice? we must prevent the detachment from taking place because of administrative obstacles, and i come from the border region, difficult or not at all. we must do everything possible, mr president, to ensure that the issue of temporary agency work is also dealt with smoothly in the council. if everything is going to work properly in the employment sector, i will be very much in favour of this sector also being covered by the services directive. but since that is still not the case, it is now necessary to withdraw this secondment directive quickly. mr president, everything is up or down with the audit that will be carried out: the secondary sector, the selfemployed and the unemployed. as far as the control of secondment is concerned, we are now looking only at social security and taxes, but we could also require that the minimum wage be filled in on the forms used. that, mr president, is the process of the services directive and i welcome the compromises that have been reached.
mr president, first of all, let me say this: it is not about east versus west, because i am also from the west and i am a big supporter of the services directive. let us not lose sight of the aim of the directive, which is to remove unnecessary obstacles to small and mediumsized enterprises offering their services in other countries. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the directive is implemented. the protection of valuable social assets, yes, of course, but not protectionism, nationalism and certainly not xenophobia. in the global economy, we must strengthen the european market, not fragment and weaken it. the service market offers many great opportunities. it is in the service sector that innovative and quality jobs can be created. there is a high degree of hypocrisy in the debate, because the old member states, the west, fear competition from the east, but they forget to mention that western companies have been doing business freely and with great profit in eastern europe for 15 years. the directive must be adopted with the widest possible scope, i.e. services of general interest or services of general economic interest, and we should perhaps also clarify what this means, which are already being offered on the market, should simply be covered, as well as health care, employment agencies and gambling. the principle of the country of origin, even if we call it by another name, must simply be preserved. i shall vote for this directive only if it leads to greater freedom of movement for services. if we are going to adopt a compromise which actually seals the markets even more, i will not be voting for it.
the compromise which is now before us, mr president, is in keeping with the best dutch tradition of linking open markets with a reasonable level of social protection and can therefore be fully supported by my delegation. our minister for economic affairs has recently expressed his concern about the number of exceptions in the directive. i can't share that concern. i would like to explain why i think some of these exceptions are very important. firstly, the exception for health care and other sectors where there are too many compelling reasons of public interest. these are sectors which i think could be much better regulated by sector, and in addition to this there is a need for a horizontal framework directive on services of general interest which will clearly define the possibilities for non-central authorities to impose regulations on such sectors for reasons of general interest. another sector i would like to talk about for a moment is that of temporary agency work. as you know, mr president, we also reached a wonderful compromise on this subject here in this house four years ago, which was widely supported in this parliament but which has been frozen in the council for some time. the directive we drew up at the time, which struck a balance between protection and opening up of markets, was also a much better basis for imposing more precise and careful european regulation on the sector in question, and i would therefore ask mr mccreevy to honour his commitment on those various points, health care, temporary agency work, and to draw up other regulations to that end rather than this directive.

in writing. the former commissioner bolkestein's commitment was that good laws and good collective agreements, as they exist in some eu member states, should be in permanent competition with the bad arrangements in other member states. existing differences are turned into a competitive factor, with the intention that the worst always wins. this extremely neoliberal measure would destroy all the gains the workers' movement has made in a century of struggle. the directive will not be adopted in this form, thanks to the massive action of the trade union movement and other organisations. instead, there is now an unclear compromise between the major groups in parliament. the fact that this parliament is not making clear choices will leave much to the interpretation of the court of justice. it may still choose the highly controversial principle of country of origin. we, the representatives of the socialist party in the netherlands, are not involved in this compromise. we reject it completely, but until the directive is completely repealed we support all the amendments which the trade union movement considers to be an improvement. in the meantime, the fight we are waging with the trade union movement against all attempts by employers to hire people at a lower wage continues.

in writing. in bosnia and herzegovina, people have experience with different types of government. the turkish era was in favour of the muslim population in the centre and north-west, who consider themselves the real bosniaks. the austrian era was in favor of the roman catholics in the southwest, who call themselves croats. the yugoslav era was in favour of the orthodox in the north and east, who see themselves as serbs. bosnia was erased as an administrative unit in the 1920s, and re-established after the second world war as a mixed area, a small yugoslavia in the centre of the federal state that should contribute to the fusion of the different peoples within yugoslavia. this is a complete failure. in the 1992-95 war, each of these groups wanted to unilaterally push through its own wishes. dayton was the miracle cure for peace and reconciliation. it is an inefficient and costly structure, but it is the result of the need to reconcile widely differing wishes. that is why we must not simply throw away the flawed dayton structure, but use it to build a federal structure in which all can live together in peace for a long time. the people must be able to make their own choices, rather than being forced by europe.

i am glad to be able to raise the following matter in the presence of commissioner frattini. the dutch minister for integration, mrs verdonk, recently announced that she would change the return policy for homosexuals and christian asylum seekers from iran. she says that homosexuality and christianity are not banned in iran, so that asylum seekers can just go back. that may be true in a strict sense, but in practice homosexuals and christians do have reason to fear for their safety. homosexual activity is punishable by death. my question to commissioner frattini is whether the commission wants to find out whether the dutch return policy is in line with the european minimum standards for asylum seekers under the directive which is to be implemented in october this year and whether it is in line with international agreements.

i am speaking on behalf of the alde group. mr president, i fully agree with what the previous speaker said and, by the way, good evening to commissioner frattini for the third time today. we do not need to give reasons here tonight why citizens from the new countries should have access to the labour market. on the contrary, it is the countries that continue to close their markets that must explain why they continue to deny full-fledged eu citizens their basic rights. but of course it is also sound economic policy to lift the restrictions. the european economy and the labour market need people. if we as the european union and as the single market are to compete with large markets outside europe, we need dynamic, young, well-educated people who are mobile. that is what we have always sought to achieve in the european economy. so keeping the labour markets closed is absolutely pointless. it is also an illusion that these restrictions are holding back workers from eastern europe. the fact is that these eastern european workers have been here for a long time, but they are being exploited by rogue employers and are living in degrading conditions in our countries. i find that a shame. it is therefore for reasons of civil rights, the economy and solidarity that the restrictions must be lifted. i am therefore glad that my country, the netherlands, is probably going to lift the restriction as well - a majority is now in favour of this - and i would like to call on all member states to do the same in the year of mobility.
the vvd euro group is in favour of the 'old' member states lifting restrictions on workers from the new member states. all european countries benefit economically from open borders. in the netherlands, for example, we are facing shortages in the metal, horticulture, agriculture and health sectors. in britain, ireland and sweden, open borders have proved successful. if we can get the front door locked, we have control over who we let in instead of trying to keep the illegal back doors closed. that is not possible, because every year we see that the seasonal work is mainly carried out by illegal workers.
i would like to congratulate mr cottigny on his report. in my country, the netherlands, restructuring and globalisation are important topics. opinions differ on how to deal with it. the social democrats, of whom i am one, believe that unnecessary money pumping from the netherlands to brussels and back is not the solution. that is why not everyone in my country is happy with the proposed european globalisation fund. i would like to express my support for this fund, however, adding that i think that as much as possible it should be linked to existing esf structures. why am i in favor? because citizens need support in coping with the negative effects of globalisation. if we cannot expect this support from the national authorities, as in the netherlands for certain regions, such as the north, we are happy to receive it from europe.
in writing. the christianunie-sgp parliamentarians were unable to agree with the opinion to the council on the instrument for rapid response and preparedness in the event of serious emergencies. the amendment of the legal basis, the extension of the instrument to finance preventive measures and the increase in the budget cannot be counted on our agreement. we particularly regret the change in the legal basis, all the more so since parliament has in previous, similar votes opted for article 308 as the only correct legal basis. the extension of the definition of article 175 of the treaty to include this instrument is not the right way forward and can only be interpreted as a majority position in parliament to extend its influence to matters which are not within its competence under the treaty. in addition, the financial consequences of the deployment of relief teams should not be regulated by a community instrument. this is a matter of solidarity between countries.
in writing. the vvd delegation thought it necessary to vote against the hutchinson report on relocation of firms in the context of regional development, because it expresses unambiguous support for the commission's proposal to set up a globalisation fund. the vvd is strongly opposed to this fund because this form of state intervention is counter to the internal market. social policy is a matter for the member states themselves. in addition, a european system already exists which provides for the possibility of retraining workers. the existing european structural funds provide the member states with the financial resources to (re) educate their populations. the hutchinson report also contains a number of unnecessary and intrusive bureaucratic provisions which impede freedom of establishment and thus undermine the dynamism of the internal market.
in writing. the christian-union-sgp parliamentarians can agree with the motion for a resolution on the mercury strategy, but would like to point out that exceptions to the ban on the use of mercury in measuring and control equipment should remain possible. one of these exceptions should be the production of conventional mercury barometers, as reflected in the adopted text of the resolution. the production of these products uses very little mercury, which also poses a relatively low environmental risk by being contained in glass. several small companies in the eu depend on its production for their income and would have to close down by an absolute ban due to lack of a sufficiently suitable alternative material. we believe that the production of this european heritage should be allowed to continue in a controlled environment.
mr president, less than a year after the problems in the textile sector, europe is on the verge of a new trade conflict with china. the rapid economic rise of asian countries has surprised europe time and again, and europe has been divided again. but the eu must not allow itself to be paralysed by the contradictions between northern and southern member states. once again the commission's policy is a weak compromise between free trade and protectionism. the commission must therefore give priority to the development of a sound and unambiguous trade policy with the asian region. the continuing increase in sectorial conflicts must be stopped. i understand the frustration of importers and of member states which do not have their own industry. on the other hand, i think it is very important to point out to china the rules of the wto. these words must sometimes be followed by action, commissioner. i therefore call on you to take account of the state intervention in the footwear sector in the negotiations on the granting of market economy status to the people's republic of china.
it is absolutely true that the european arrest warrant already has many advantages. it makes the extradition system more efficient, considerably faster and also less arbitrary, but that doesn't take away from the fact that the arrest warrant is still flawed. my group was opposed to the introduction of the european arrest warrant at the time because it did not involve agreements on minimum standards in criminal proceedings. the only thing that has been done is to investigate, without taking the necessary guarantees for the rights of suspects. that seems very proactive and tough on crime, but that's also what's been an obstacle to cooperation in that rendition. three years after the arrest warrant was issued, the situation has not changed. the framework decision on the procedural rights of suspects has not yet been adopted by the council. i agree with mr watson's call for the council to clarify which member states are opposed to improving the rights of suspects, and for what reasons. it is true that mr demetriou has said that all member states must respect the rights of the accused and the convention on human rights, but there are many cases in strasbourg. and there are also many differences between member states, particularly as regards procedures. if we were all in agreement on the rights of the accused, we could of course sign the agreement on procedural rights in the middle of the afternoon, but we cannot do that because there are very big differences. and the result of these great differences is that national courts are still trying to decide when you can hand over someone and when you can't? this has led to a number of court cases, which have prevented people from being transferred to another eu member state. an example of this was a court case in bolzano; the court there refused to extradite an italian to austria. once we have joint agreements on the rights of suspects, the transfer of suspects will also be much smoother. i would therefore also ask the council to see this not as a gift for the rights of the accused, but as a necessary step forward, which is to do both with repression and with investigation, but also to guarantee the rule of law, also at european level; if there is no trust between member states, then in the end it will not do any good to that investigation either.
i am therefore in favour of this measure to combat terrorism. in the 2002 debate i suggested that only offences for which a european or international definition existed should be included in the list. the current list of offences is too broad, creating a risk of legal uncertainty and discrimination. member states therefore take every opportunity to maintain the double criminality criterion. mr hazan rightly calls on the council to ensure that this double criminality is abolished. initially, she even asked for an extension of the list of offences; the commission has rejected this proposal. in view of the current signals, this was also totally untimely. it is better to follow developments closely over the next two years. i think that the assessment will have to lead to a reduction in the current list. the only way to ensure legal certainty in extending the list is to define all the offences in question in a european way. however, it is undesirable that this decision should be used to achieve a gradual harmonisation of criminal law.
i would like to thank mrs gräßle for her work, for even sacrificing her christmas holiday, as i understand it, and for the many fruitful discussions we have had together. the revision of the financial regulation is really very much needed. the santer commission's resignation led to stricter rules being introduced, the control mechanisms being revised and my group has always been in favour of this. we have also had to conclude, however, that this process of cleanup has gone a little too far. it has led to excessive bureaucracy in the application of grants, particularly affecting ngos, non-governmental organisations and small and mediumsized enterprises. the fear of misuse of funds is now so great that european officials shift the risk, where possible, to the applicant for a grant. we are therefore still not getting what we wanted, namely a european commission in which officials are really responsible and can take responsibility. i am therefore very much in favour of the amendments which are before us. most of the amendments tabled are about simplifying procedures. the principle of proportionality is introduced and i also welcome amendments nos 110 and 101, which deal with the percentage rule. at the moment, when beneficiaries acquire their own resources, they are likely to have to pay back a lot of money immediately, and the result is that you actually discourage people from taking initiatives to acquire their own resources. the european commission has said that it will adopt some of the main points of those amendments, but of course the devil is often in the details. you have seen in the proposals before you that the aim of the european parliament is to simplify procedures. this is not just a matter of the financial regulation; the commission will also be able to make a number of recommendations on the basis of the information it has received from the member states. the european commission will also be preparing a report on this subject. so i hope you will adopt the principle that simplification must take place, which will lead to real relief. at the moment, there is much talk of reducing the regulatory pressure from europe, and the big companies are immediately brought to mind, whose competitiveness would otherwise be hampered. but it is also very important for small businesses and non-profit organizations, if they can now benefit from a reduction in administrative burdens. this is important for the european commission in drawing up the implementing rules, but i hope that the council will also take this into account, that it is not just the big companies that are concerned, but that the smaller companies and the ngos should also benefit from the burden-relieving measures from europe.
i would like to thank mr o'neachtain for his excellent report. our group welcomes the report and we also support the two amendments which have been tabled. the report comes at a crucial time. last month it was announced that a number of deep-sea fisheries are threatened with depletion and even extinction. we cannot afford to lose sight of this, fish stocks are in a bad way, and a change of course towards sustainable and environmentally friendly fishing is urgently needed, and this change affects us all. let me start with the fishermen, they will have to adapt to the fact that fish stocks are being depleted. technical measures are needed, such as acoustic deterrents, electrical impulses to avoid drilling, adequate nets and hooks to minimise by-catch, but the non-technical measures are at least as important. i am thinking in particular of the prevention of illegal fishing and the establishment of fisheries reserves. fish populations will have a chance to grow again. it sounds simple, but unfortunately it's not. our group stresses that fishermen must be able to use sustainable methods. the fishing industry is not doing well, not least because of the rising oil prices. this requires an active and committed public sector to promote sustainable fisheries. the authorities must also guarantee control and enforcement. illegal fishing makes the situation impossible for all parties. without effective control, this inevitably leads to a tragedy of the commons. everybody loses, and there's not gonna be any fish left. thirdly, consumers must be involved in sustainable fishing. many consumers want to buy well-caught fish, but they don't know which fish is sustainable. certification and quality marks are therefore important. sustainable fish is first of all fish that is not overfished without unnecessary by-catch and discarding, and this requires good information to consumers. only then will consumers be able to make informed choices about sustainably harvested fish. finally, to make a change of course possible, we must also take responsibility as politicians. we must constantly call for sustainable fisheries and we must act; we must respond to those fishermen who allow the creation of fisheries reserves temporarily, so that they do not temporarily lose their income. the report before us is a series of good steps in that direction and these should take shape in the near future. the fisheries sector must of course be involved in the measures. only with responsible politicians and active public authorities, innovative fishermen and concerned consumers can both the fisheries sector and fish have a sustainable future.

support the additional attention to the criterion of absorption capacity. the impasse over the ratification of the constitutional treaty plays an important role in this. without internal reforms, it will be difficult to steer the accession of new member states. as stated in previous resolutions, we believe that the treaty of nice is not a basis for new accession decisions. but there is also a greater need for greater clarity around the concept of absorption capacity, as laid down in the copenhagen criteria, and this too is rightly addressed in the report. i should like to take this opportunity to outline briefly the position of the social democrats on the possible accession of various countries. as regards our eastern neighbours, ukraine and moldova, we believe that it is not appropriate to have an accession debate at this stage. the best we can do is to invest in practical cooperation. we have agreed action plans with these countries and let us try to make them a success. we support the council's position on the balkans. the western balkan countries have a general prospect of membership, but this will be completed at different rates for different countries. we are continuing with turkey on the path we have taken, we are at the beginning of a process which will last for many years, and it is extremely important that we stick to the commitment, but also to the conditions we have set. i would add, speaking of the balkans, that the copenhagen criteria are of course still of vital importance to us, but full cooperation with the yugoslav tribunal is also an important demand of the pse group. i also believe - and this has been the subject of much discussion recently - that regional cooperation in that region can make an important contribution to bringing those countries closer to the european union. finally - and this is probably the most important point of the whole debate - the report speaks of the possibility of creating a new multilateral structure for european countries which are not yet members of the european union. for some countries this could be an alternative to membership, for others an intermediate step on the way to membership. for ukraine and moldova, for example, such a structure could be a good next step, but for those countries which already have a recognised prospect of membership, it is an option, emphatically, and not an obligation. that is what mr brok's report says very clearly. it is an option which these countries can choose for themselves if they consider it useful. it is not an alternative to the prospect of membership, which is for turkey and also for the balkan countries. i would therefore like to emphasize that this is our interpretation of paragraph 10 of the motion for a resolution. this is the way in which we in the committee on foreign affairs and security agreed with the rapporteur.
i am very pleased that the commission has taken the initiative to put forward a proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos. we believe that there is a need for greater clarity on the concept of absorption capacity, a fashionable concept of containers where everyone can put in whatever he wants, and that means that the question of geographical boundaries will eventually have to be answered. that question, ladies and gentlemen, can no longer be avoided. and yes, we also think that the eu needs to start thinking about some sort of intermediate form between full membership and neighbourhood, for those countries that do not yet have a prospect of membership. so it is not about turkey, it is not about the western balkans, but it is about ukraine, moldova or belarus. i must therefore say that my group and i were very disappointed, and even more so extremely annoyed, to see that in the run-up to this debate, in the media, the rapporteur has given his report a twist which goes against a number of key points. if one reads the media in preparation for this debate, the final conclusion for turkey, and for the western balkans, is that there must be some form of intervention rather than membership. it is not a coincidence, ladies and gentlemen, let us be frank about this, it is the rapporteur's opinion, he has never put it under chairs or benches; the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environment from harmful substances. that is the rapporteur's right, but it is not the view of the majority of the committee on foreign affairs; it is not, and that is much more important, what is in his own report. it would therefore be a great honour for the rapporteur to say clearly and clearly what is in his report outside this chamber and not to confuse it with the fantasy in his head. what parliament says in this report is that we do not want to undermine the membership prospects of turkey, of the western balkans, and that interventions for those countries are only possible if those countries themselves choose to do so. everyone knows as well as i do that all those countries of the western balkans and turkey do not want to go in that direction, they want full membership. let us stop creating confusion in this house, but especially outside it.
president. - until recently, a rapid and substantial enlargement of the european union was presented as a great step forward, deserving of everyone's support. it was seen as a reunification of europe and as the victory of the west in the cold war. after the major enlargement of 2004, this climate has changed considerably. public opinion in the old member states does not see this enlargement as a success, especially because of the increasing abuse of the gap between high and low-wage countries. the politicians are also becoming more reserved. the report on the enlargement strategy which we are discussing today makes this change very visible. attention is drawn to the european union's absorption capacity, its external borders, the costs of enlargement and administrative problems attributed to the lack of a european constitution. romania and bulgaria therefore appear to be the last countries to be allowed to join in the foreseeable future. other european states are referred to the neighbourhood policy. even the three countries already declared candidates for membership are not given a date for accession. all over the western balkans, in recognized states and in states or protectorates seeking independence, where groups of people with different languages and religions were sharply pitted against each other in the 1990s, public opinion now expects miracles from rapid accession to the european union. the union uses these expectations to demand reforms and thus to intervene deeply in the administrative choices made there. the union does not want enlargement for the time being, but it does want influence beyond its own borders. bosnia-herzegovina, for example, now has a tax system that nobody wanted there, and the regional autonomy guaranteed by the dayton agreement is being reduced. the propaganda posters say that thanks to the presence of a european military power, this country is on its way to the european union. public opinion in montenegro and kosovo, which adopted the euro as legal tender four years ago along with 12 other member states, already expects to be admitted to the union as independent states soon. in vojvodina, the hungarian-speaking population expects protection from slavic domination. in all these cases, the union's action so far has been disappointing. the european union has a duty to help the countries of the western balkans to achieve their goals. my group is not very enthusiastic about this proposal. on the other hand, we recognize that this text provides an opportunity to stress that the forthcoming referendum in montenegro must be taken seriously and that the conflict over the use of the name macedonia must be resolved quickly in good consultation between greece and its northern neighbour. it is also positive that a solution must be found quickly in kosovo that meets the needs of both the large albanian majority and the serb and roma minorities.

on behalf of the pse group. – mr president, imagine that next weekend president barroso called a press conference to announce the merger of euronet, deutsche börse and the london stock exchange. a dream scenario or a nightmare? i tend to say neither, but simply note that it is rather improbable that would happen. the message of my group, nevertheless, is that president barroso, the commissioner and their colleagues should not be caught napping in the situation we now find ourselves in, with the mergers, acquisitions and developments we see in the financial markets, energy and other sectors – sectors that are all very important for the european economy. we social democrats still believe that the state has a role to play in the economy as a facilitator, as a regulator and as a defender of the public interest. i do not say that we politicians should take the leading role and be the stars at press conferences, but we should play a role in silent diplomacy and in creating the conditions in a dialogue with companies; we should not only commence with what has happened but also anticipate what will happen. that is why i do not like your approach, commissioner, which is only negative and seeks to prevent authorities from interfering in what is happening with companies. i think there should be a proactive approach and i shall mention three questions on which we should have a thorough debate in europe. the first is industrial policy. i was very happy with some of commissioner verheugen’s remarks this morning, which were made more from the perspective of industrial policy. the second – which was also mentioned by mr lehne – concerns what is happening in takeovers and the way we organise this. we need a debate there, not only on shareholder democracy, which the financial times and the economist are preaching, but on other aspects too. the third is the european approach versus the national approach.
i would like to thank mr graefe zu baringdorf for his usual enthusiasm in carrying out his task as rapporteur. i agree with his main conclusions. now that we have more and more liberalization of trade in agricultural products, it is necessary that we have more international recognition of geographical indications and all this kind of thing. so, totally right. what is also important to me is what exactly the definition of a geographical indication is in europe itself. mr graefe zu baringdorf has already pointed out that parma ham must come from that very region. i have always been surprised that the largest producer of edammer cheese is not the netherlands but germany. i think that should be changed as far as i am concerned. if we do this in the wto - and i am glad he agrees with me - and we ask others to recognise our products, then it seems to me that it is only natural that we should recognise the products of others. i do not entirely agree with mr graefe zu baringdorf when he says that there are currently only two quality categories of agricultural products in the european union, namely geographical indications and organic production. that may be the case at the moment, but we have to go much further. it is necessary to establish a european quality mark for agricultural products. if we ask our farmers to respect animal welfare standards, to respect environmental objectives, etc., then it is very unfair to say to those same farmers, you have to compete with the rest of the world, with farmers who do not have to adhere to the same standards. the customer must be able to recognise it in the shop and therefore we must also develop a quality label for non-geographical and organic products.

author. mr president, the various political forces in moldova are keen to join the european union. however, it is not a candidate country and this does not seem to be changing any time soon. today we voted on the enlargement strategy as proposed in the brok report. if we restrict entry to newcomers on that basis, moldova will only be able to enter via a bypass. that is the model of german reunification in 1990. this would mean that moldova would eventually have to give up its own independence to return to romania, from which it seceded in 1940. such a solution was also on the table immediately after the collapse of the soviet union, but romania did not appear attractive enough to implement it in the short term. this may change if romania's membership of the european union becomes a great success and moldova comes to the conclusion that it is in a much worse situation as a neighbouring country. but such a reunification has never had the support of the slavic-speaking minority. this minority is concentrated mainly in the east and is much more oriented towards the old ties with ukraine and russia. the de facto separation of the eastern shore, the long stretch of transnistria bordering ukraine, may not be resolved until ukraine also joins the european union. in the meantime - and this may take a long time - efforts must be made to find peaceful solutions, to cooperate and to bring about democratic changes in transnistria, which is still ruled by authoritarian rule. my group has emphasized this in its own motion for a resolution. we regret that the joint resolution speaks of weakening the administration of transnistria rather than reforming it, but we can agree with the compromise text. on the one hand, because it does not call for violence against transnistria, and on the other hand, because it does not seek to isolate moldova as a punishment for the strong position that the communist party in that country has won from the voters.

the collapse of the soviet union has not proved to be a guarantee of democracy. - no, it's the opposite. some politicians who emerged from the communist period have lost their ideology, but this is precisely what is now hindering them even less than before in their techniques of remaining in power themselves or of transferring state power to their children. one of these techniques is to extend the term of office of incumbent presidents by ten years or even for life by means of a referendum without the possibility of putting one or more candidates against them. another means is to eliminate serious opposers by having them imprisoned on false charges, by having them crash or by having them simply disappear. in ukraine, georgia and kyrgyzstan, a broad popular uprising against such a regime has succeeded, but the question remains whether things will continue to improve there in the long term. in belarus, uzbekistan, turkmenistan and the great country of kazakhstan, the powers that be are still succeeding in breaking down any resistance. some have powerful foreign friends, for example, because of their role in energy supply. kazakhstan was for a long time a dry and sparsely populated area, with a small turkish-speaking population. russian colonization took place in places where industry or mining was possible or an experimental rocket base could be established. meanwhile, a new capital has been founded, far from the big city of almaty, and the influence of the russian population is being greatly reduced. the future of the large, sparsely populated kazakhstan, with its two major populations and the remnants of minorities who were once exiled from the russian empire, is extremely uncertain. the resolution rightly urges that relations with kazakhstan should not be limited to economic relations but should focus on political prisoners, the right to freedom of opposition, democratic decision-making and human rights.
author. president, african states are not the product of africans themselves, but of european colonization. their borders are drawn by foreigners. these borders cut across peoples who had wanted to stay together and they combine groups of people who have very little in common in history, culture, language and religion. these people can't see the government as their own. in practice, this is a serious obstacle to democracy. in such situations, persons who use violent means to favor one group of the population and oppress another have many opportunities. they can only hold their unstable states together by using atrocious means. under such conditions, only violent profiteers can hold state power in their hands for long. this is the situation in all parts of africa, but particularly where arab-muslim and non-muslim black populations are united in a state. the tragedy of sudan, with its continuing civil war and refugee flows, is now known to all. we have already adopted a resolution on the neighbouring country of chad yesterday. in that desert country, hissène habré was once the leader taken for granted by the outside world and he subsequently retained power in part of his country until he was forced to flee to senegal in 1990. even after his departure, there is no room for political opposition, armed gangs are terrorizing the starving population and trying to take over part of the territory from neighbouring countries. it is not just charles taylor, who emigrated from liberia to nigeria, but also mengistu haile mariam from ethiopia, who now lives in zimbabwe, who would be entitled to a judicial review. this may prevent african politicians from becoming violent dictators in the future. the situation in rwanda is not exactly comparable. some view the present dominance of the tutsi minority as just punishment for the hutu majority who have tried to oust and murder their centuries-long rulers. the long-term persistence of the present situation - and we must take account of the continuation of that situation - continues to feed the centuries-old mutual hatred. that is why we should not be complaining about the situation, but we should be paying attention to the horrors that have taken place there.

the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the energy market is not affected by the crisis. energy is essential for every country. what can we learn about security of supply? the interlinking of energy policy with foreign policy is now a fact. the european union must beware of pressure from energy-producing countries. the commission has also proposed that the member states should be able to apply the principle of subsidiarity to all energy suppliers. in addition, the russian-ukrainian crisis has brought the option of importing lng from other regions back into the spotlight. the failure of the internal market also requires our attention. the recent debate on the creation of national champions should not obscure this. however, the member states themselves have strong resources in terms of security of supply. in addition to a more diverse energy mix, they can seek to increase their emergency reserves. these measures, together with the intensification of cooperation between the member states, are more important than a comprehensive european energy policy. the energy agreement with russia also calls for caution. critical distance is preferable to further integration in the energy field with countries with which we have an unstable relationship.

the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i happened to look into that case at the time and i think it was typically a case of lack of supervision in the united kingdom and lack of regulation in the united kingdom and that it is therefore wrong to blame europe for that now. and that was in the thatcher period, just to be clear. i shall confine my remarks to a number of issues which are also dealt with in the report and which have been raised recently, particularly in the debate on how national interests can be brought back into competition policy. in this respect, there are at least two issues which are also relevant to the rules of play of european competition policy. one of them is the rule of two thirds. it is good that further steps are taken in this area, because they do not take into account the differences in the size of the member states and the impact of certain measures, including for neighbouring countries and for regional policy. that is a very important point which we have raised again. the other point has already been raised by mr berès, using the example of the stock exchanges. it is clear that even if the turnover is below the threshold of european involvement, the effect of such a concentration can be european, and that is a reason - as you have already indicated, commissioner - to consider whether we cannot broaden the criteria for european competence in this context. i would like to raise these points again and i would also like to ask for support for our amendment no 9.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) amending regulation (eec) no 1768/92 on the common organization of the market in bananas (com (97) 0406 - c4-0179/97). the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the proposed constitution, which was rejected by voters in france and the netherlands last year, would not have altered the council's powerful role. the council is both the government and the senate. the council meeting behind closed doors is one of the union's greatest democratic shortcomings. in practice, this makes it impossible for meps and national parliamentarians to be sure that their member states' ministers are voting as they have promised. a good practical example is the vote on software patents a year ago, where either the dutch or the danish minister lied to his own parliament about his own voting behaviour. democratic control of decision-making becomes impossible. the publicity of council meetings, not just legislative meetings but all meetings, does not have to wait for a constitution but can be made now. further delay means a deliberate undermining of parliamentary democracy.
the austrian presidency should make it a priority to make the meetings of the council of ministers public. the european parliament meets and votes as a co-legislator in public and all the meeting documents are on the internet so that all people, organisations, media and national politicians who wish to follow us can do so. i see no reason why the council should not do so. the council conclusions of 21 december on this subject show too little courage. it is particularly important that both the vote and the debate be held in public on matters on which the council decides by unanimity. it's the only way to get rid of backroom deals. this is not difficult, since the opening up of council meetings does not require an amendment to the treaty, but only an amendment to the council's rules of procedure. it is not the time for the council to take decisions, for the most part behind closed doors. access to documents of the institutions must also be improved. the european citizens who rejected the constitutional treaty in the summer of 2005 demonstrated the need for a more transparent and democratic union.

firstly, by means of an actual impact assessment. and that's what's missing at the moment. we have seen many examples of impact assessments. they all differ in quality. some are good, some are not. if we as a parliament want to benefit from an impact assessment, it must be an impact assessment that provides neutral information, that provides good information and that is clear. that's what's missing. we have had some experience in parliament of impact assessments of amendments. the quality of the food varies. that is why i am proposing that there should be an independent review of these impact assessments. it does not have to be done by a heavy authority. that could be a panel of four experts in the field of impact assessment, who simply look at the impact assessments that the commission is making and the impact assessments that we are making here in parliament, and then give advice on them. so no agency, no heavy authority, but just an independent quality audit, an effective external quality audit. mr president, i now come to my next point, the comitology. that article i was just talking about was specifically about comitology. there was another passage i don't want you to remember. it's about the committees. rapporteur for the opinion of the economic and monetary committee. i fully agree with these final words of mr barroso. i have drawn up an opinion on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs on mr doorn's report. i would like to congratulate him on this report and also thank him for including almost in full the various paragraphs which we had included in our opinion, based on our experience in the committee on economic affairs. i would also like to echo the words of mr winkler, and say, in a slightly different way: better regulation is not always less regulation or deregulation, but more effective regulation that is focused on the outcome and on the bottom line. this cycle of preparation, consultation, rule-making, impact assessment and then implementation and enforcement has been mentioned by several people. in our opinion i have advocated that we should start from the back and look at the process from there. how to make the process effective and from there, what rules to draw up. a bad example of how this has not been done, how this has not been thought about and how the field has not been involved in the preparation was the services directive which we have just discussed in its original form. fortunately, parliament has now changed that. i would also like to mention some good examples from the same directorate-general market. in particular, in the area of financial services, a practice has been developed and proposed by the lamfalussy committee of experts - which we call the lamfalussy procedure. in this context, those who are actually involved in the regulation process, namely the regulators, the market participants, the consumers and users involved in the process, are much more influential in determining what should be included in the rules. we have used that example to present to others and we would like to emphasize it again in this debate. we have problems with the call back and the responsibility of parliament as co-legislator to be able to monitor what comes out in the end, but the process as such is something we very much support.
it is extremely regrettable that in the debate on subsidiarity and better regulation there seems to be only one main motive, namely greater economic growth and more competition through less regulation. this creates the illusion that it is precisely in the field of social protection, the environment and consumer protection that more attention should be paid to subsidiarity. all of this fits into the neoliberal mindset that less regulation is by definition a good thing. the reality is that it is the european rules on the sacred internal market that are in the way of european citizens. for example, they enable a small municipality in the north of the netherlands to be told by the european commission that it must first ask for permission before it can build a fibre-optic cable network for its own citizens. or that the public support from the city of amsterdam to the local zoo is seen as a distortion of competition from brussels. it is no wonder that the dutch citizens no longer see any point in further extending the powers of brussels and in the proposed constitution which approved and supported that process. they have, by a large majority, thrown that text into the trash. the reality is that subsidiarity has long since become a meaningless concept. the european institutions, this parliament in particular, rarely or never ask themselves whether european involvement in a particular subject is actually contributing to a better well-being of people and the environment. on the contrary, a flood of european regulations continues to undermine the control of national and regional authorities. i refer here, for example, to the european services directive, which, even in modified form, will seriously undermine the autonomy of municipal authorities in the field of licensing or local services. in short: it is good that there is a debate on the quality and subsidiarity of european regulation. it is, however, utterly naive to assume that the fundamental problems of brussels' interference can be solved by scrapping a handful of regulations or by carrying out impact assessments. the first step will be to make fundamental changes to the rules of the internal market.

on behalf of the pse group. mr president, commissioner, on this world health day i would like to reflect with you on the european contribution to the achievement of the millennium development goals. three of the eight goals are related to health care, the fight against diseases such as aids and malaria, and the reduction of infant and maternal mortality during pregnancy and childbirth. the world, and africa in particular, is still far behind in all these objectives. each year, worldwide, more than 1 million children die of malaria, 6 million children under five die of poor or no nutrition, and 2 to 3 million children die of diseases that could have been prevented by vaccination. in 2005, one woman died every minute from complications during pregnancy and childbirth. the european commission and the member states have repeatedly promised to give high priority to health care in developing countries. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the eu's development cooperation budget is spent on primary health care at less than 5% a year, far short of parliament's commitment and desire to spend 20% of the eu's aid budget on primary education and primary health care. put your money where your mouth is. we must also be much more strict in our criteria for spending when we give budgetary aid. no budget support to countries that do not qualify for it because of bad governance. and where budget support is given, it must be sectoral, i.e. explicitly allocated to health or education. it must also be very clear that the money has actually been spent on health care and what the purposes and results of that are, with a specific percentage allocated to the control of spending by a civil society watchdog. we owe that to the european taxpayer and to the people and parliaments of the host country. moreover, spending does not have to be done through european programmes alone, but can often be done more efficiently through cooperation with organisations such as the world health organisation, unaids, unfpa and through global initiatives such as the global fund. president. - mr president, europe must guarantee the financing of sexual and reproductive health projects which are underfunded because of the us president's policy of blocking funds for these projects. meanwhile, the united states is spending $12 billion on a ridiculous campaign promoting abstinence and fidelity to one's spouse. the united states congress today determined that this campaign is confusing and complicating existing campaigns. many women and girls have died as a result of this scandalous policy. as europe, we cannot and must not allow that to happen. i hope that colleagues from other parties in this parliament will also take the side of millions of women and girls, not bush. in addition to increasing spending, it is also a structural integrated approach to health care in developing countries. access to basic services such as health care is essential for the poorest, but lack of knowledge and infrastructure at the grassroots level often makes access insufficient. we must therefore invest in logistics and social infrastructure, in knowledge transfer and training of local staff, and also in basic education, teaching basic knowledge of hygiene, safe drinking water and healthy food. only if there is a basic structure can poverty-related diseases and other unnecessary causes of death be truly eliminated structurally. the resolution rightly also mentions the shortage of health workers, which is often caused by brain drain, highly qualified doctors and nurses going to work elsewhere in the world. we must prevent this brain drain by actively concluding agreements between sectors and countries, by encouraging circular migration, which involves returning to another country after a short period of time, by training and transfer of knowledge and by creating better working conditions. finally, in many countries, a large part of the money spent on health care goes to the poorest of the poor because of corruption and mismanagement. there are studies that show that in a country like chad, only 1% of the public money spent on health care gets to its destination. i come now to my own report on corruption and development cooperation which will be discussed in plenary tomorrow. good governance and the fight against corruption are essential to achieving the millennium development goals. this is something that both donor and recipient countries will have to work hard on in the years ahead. i cannot stress enough in this house the importance of the millennium development goals, particularly in the areas of health and education. the doubling of the budget allocated to this objective is now absolutely necessary. in doing so, you are doubling the chances of the world's poorest people to develop and escape from poverty. all of them healthy. make history of the poor!
i would like to join the words of the chairman of our committee on agriculture, mr joseph daul, and congratulate the commissioner on having come up so quickly with an action plan and also with unorthodox proposals to tackle the problem that is occurring in poultry farming at the moment. it has clearly understood that europe must do everything it can to cope with this crisis. the spread of avian influenza by migratory birds and the threat of the virus to humans demonstrate that society has a vested interest in stopping the disease and preventing the poultry sector from collapsing. it is really beyond words that, despite the threat to public health and the uncertain spread via wild birds, vaccination against avian influenza to protect humans and animals is still a taboo. the oft-heard argument that vaccination leads to sales problems has almost become a self-fulfilling prophecy. for as long as europe, as a prosperous market community, hides behind this argument, the global vaccination itself will continue to be treated as a contagious disease. then the proposal itself. the adoption of market measures and their financing have always been the exclusive prerogative of the european commission. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. the commission is now proposing to move to a 50% co-financing rate. i have to say, also on behalf of my party, the cda, that in this case, given its limited financial resources, the commission has indeed perhaps chosen the best way of working with parliament will support that amendment.
i would also like to congratulate the commissioner on her tenacity. she has been listening all evening to a persistent misunderstanding among colleagues in the european parliament. the european union, ladies and gentlemen, is not an insurance company. where entrepreneurs take risks, entrepreneurs must and can take insurance. when france was a small and medium-sized enterprise, there was first a soft loan with a low interest rate from the french government and then the cattle farmer himself paid for the damage he had suffered by means of a self-built emergency fund. that's the way it is. the european union cannot be held responsible for all the damage suffered by a sector, in any industry. in the absence of a good insurance system in the poultry farm, you will have to take measures. the european union cannot help it. but what measures? not the strengthening of export subsidies. this is a scandal. the problem of the third world is not acceptable, it disrupts the world market and goes against the negotiations and agreements within the wto. what can be done is to encourage member states to grant this type of soft loan to poultry farmers in the event of a crisis. then the measure proposed by the commission. okay, i can temporarily support it, but i think we should first insist on a good insurance system for the poultry farmers themselves. then entrepreneurs take on their own responsibility, which they can be really proud of.

- i'm the reporter. mr president, commissioner, i am pleased that we are having a serious debate in this parliament today on a subject which has not always been so easily discussed in a discourse on development cooperation. europe is the world's largest donor in terms of quantity, but recently there has been a growing global awareness that the effectiveness of aid is at least as important as the quantity. the 2005 paris declaration on aid effectiveness is an important benchmark for action in this area and the european commission has recently followed up this declaration with a package of measures on aid effectiveness, which we will soon be discussing in the committee on development. my own-initiative report, on which we are voting today, also makes a number of serious recommendations to make aid more effective. good governance free of corruption is an important condition for aid effectiveness. it not only prevents aid money from hanging on the wire. corruption is also a barrier to development and disproportionately burdens the poorest sections of the population. access to essential services, such as basic education and health care, is made more difficult and sometimes even impeded by corruption. corruption is a huge problem all over the world. the world bank estimates that over $1 trillion, or $1,000 billion, is paid in bribes each year. in africa, the poorest continent in the world, an estimated $148 billion, or about 25 percent of africa's gdp, is lost. moreover, corruption is present in all layers of society and involves almost all social actors: the european union is a global player in the field of education and training. good governance and aid effectiveness are the joint responsibility of the donor community and the developing countries. it takes two to tango. that is why my proposals are also about what we can do as the european union, the commission and the member states, preferably in cooperation with the entire international community. my main proposal is to draw up a blacklist of corrupt regimes to which banks and other financial institutions should no longer be able to lend. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial interests are protected. this is how we prevent the people from being the victims of marcos, kabila, abacha or any of their successors. i also propose that a small percentage, half a percent, of the amount paid directly to the government of a country in the form of budgetary sectoral aid, be made compulsory for the watchdogs of civil society organisations. these civil society groups carry out public scrutiny of government revenue and expenditure and the achievement of development goals. there must also be support - and this can be from the same amount - for national parliaments to be able to exercise their control function better together with these watchdogs. a further proposal is to exclude companies involved in corruption in projects in developing countries from european procurement in the future. i would also like to propose that a blacklist be drawn up of these companies. anyone who can show improvement, for example by adjusting management, will be given a second chance and may be removed from the list. finally, all european member states should speed up the ratification of the 2003 un convention against corruption. at present, only two member states have ratified the convention: france and hungary. it's got to be different. this convention is the first international agreement to establish international legal remedies to tackle corruption. i mention the prevention and prosecution of money laundering, the freezing at an early stage of financial assets held in banks abroad and the recovery of these assets in cases of proven corruption. these are some of the proposals i make in my report. of course, these are just a few steps that europe can take in the gigantic problem that corruption poses to society. let us all - the commission, parliament and the member states - make this a priority and not only point the finger at the developing countries, but also put our hand in our own bosom. i am curious to see what the commission's reaction will be to these specific proposals. because remember: it takes two to tango.
mr president, i should like to support the report by mr van den berg. corruption is an obstacle to the optimal efficiency of aid. a proactive approach to corruption is therefore desirable. the report proposes concrete and useful instruments for strengthening the control mechanisms. this will create a better legal framework for the fight against corruption. the legal framework is not the only factor, however. rejection of corruption as a social phenomenon is equally linked to prevailing values in a society. civil society organisations, particularly religious leaders, should therefore be called upon to play their role in promoting good governance and fighting corruption. the debate on this ideal level needs to be strengthened. after all, both donor countries and donors to aid agencies must be able to trust that their funds are being used efficiently for their intended purpose. mr president, an expert whom i consulted on the van den berg report told me the day before yesterday that he hoped the dutch government would apply this excellent report without delay. i can't think of a better compliment to give my colleague.

president. - the next item is the report (a4-0176/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs and security, on the situation in iraq. we have now had practical experience of whether you can really improve a situation like this through outside intervention. perhaps that is the case for some groups. this applies to the kurds in the north, who had in fact long since seceded from iraq, and also to the political opponents who had fled abroad. for most people, iraq is not their common state but a battleground between foreign interests and the very different group interests and views of shiites, sunnis, kurds and also the pre-islamic christian minorities, who unlike the other groups do not have their own territory as a possible member state in a federation. we fully share mr posselt's concerns about this group. yet, under the present circumstances, no one can offer a real solution. the foreign occupier has further degraded the prison system and allowed the reintroduction of the death penalty, and cannot offer the vast majority of the population any prospects for the future. the resolution rightly identifies a number of errors, but it is far too optimistic and does not really help to solve the problems. it is important to learn that military intervention solves nothing.
author. mr president, egypt's stability has long been based on the model of a one-party state. the party no longer represents a common ideology after the period of president nasser, but only the common interest in the possession of governmental power. outside of this monopoly on state power, individual outsiders can now participate in elections, but only as long as they do not become a threat to that established position of power. this puts opposition candidates at constant risk of harassment, intimidation or imprisonment. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. the state of emergency declared in 1981 is still an important instrument for the continuation of this situation. this time, former mp and former presidential candidate ayman nour has fallen victim to it. the contribution of the egyptian state to stability in the middle east, and in particular to peaceful coexistence with neighbouring israel since 1978, has led to remarkably little criticism of this situation from the european side. on the contrary, the european union is seeking good cooperation with all the mediterranean coastal states. my group wholeheartedly supports the demand that egypt democratize, end the state of emergency and stop obstructing the opposition.
the central question in this debate is what is the best way to ensure that the reforms, particularly in the field of justice and the fight against corruption and organised crime, continue beyond 2007. what does the commission now think is the best way of ensuring that? what do we think? should we now stick to the 2007 date or should we still postpone accession to 2008? my group is united in its support for enlargement, further enlargement of the european union. it was and it is. if in future countries in the western balkans or turkey meet the conditions, we would welcome them. nevertheless, on that basis, there are two possible answers to that central question. part of my group says that precisely because we are so strongly in favour of enlargement, we must be extra critical and see whether the countries at the beginning of accession actually fulfil the conditions, in order to preserve the credibility of the eu and its enlargement strategy. if we look at romania and bulgaria, with all the differences between them, we must note that at the moment - i stress this - they do not meet a number of the conditions that have been set, and that there is justified scepticism as to whether this will be the case in eight months' time, on 1 january 2007. and that is why part of my group thinks it is justified to keep the pressure on until the very last moment, until 1 january 2008, to ensure that all countries meet those criteria. another part of the group, including myself, thinks that there is a difference between romania and bulgaria. if we look at the last year in bulgaria, we see that reforms are happening superficially, that implementation is weak, that the pace of reforms has slowed down and that some crucial reforms have been implemented at the very last moment. on the other hand, if you look at romania, you can see (not only from us in parliament, but also from the reports of many national and international ngos and experts) that the pace has increased and that there is indeed tangible progress, not only on paper but also in practice. for example, judges and prosecutors finally feel free, free from the burden of political influence. the independence of the fight against corruption is really guaranteed and fortunately a number of cases of high-level corruption have finally been initiated. it is therefore also right to reward this tangible progress in romania by saying that we are sticking to the 2007 accession date but - and i say this to mr neyts - only with a strong monitoring mechanism, not in the general sense as the commissioner has said, but specifically in the areas of justice, reform and the fight against corruption. we believe that it is necessary to look at whether these positive developments will continue after accession and we believe that this could even have financial consequences if this is not the case. the message we want to send to the people of the eu, but also to the candidate countries like croatia and turkey, is that if you really put in the effort to reform, you will be rewarded as a candidate country. on the other hand, if you think the eu will turn a blind eye, you will be punished.

i have two arguments in favour of this. firstly, this commission has been in office for about one month in 2004 and secondly, this commission has started very ambitiously in order to obtain a positive declaration of assurance. i'll get back to you on that. it is a pity that terry wynn is not here, but i would like to start with the resolution he passed last year, which introduced some new elements. the most important element was that we felt that the highest political authority in each member state should make a statement at the beginning and end of each financial year that everything was in order as far as political control was concerned. this demand of parliament has met with great resistance in the member states, and that is regrettable. in the meantime, a few things have happened. we have concluded an interinstitutional agreement and in it we have reached a compromise with the council, in which we say that it does not matter whether it is at the highest political level or not. we also accept a declaration at a certain level appropriate to that in each member state, provided that such a declaration is made. my question to the commission is what it is going to do about the interinstitutional agreement. secondly, there have been a number of developments in the meantime, namely that some countries now say they object to this. what is the commission doing with this attitude? i have the impression that more and more countries are joining in. i have to say this to all of you. it is never the member states alone that are responsible. it is always the european commission which has the ultimate responsibility. we should welcome the commission's action plan. it is good that there are common standards, that there is integrated control, that there is better cooperation with the national audit bodies, and so on. and we would also appreciate it in another respect if the commission would indicate risk areas in the budget, namely what are high risk areas and low risk areas. it is therefore important that member states assume political responsibility. we are asking the commission to do the same. it's not just the ceo who has to sign off on the financial statements. we believe that the commissioner responsible for a particular policy should also sign it. i have to go through the points quickly because the clock is ticking. the president of the european court of auditors published his annual report again in november and made an interesting suggestion. he suggested that a peer review should take place, that former presidents of national audit bodies should look at whether the methodology and the way the european court of auditors works is correct or not. we think this is important, we welcome it and we would like to cooperate with the european court of auditors on this. there is still more work to be done for the european court of auditors. the discharge report specifically requests two reports. firstly, the management of the money which the european union gives to united nations bodies. what is the added value for europe if we give the money through the united nations? and secondly, how is the financial control there? we also want the non-governmental organizations to be subject to an inquiry by the court of auditors. how much non-governmental is there to these non-governmental organizations? how much money do they have? finally, the next few years will be crucial for the commission. there has to be a positive reliability statement sometime. i would like to thank the members of the cocobu secretariat for their great efficiency and competence, and of course also my personal staff.
mr president, the risk of debates like this is always that members start repeating what others have said. so firstly let me start by repeating the criticism that a number of members of this house have made concerning the timing of this debate. it is a scandal that we are not having this debate at a time that would do justice to the importance of the reports that we are discussing. i would like to agree with mr staes in saying that mr ferber’s report was excellent. the pse group has absolutely no problems with the report as it is presented right now. it calls for an assistants’ statute and a dialogue with the bureau about their political role: these are all things that we welcome. however, i think that we will see an overwhelming majority from all the political groups behind his proposal not to grant discharge tomorrow, and i welcome that. it is only right. it is only right that we will not grant discharge to a report that would say that it is ok that we pay x amount of rent that we now all know is probably too high. in fact i do not agree with the members that said that the press statement that strasbourg sent out today is only adding to the lack of clarity. it is very clear what they are saying. it says here on the second page of the press statement that they need money for services rendered and that there is a risk of the building at one point being empty and that risk needs to be financed. i am sorry, but where was the invoice for this?
