- i'm the reporter. mr president, commissioner, let me begin by expressing my support for the commission's approach that you have proposed, namely less but better state aid. i shall continue to praise the commission throughout my speech, but i am afraid i must begin by making one small criticism. i find it regrettable that the commission is already putting forward proposals, even if they are only draft proposals, a week before the european parliament votes on our position and our recommendations. i think that if we consider each other as allies in the fight for a stronger economy, we should also cooperate in that sense and respect each other. i therefore assume that even if we now come up with proposals, they will still be taken into consideration. the core message of the report, which we hope will be adopted tomorrow, is that state aid must remain the exception. it is a tool to correct where the market is not delivering the desired results. state aid is not a standard policy instrument with which the public can create innovation, so to speak, and it should not be a remedy for our failure to implement structural reforms. all studies show that state aid is not the decisive factor in business decisions and state aid is not the main competitive factor. it is much more important that we create favourable conditions for innovation, experimentation, risk-taking and above all for investment. because the investors will come out of the market if there is money to be made and if the barriers are removed. that means that we must work towards a good patent system, fair competition and free trade in the internal market, and in this connection i also regret - i will say this again - that we have not succeeded in opening up the market for services. we need to work on an immigration policy that will keep the best people in europe and attract talent from outside. we need to work on a good supply of financial services, a well-educated workforce, and so on. state aid can indeed distort the market to the extent that it actually hinders innovation developments. the balancing test proposed in your document seems to me to be a good instrument. the emphasis on evaluating the results of past aid is also in line with parliament's position, which is also taken up in my colleague mr hökmark's report. and i am actually curious to hear what the commission will think about state aid being granted in cases where there is a clear need for structural reforms, but where the member states simply fail to do so. i would draw a parallel here with an initiative which i myself find rather absurd, and that is the globalisation fund. if we fail to make the european union competitive, we should not try to find a remedy. an interesting example in this context is that just today the government of one of the large member states is announcing a new injection of €600 million for innovation - which is very welcome in itself - and that on the same day the oecd is issuing a rather critical report on the results of the previous support programme of that same large member state. i highly recommend its reading. i am very pleased that the commission's proposals, as parliament will also recommend, also include non-technological innovation, because that is absolutely a very important development in our service economy. but as far as the practical effects and criteria are concerned, the rules seem to be very much based on innovation in the technological sectors, and i would therefore like to insist that the rules for non-technological innovation are also workable. i also expressed my concern - we met yesterday - about the so-called matching clause. it is indeed written in the text - i have just checked it - that the percentage of aid authorized may be increased if a competitor from a non-eu country receives more subsidies. i think that this is a very important point. because if we have a matching clause for state aid, then i would like to have one for all those other policy areas that i have just mentioned, which are much more important competitive factors. large companies are also a point of discussion and i see that there are all sorts of precautions so that we do not subsidize individual large companies, but i still see a small point of concern. i see in the proposals that the large companies can get a so-called top up, a so-called bonus of 15% aid intensity if two or more large companies cooperate across borders. i'll round off, chairman. could that mean we're going to subsidize multinationals? i don't think that's the point. i'll leave it at that, because i've already used up my time.
it is good that we are speaking today on this important subject. unfortunately, when it comes to innovation, we in europe are still lagging behind our competitors and it is therefore good that the commission in its plans for reform of state aid policy places great emphasis on measures, especially for smes, to boost innovation and innovative entrepreneurship in the european union. the report by mr int'l'feld, our colleague and rapporteur, which is before us, has the full support of the ppe-de and i thank the rapporteur for her good report and also for the good cooperation. the basic principles on the basis of which and the groups for which aid for innovation can be granted are clearly stated in the report. we know that you, commissioner, always take the views of this parliament very seriously. and that is why, despite your statement, i am still unpleasantly surprised, with the in t'feld committee, by the fact that the european commission presented the preliminary framework last week. it was for the informal council, but you would have been disappointed if you had formally postponed that consultation in order to take account of parliament's opinion. that would have only delayed it by a few weeks. there are a number of points in the report which i would urge you to take into account and give greater weight to. and fortunately two of those points have been on the table today, so we must finally agree on the substance. i am particularly concerned with the contribution of large companies and, as you say, of course, in the context of clusters. but we should not underestimate their innovative power and the support that may be required in cluster terms. i would also like to emphasize non-technological innovation again and, thirdly and lastly, that there should also be more clarity on the way in which state aid applies to universities and research institutions.
the need to agree on ecall at european level is great, mr president, because only a standardised system can guarantee assistance in the event of accidents abroad. and in view of the still increasing international traffic, this is of great importance. the ecall system can make an important contribution to reducing the number of road deaths in europe, in addition to 112, but member states will have to speed up its implementation. that is true of the 112 system, but it is certainly true of the ecall system. my colleagues have already raised this issue. fortunately, the netherlands can be a good example here because they do make sails, but we must of course do it all over europe. the cost of the ecall system should not be excessive for its successful implementation. the cost savings can be achieved by making the most of the possibilities of the technology, without, of course, jeopardizing the emergency response function. i therefore think it is a missed opportunity that parliament does not explicitly recognise the need for a so-called open platform structure, which would allow every motorist to call emergency services via the ecall technology, for example. in such a case, service providers could pay for the use of the technical infrastructure and thus the costs could be shared more. i very much hope that the member states will allow the wide range of uses of this technology to be exploited, again, of course, without compromising safety. i welcome the broad call from this parliament to the member states to speed up and we will be following this closely from parliament in the coming days.

in writing. since 1999, all dutch members have undertaken not to participate in the pension fund. this is because, on the one hand, participation gives rise to a right to a double pension and, on the other hand, the pension premium is largely - or in practice even entirely - paid by parliament. it is extremely regrettable that the european parliament will be responsible for the shortfalls which have now arisen from mismanagement of the members' pension fund. in this way the taxpayers are turning a blind eye to the irresponsible behaviour of their elected representatives, who are primarily seeking to enrich themselves excessively. i am even more disappointed that the justified criticism of the pension fund, which was included in the original version of the ferber report, has been largely removed by the committee on budgetary control. the commission has failed completely as a monitoring body. that is why i shall not vote for this discharge.

vice-president of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. i would like to reply on behalf of the commission in the same constructive spirit as the rapporteur has just spoken and say: the most important thing about this project is that the institutions work closely together. i am very grateful to you, mrs kaufmann, for saying that from a political point of view this is a project which parliament fully supports. i know that from the debate we had earlier, but the confirmation was important. i'm also happy to admit that the test run we're talking about today was workable. the commission will certainly take up a number of suggestions you have made in your report. i think we will have no grounds for complaint in future cooperation on this matter. but i would like to describe again what we actually did. the legislative procedures under way have been examined in the light of the new aspects and priorities which have emerged in the meantime. what happened in the meantime? the commission's work is focused on a strategy for growth and employment, and we wanted to know whether the proposals on the table are in line with the objectives and priorities of that strategy for growth and employment. as you know, mrs kaufmann, the problem with a whole series of these proposals was that there had been no impact assessment at all, although there were massive economic implications of individual proposals. that's the second argument. the commission had already decided last year that it would no longer submit proposals without a comprehensive impact assessment tool, backed up by methodological guarantees. the commission has also been asked to draw up a report on the implementation of the directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos. and finally, the question was: does a proposal have any chance of being accepted? i am at the point you have rightly raised. there have been some proposals on which parliament does not share the commission's view, for example on the legal status of mutual societies. these proposals have been in the council for 12 years without the slightest change. there is no indication that the council intends to deal with these proposals. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the commission has announced, in the very cases where parliament has expressed its concerns, that it will examine the underlying issues in detail - i recognise the social importance of these issues - and that it will then, if necessary, make new proposals which have a chance of actually being dealt with in due course. as regards the question of justification, i agree with you. i do not see this as a political or legal problem. the commission was given detailed reasons for each proposal when it took its decision. i cannot explain to you why these particular reasons have not been put to the house. i didn't have the slightest problem doing that. i have also personally handed over the individual explanations to the rapporteurs of the political groups. so we will certainly be able to take these proposals into account. it is important to me - and i want to make this clear - that the rapporteur states that withdrawing proposals is part of the exercise of the right of initiative. the commission is also open in principle to its proposal that in future the annual legislative and work programme should take into account whether and, if so, which proposals the commission intends to withdraw, so that it can be prepared in good time. i think that's a very reasonable suggestion. i hope you can see from my reaction that i don't think we should be having a debate about principles here. i'm happy to admit that things could have gone better at one point or another. it is important to me that we work together to make the political project a success, and i can only agree with the rapporteur that this is what the citizens of europe expect from us. they do not expect deregulation, they do not expect a reduction in rights and guarantees, but they do expect clear, simple, understandable and manageable european legislation. that is what we are going to do together.
i would like to make two comments on mr kaufmann's report, which i can broadly support. first of all, i would like to point out the need to check the commission's legislative proposals not only against the principle of better regulation and the lisbon agenda, but also against subsidiarity. i know that there is a growing aversion to this concept in this house, but that does not change the fact that european legislation must have a clear added value over national legislation. i believe that the fight against overregulation at european level is an important part of the drive towards a better and simplified community legislative environment. my second comment concerns the role of national parliaments. i support the initiative of commission president barroso to send new legislative proposals to the national parliaments. subsidiarity can be carefully tested in the member states. this will certainly increase the support for european legislation in the member states.
i would like to thank mrs poli bortone for her report. i think we have reached a very good compromise with her, with each other and with the council. i also thank mrs sommer and the other groups who have worked on this. many consumers are concerned about their health and cholesterol, blood pressure or struggle with excess weight. the food industry is playing on this. there's a proliferation of all kinds of claims. this morning i had a sandwich that's good for my gut flora, buttered with butter that lowers my cholesterol, and a little egg that's high in omega-3. fine... i guess, because i don't know. consumers do not know whether the claims made by manufacturers are true and therefore it is very important that this bill requires that the health claims made by food manufacturers must be scientifically substantiated. we must prevent consumers from being misled. consumers are often willing to pay more for a product that claims to do something for their health. but then the consumer must be able to trust that it is true. misleading advertising is already prohibited, but the burden of proof has been on the authorities. in practice, of course, that doesn't work. governments can't spend all day chasing claims or packaging and then try to prove that they're wrong. this proposal has helped consumers. in all 25 countries, manufacturers must now provide honest information. in the first reading parliament gave in to the pressure of companies which were afraid of being exposed, companies which deliberately mislead consumers by misrepresenting the facts, and which can in no way count on my sympathy. no profit on the backs of people who are worried about their weight or their health. the internal market must not be a free-for-all for misleading consumers. fortunately, the compromise broadly recognises this. deception comes in many forms. article 4 states that health claims may only be made on products meeting a certain profile. and, mrs. sommer, those nutritional profiles are real. they exist in the united states, all the big companies are working with them, and it's working very well. it is also right that nutrition claims should only be made on products that are in principle healthy. because the fat burger doesn't get healthy by adding a little bit of vitamins to it. the sugary lollipop is not going to be a healthy food with vitamin a either. the compromise is against health claims on alcohol. nutrition claims on alcohol may only be made if they are reduced in alcohol or calories. when you look at all the problems with alcoholism, liver damage and brain damage, it's hard to justify health claims about alcohol. fortunately, all parties were willing to support this proposal in the end. the first reading showed that the proposal is unnecessarily bureaucratic. that was the view of our group, but we wanted to ensure that there were adequate safeguards for proper procedures. we've reached that point. for claims directed at children and for claims relating to reduced disease risk, a cumbersome authorisation procedure applies. our group stands with the consumers and with the benevolent companies. this legislation ensures fair information to consumers. i hope and expect that this legislation will contribute to better nutrition and better health. thank you for your attention.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the health claims are not used in the same way as the products. the persistent lobby of the food and drink industry in particular seems to have been less effective this time. or are members simply ashamed of the fact that the council was for once more progressive than parliament? the compromise proposal now before us has some good points. banning health claims on alcohol is clearly a win. the nutrition profiles are back, and hopefully, at least, they'll prevent the most outrageous health claims. unfortunately, consumer protection against false claims will be far from watertight. there are still many loopholes in the law that allow for unfair child support claims. i also have serious doubts about the very influential position that the efsa is going to take. the independence of the efsa is regularly called into question. i think that in other dossiers such as gmos and aspartame the efsa is very quick to adopt the industry's position without hesitation. but, imperfect as this proposal is, it is at least considerably better than the sad result at first reading. moreover, without compromise, we risk dragging this case out for years. it is unacceptable that consumers should not be given clarity for so long. my group will therefore support the compromise proposal.
i would like to thank mr scheele and mrs poli bortone for their contribution to the conclusion of both agreements. as regards the report on additives, i regret that this agreement prevents a real debate in the house on the usefulness and necessity of certain additives. the use of certain substances has been clearly demonstrated, but i have my doubts about the addition of fluoride, for example. that is one of the reasons why some of my group will not support this agreement. i would also like to say on the subject of food and health claims that i will agree with the outcome of the negotiations. the amendment by this house at first reading has already improved many points in the common position. i would point out that companies can apply in their own member state and that the deadlines have been shortened. i would like to ask commissioner kyprianou what time the european commission expects to have to draw up the nutritional profiles and what role it thinks the european parliament could play in this. in conclusion, i would like to say that i fully support the agreement on food claims and hope that it can be properly worked out in the member states so that consumers can enjoy good protection.
the compromise reached between parliament and the council is not perfect, but i can support it. it is much less bureaucratic than the original proposal, without prejudice to consumer protection. consumers are given proper information, but not handed it. names like "cough drop" or "cough candy" can survive, for everyone understands that these sweets do not cure coughs instantly and forever. consumers should be able to rely on the fact that nutrition and health claims are genuinely true and scientifically substantiated. research has shown that 53% of consumers trust nutrition claims. we have removed unnecessary bureaucracy from the regulation without affecting consumer protection. this enables the consumer to make an informed choice. but at the same time, the baker on the corner can just go on promoting his high-fiber sandwich without having to go through an age-long authorization process.
i would like to thank commissioner borg very warmly for the excellent cooperation and also for the way in which we were able to examine the technical possibilities. i shall come back to some of the comments on amendments. i would like to speak first about the intention of this debate. the debate on the regulation on the restoration of the status quo shows that the european parliament can legislate in areas where it has only the right to give its opinion. although inland fishing is formally a matter for the member states and not for the european union, in close cooperation with my colleagues in the european parliament and with the european commissioner for fisheries, mr borg, we have managed to give the eel a literal helping hand. the special thing is that the eel is both a saltwater and a freshwater fish. the regulation of fisheries at sea is a matter for the eu, but on rivers and inside dikes it is a matter for the member states. in this way, the eel was literally stranded for years. the necessary regulation, both at european and national level, was not in place and the needle kept slipping through the net of the legislator. the decline in the standard of living, however, has been dramatic. the number of young eels at sea, the so-called glass eel, has declined by more than 95% over the last half-century. and for the adult eel, this is not much better: there's only 25% left. it is therefore five to twelve for the european aal and a broad and integrated approach is badly needed. and for that reason i also thank commissioner borg for having translated the owninitiative report of the european parliament very quickly into a proposal for a regulation. the only way to bring about a change is to bring about a united approach by all those involved, the commercial fishermen, the sport fishermen, regional and national authorities, environmental and nature protection organisations. it is therefore unique that the new approach to the conservation of eel is supported by all these parties in the following way. to restore the status quo, the fishing for eel is to be halved. however, instead of this halving - which in practice would often mean closure - member states can also draw up a management plan for the recovery of the eel. they can do this for the whole territory, for a region or for the catchment area of a river. and this management plan can be the ultimate collaboration between all parties involved. so it is not a top-down approach from brussels or from the capitals of the member states, but a bottom-up approach involving citizens and outsiders. this approach can regulate the export of glassware and can be used in particular for european rivers, canals and lakes. the recovery plan also provides a huge incentive to increase the use of technical measures, such as hydroelectric power stations, locks and dikes, to ensure that fishing is not hindered. the broad support from north to south in the european parliament's committee on fisheries is key to successful decision-making in the european fisheries council. if the european commission adopts the proposed amendments of parliament, as commissioner borg has already indicated on a number of points, a successful decisionmaking process can be ensured with a wide support - from sweden to spain - from fishermen, sport fishermen and environmental organisations. that means that the commission and the council must not let this unique opportunity pass. i am particularly grateful to the commissioner for accepting a large number of amendments. i also thank him for the initiatives he has taken with his colleague mr mandelson, particularly in relation to international trade policy, to make it possible to regulate not only the catches of glass eel but also the exports in this area. this opens up prospects for the use of the glasshouse, including in european waters, and it also makes it possible to restore the commitment to sustainability. the commissioner rightly pointed out that parliament had removed a specific sustainability criterion in an amendment and he indicated that he wished to examine with the technical specialists how to arrive at an objective method of measurement. i welcome that, because that is also the spirit that has lived in this parliament. but the specific figure of 40% in particular has caused problems here and there and perhaps regional differences in this area can also be taken into account. in short, i am glad that the commissioner has accepted a large number of amendments. and as far as trade policy and a number of other areas are concerned, i am convinced that we must now succeed in getting things back to a much better state in the european union in five or ten years' time. this will benefit everyone, not just the citizens but also the fisheries, the sport fishermen and all those who care about the eel. i thank my colleagues once again for the excellent cooperation in the preparation of this report and i look forward to the further debate and also the vote on my report.
mr president, fisheries agreements must be used to promote sustainable fishing. unfortunately, that is not always the case. the 22 trawlers that are authorized are also subject to questioning under this agreement. but this agreement is more limited than the previous one, which ran until 1999, and it excludes a number of vulnerable fish species, especially octopus and crustaceans. there is more emphasis on small-scale fishing, and that is important. the agreement with morocco will contribute to the sustainable management of scarce fish stocks and will be based on the principle of maximum sustainable yield. that is very good, of course, but unfortunately parliament can hardly assess all this at the moment when we are giving our opinion on the new agreement with morocco. we have no access to the commission's ex ante evaluation. i support the rapporteur's amendments which call for more information both on the fish stocks and on the way in which the agreement benefits the parties concerned. but i also strongly urge that in future parliament should have timely access to these evaluations or impact assessments so that we can give our informed opinion. and then, of course, there is the question of western sahara. the report is incomplete on this point. the agreement is not limited to the moroccan coastal waters but also applies to the waters belonging to the western sahara. although a joint committee should ensure that the people of western sahara will benefit from this agreement, it is still a question whether the saharawi people will actually benefit from it. at least that's not clear in the agreement. we should state unequivocally that this agreement will only concern the moroccan coastal waters. i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this.
mr president, this is not the first time that this house has debated the future of europe. the contribution of the european commission to the european council of june 2006, which we are discussing this morning, unfortunately offers little analysis. i have one comment and three recommendations. the loss of sovereignty was one of the main reasons for the dutch "no". the commissioner has been informed of this by a report of his own. it is incomprehensible that the commission should then try to extend the european union's powers in the area of freedom, security and justice through the back door. i support the extension of the period of reflection, provided that it reflects on the future of europe. the text of the constitution must be abandoned and a rearrangement of the existing treaties must lead to a handy institutional framework. this framework - call it a basic treaty - must favour concrete policies over the creation of new institutions such as an eu foreign minister. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environment from harmful substances. we are not going to get anywhere with that, it will not impress the dutch voters and my dutch colleagues know that too.
the debate on the future of europe will continue for some time, mr president. in fact, the debate on the course and policy of the european union is a permanent fact. and the commission rightly points out that plan d is not linked exclusively to the debate on the constitutional treaty, important as that is. it is a first exercise in a more open, more active european communication. more and better communication is an essential precondition for successful european cooperation. last week the commission presented the first results of operation plan d, which i warmly welcome. the commission clearly indicates the concrete actions taken and by this alone it points out the importance of a clear message. at the same time, we must be careful not to fall into the trap of communicating for the sake of communicating. less is often more in communication. moreover, the way you talk is only one of the two components of successful communication. we must also ask ourselves what exactly we want to say about the future of europe and, above all, how that message relates to the actions that the european union is taking. the information which has been produced by the european commission's activities so far is very interesting in this respect, and i refer to the eurobarometer. the surveys show that europeans have high expectations of the european union in many areas and the conclusion is therefore inevitable that a negative vote on the eu is at least partly prevented by the fact that the european union is not performing as it should. i therefore welcome the commission's focus on a concrete policy agenda. but does that sufficiently reflect the context in which we must operate? i have a few notes. firstly, the citizens' agenda must be sufficiently substantial to meet the expectations of our citizens to some extent. has the commission gone far enough in this respect or has it only looked at policies already in the pipeline? we must not only look at expectations - as the commission has clearly done - but also at the experience of the citizens. should we not also investigate the way in which citizens experience europe and ask ourselves whether we can make a positive contribution to everyday life with the citizens' agenda? secondly, such an agenda creates expectations and we will have to live up to them. because if we say loudly and clearly what we want to do and then do not do it, it will only contribute to a negative vote on the european union and that brings me to my final point. that is the question of the direction of the debate on the future of europe in the various member states. the commission has been able to identify the issues that are more common among europeans with the results of the eurobarometer survey, but how does it assess the state of play in all these national debates on european cooperation, its course and the constitution? is there a certain convergence or are we still as far along as we were in july 2005? all the institutions have a role to play, but i would like to point out to the council, especially as a member of parliament, its special responsibility, the responsibility for implementing a substantial policy agenda which is necessary to get citizens behind the european project.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the transport of goods by road is not subject to excessive congestion. the white paper on transport rightly mentions the transition of goods transport from road to rail and inland waterway as an important objective. this must continue after the upcoming update of the white paper. because without a modal shift, our transport cannot meet the demands of sustainability. the fact that the ambitions of marco polo ii have been significantly reduced from eur 740 million to eur 400 million until 2013 may hamper and delay the achievement of this objective. nevertheless, my group still believes that the programme before us is of great importance in improving the environment and curbing the totally uncontrolled growth of road transport. my group supports the extension of the programme to surrounding countries outside the european union and also the additional attention to shifting more freight transport, which is still taking place along coastal roads, to motorways by sea. at least as important is the reduction of unnecessary transport by better logistical coordination. the number of kilometres travelled can be reduced by avoiding idling, by reducing the distances between the different stages of machining as much as possible and by making the best use of cargo space. we have always advocated the principle of transport avoidance introduced here. the reduction of the thresholds for subsidies will create additional bureaucratic burdens. although the proposed volume equivalents are a good complement to the calculation of tonne-kilometres saved, the coefficient of 1 to 1 applied for this purpose may lead to all applications being submitted in volume units in future, as this is more favourable to undertakings.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the programme is not only accessible to small businesses, but also to smes. since the transport sector is home to a large number of these types of companies, this is a significant gain. the main reasons for this are the pooling of projects and the reduction of financial and administrative burdens. the only fundamental objection i have to this programme is that there is no evaluation of marco polo i. the absence of such an evaluation means that it is not possible at this stage to say whether marco polo ii will have the desired effect, simply because the relationship between objective and resources used is unknown. i am therefore glad that there will be an evaluation next year. i hope that the findings will strengthen the design and implementation of the programme. i would like to conclude by thanking the rapporteur for the result achieved. i will certainly support it.

the two dutch protestant parties which i am allowed to represent in this house have consistently advocated the healing of the post-war division of europe. at the same time, we have always been in favour of a solid enlargement of the european union, and therefore of a clear respect for the accepted accession criteria, the well-known copenhagen criteria. we were struck by the fall-back of romania and bulgaria in 2004 in the biggest enlargement in the union's history. after the revolution of the european year of miracles 1989, the ecclesial and social contacts between our supporters and the two candidate countries were strengthened. we were able to gain a certain understanding of the 2004 postponement. what is the situation now with regard to the two accession dates of 1 january 2007 and 1 january 2008? as i said, my party is in favour of a solid accession of sofia and bucharest, that is to say on the basis of separate assessments of bulgaria and romania. the word 'solid' means facts, actual steps towards reform, not intentions. finally, the council and the commission did not add two specific safeguard clauses to the three usual ones in the accession treaties with bulgaria and romania for nothing. that is the message we are sending to the commission. i would like to see a clear signal in october on the accession readiness of bulgaria and romania as of 1 january 2007, because clarity is in the interests of all parties in europe.

the forerunners of the european union, mr president, started with six states, mainly in the direct sphere of influence of brussels and strasbourg. no one could seriously foresee at the time that these european communities would develop into the umbrella association of 27 or more european states. since the early 1970s, however, these precursors have gradually succeeded in establishing a monopoly position in cooperation between european states. states that were formerly members of the european free trade association (efta) or the council for mutual economic assistance (comecon) are now members of the european union. the forthcoming accession of romania and bulgaria will be more or less the final piece of this. it is to the advantage of their membership that this enlargement can be interpreted as a victory in the cold war over the former adversary. it provides support that otherwise would not be there. that is why this parliament decided last year that the two countries which had not completed the preparations in 2004 will still have to be admitted in 2007 or 2008. i voted for it, too. nevertheless, there is widespread criticism of the domestic situation among these two newcomers. the roma population is still being driven out and their houses are being demolished. the construction of motorways and the extraction of minerals are still the most cost-effective means of protecting nature and the environment in the region. there is still a lack of visibility into how governments manage their money and which companies benefit from it. there are still doubts about the independence and objectivity of the judiciary. there are still ethnic groups who feel that they are treated as second-class citizens and many citizens still leave those countries. this makes it very likely that their accession will now be judged in retrospect as a big mistake and a failure. these shortcomings will not alter their membership, but will add to the arguments against further enlargement. the first country to pay the bill is croatia. it already meets those criteria better than romania and bulgaria and has much in common with the current member state, slovenia, but may have to wait a very long time. this is even more true for the applicant country macedonia, the rest of the balkans, moldova and ukraine. how can we prevent such a negative outcome? lasting solidarity may be better served by more active support to move faster to union level than by rapid admission or more free markets. in the dutch parliament, both my party, the socialist party, and the christian democrats have concluded that the risks of enlargement in 2007 are too great.

mr president, for half a century bulgaria was under communist rule. however, when the berlin wall fell it was brought under post-communist rule. agents of the former secret service, the darzavna sigurnost, continued to control the state. the west did business with them during the privatisations, which were often carried out illegally. financial support from the west hardly reached the unemployed bulgarians and the victimised roma. only the former secret agents flourished in their roles as businessmen, judges, bankers, politicians and leaders of ngos. has the population ever had a fair chance under post-communist rule? in the 2001 progress report, the rapporteur, mr van orden, urged the bulgarian authorities to provide further information on the murder of dissident writer georgi markov in london in 1978. it was one of the many contractual killings left unaccounted for long ago and more recently. the report stresses the need for greater efforts for the social inclusion of roma. i also agree with the commission that further efforts are needed to combat all forms of intolerance, racism and xenophobia. i appeal, therefore, to all my fellow meps to sign written declaration no 19 – initiated by meps from five different political groups – on protection of people in bulgaria against neo-totalitarianism. holocaust-deniers in bulgaria close to the former secret service do not belong in the eu. i am also grateful that the commission has concerns about the unacceptable living conditions of the roma. they are scapegoats, like jews, turks and homosexuals. finally, i am very pleased that last week the bulgarian minister of the interior, mr petkov, promised the verts/ale delegation that all the archives of the darzavna sigurnost would be opened before 20 july. only then will we know who killed georgi markov, who committed other crimes and who is really ruling bulgaria today. let us support real reformers and free bulgaria from its past, instead of postponing its future.

in writing. the european parliament has received an additional €4 billion in the eu budget during the negotiations on the financial perspective for the period 2007-13. this addition is unnecessary, all the more so because the additional resources are used to support policies which cannot be considered as core tasks of the european union. this supports my earlier statement that this house is merely paying lip service to the principle of subsidiarity. the interinstitutional agreement contains good elements in terms of better management of the eu budget and more effective fight against irregularities and fraud, with member states also taking their responsibility. i am not in favour of spending money on the globalisation fund, the flexibility instrument, the solidarity fund and possibly on european schools.
in writing. the vvd group voted today in favour of the roth behrendt report, although it is not entirely satisfied with the compromise reached between the european parliament and the european council. the compromise takes steps to relax the ban on feeding animal meal to non-ruminants, provided that animals are not fed animal meal from their own species. for this purpose, reference is made to the regulation on animal by-products (ec 1774/2002), which prohibits the feeding of animal by-products within the species, on the grounds of possible risks to food safety. the vvd welcomes this relaxation but is dissatisfied that the compromise does not say anything about imposing the same requirements on imported products. products from non-ruminants from third countries do not have to meet any conditions in the field of animal meal feeding. the vvd believes that products from third countries should meet the same requirements, especially if food safety is at stake. the vvd voted in favour of the compromise because it is an improvement on the present situation in many other respects. the adoption of the compromise means agreement at first reading, so that these improvements can be implemented in the short term.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on regional development. president, commissioner, members of the council, concrete support for citizens on both sides of the eu's external borders in the coming years is what we have been particularly committed to in the committee on regional policy, i as co-rapporteur (article 47). and this first reading agreement on the enpi, as mr szymański has already said, is now within reach. the european border regions will be given the opportunity to implement concrete programmes on the external borders in the coming years, also with the help of the european regional development fund, some 700 million euro per year, covering a wide spectrum of areas such as health, education, the economy, the environment, etc. it is very important that we have also reached agreement with the council on implementation. we have been able to continue the interreg methodology in regional policy in particular. in this connection we have been consulting the association of european border regions and the newly formed neighbour network of external eastern border regions. they say they can get away with it: they have received and adopted actionable suggestions. finally, we also agreed on a transitional arrangement. preparatory measures may be financed. and if we fail to reach an agreement with the regions and the national state at the external border, then after 1 june 2010 we will take concrete measures to ensure that the money can be spent inside the border in the member states and not be lost. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the establishment of a european environment agency.
mr president, a year and a half ago the european commission put forward new proposals for the financial instruments for external policy, including development cooperation, with the aim of simplification and rationalisation. i couldn't agree more. but the way the commission wanted to do that would mean that, whereas the european parliament now has a codecision right - with 16 schemes - it would in future have only an advisory role in the field of development cooperation. the european parliament calls for co-decision on the thematic and geographical content of the policy and the distribution of the money between the priorities. and then the commission - this is another issue in this instrument - wants to merge economic cooperation with non-oecd countries and development cooperation by using a dual legal basis. and that is and remains unacceptable to the european parliament. we have now lost 18 months, we believe, in the absence of new proposals from the commission. but it's not too late. the committee on development and cooperation is prepared to set up a task force on the basis of the mitchell report and we are prepared to move quickly. on one condition: the commission should put forward proposals in the coming weeks. firstly, an updated instrument, solely for development aid, based solely on article 179, so limited to official development expenditure. secondly, separate proposals for thematic and geographical programmes. we are prepared to limit the number of arrangements. we want to be able to participate in the decision-making process on the broad lines of policy. thirdly, we want to give financial priorities either through a multiannual financial framework or by including a very limited number of percentages in the geographical and thematic proposals. our proposal is: 50% of all eu oda expenditure for the millennium development goals and within this, a doubling of current spending on primary education and primary health care. it is now up to the commission to decide whether it wants to do business or whether we should continue to block each other. then we block simplification and rationalization, and then we're back to those 16 tools and all the unworkable micromanagement. in the interests of credible and effective poverty reduction, council and commission, accept our outstretched hand. and i thank the austrian presidency for the commitment it has shown so far.

in writing. i voted for the krehl report on the community strategic guidelines on the basis that it goes some way in attempting to inject a more social and environmental side to the commission's proposed strategic guidelines and because it supports sound partnership between all parties and the strengthening of civil society. however, this vote should not imply acceptance of every word of the report. i have grave concerns about the inclusion in ms. krehl's report of a call for the commission to promote the use of public-private partnerships and about the absence of a specific rejection of earmarking and the thinking behind that approach.
in writing. i regret that i have not been able to agree with the reports by berend and galeote. the reason for this is not that i am against mutual solidarity between member states in the event of disasters. both reports together strongly suggest an eu framework in which prevention, response and management of a (natural) disaster play a central role. this could be justified if all member states are faced with this problem to a greater or lesser extent. that's not always the case. the eu's involvement should therefore be limited to complementary actions which cannot be carried out by the member states themselves. the eu's position is more vindicated when eu initiatives focus on cross-border disasters of an unavoidable or uncertain nature whose consequences exceed the capabilities of member states. for that reason, i could have agreed to a limited additional instrument which could be invoked in justified circumstances. nevertheless, these proposals very rightly point to the importance of preventive action. i hope and expect that both the member states and the eu will use all the means at their disposal to minimise the risk of disaster. after all, prevention is always better than cure.

i welcome this proposal because i am convinced that it will lead to more medicines being developed and tested specifically for children. this is particularly important for patients with serious illnesses such as cancer or immune system disorders. but we must not lose sight of the ethical side of the matter. it is important to prevent children from being used as guinea pigs. it is therefore appropriate to include research already carried out in third countries in order to avoid double-checking. it is estimated that between 50 and 90% of the medicines currently used in paediatrics have never been specifically studied or approved for use in that age group and therefore the negative or positive effects of this use are virtually unknown. it is therefore right that european regulations should stimulate research into medicines specifically designed for children and thus put an end to uncertainty.

the nitrate directive says: you may keep 1,7 livestock units per hectare. the groundwater directive says: you're allowed 50 milligrams of nitrate in the groundwater at most. now, suppose the following situation arises. a farmer has 5 cows per acre and a nitrate content in the groundwater of 30 milligrams. it does not comply with the nitrate directive, but it does comply with the groundwater directive. which policy will prevail? this is a matter of double legislation and a clear answer must be given to this. that is why i am proposing that if there is a progress report, and there will be, the commission will take note of it. i can hardly imagine that anyone would object to that, but i have heard from the rapporteur that she does. i am surprised and i would also regret it, because all i ask is for a better evaluation to avoid double legislation. the same applies to manure discharges. there is already legislation on this too. we must avoid giving the average citizen in europe the impression that some bureaucratic monster is being created in brussels which he has no control over but which he must blindly obey. we in the netherlands learned a great deal about this when we voted on the european constitution more than a year ago. one of the arguments was clear: we do not want that bureaucratic monster in brussels and i am a little afraid that with this double legislation we are running a great risk of that. that is why i am tabled my amendments.
president. - mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, as a citizen of a country which is largely below sea level, you will not be surprised to find me particularly interested in the debate on this directive. i live 20 feet below sea level. in recent years, we have seen what a disastrous effect flooding can have. my country, which is not called the netherlands for nothing, has experienced because this is a very fundamental point for me. finally, i would like to thank mr seeber for his good work.
i would like to begin by congratulating mr seeber. i think he has put forward a very good report which we will support in general. floods are a reality for millions of people in europe, a frightening reality. the danube, the rhine and the meuse have all flooded their banks in recent years, with devastating consequences. more and more people are facing flooding. the construction of the outlying valleys has become more intensive and the river beds have become shorter. climate change may increase water supplies. most rivers are international, and therefore an international approach is of great importance. i very much appreciate the commission's proposal, but i also have two comments. first of all, the international approach is still too vague. it just says that problems should not be twisted. that is not enough for me, because the question is: what is a spin? the pse group therefore calls for cooperation between member states to be improved. the starting point should be the natural course of the river. of course, it is not found in many rivers and in many places. but the principle is clear: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's fishing fleet is not disrupted. it is simply a matter of good neighbourliness and that is why i am asking for support for amendment no 82. the second point concerns subsidiarity. europe must not interfere with matters beyond what is strictly necessary. there's every reason for that. the commission has already proposed a number of amendments to the directive, including a proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment against the adverse effects of ionising radiation.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on agriculture and rural development. president, i would like to congratulate mr buzek on his long and intensive work, and of course the commission. the seventh framework programme is a huge step forward for science in the european union with a dramatically increased budget. for the bioeconomy as it is called, that is to say for agricultural research, it is a much bigger step forward because this research will finally have a chapter of its own and that is essential for all agricultural research. it's a huge improvement. it should, because agriculture is in a rapid transformation, technologically from food to food and bioenergy and replacement of fossil fuels and plastics, but also in another transformation, of organizational structures. that research also needs space, and it will be there. one small criticism. there was an amendment from the committee on agriculture which was also adopted and which calls for the sharing of our knowledge with poor countries in the world where agriculture is so important. i would like to have the commission's commitment that this sharing of knowledge will continue, also in the seventh framework programme. this sharing of knowledge must take place, because only then will the framework programme be at the service of what we should really be advocating with the eu in the world, namely a social and sustainable globalisation.
the seventh framework programme for research 2007-2013 includes a controversial component, namely research using embryonic stem cells. this type of research, which involves the killing of human embryos, is ethically and constitutionally problematic. that aspect has not been sufficiently discounted in the text of the committee on industry which is before us for a vote. i would ask you, ladies and gentlemen, to vote in favour of the amendments tabled by mr gargani, mr záborská and myself, and those tabled by the committee on legal affairs and the committee on women's rights. these amendments include the framework programme's focus on the alternatives to embryonic stem cell research. they certainly exist in the form of adult stem cell research and research using stem cells from umbilical cord blood. these offer good prospects for the development of new therapies against diseases such as diabetes and alzheimer's. in addition, embryonic stem cell research is a criminal offence in a number of member states. these member states would then be contributing to research prohibited in their own countries through community funds. this is contrary to the principle of subsidiarity and does not contribute to the confidence of the citizens of the member states in the european institutions. the financing of this research is a clear example of a subject which the european union should leave to the member states. it is important that politics and policy do not accept the fact that embryos are being killed. the european union should not encourage this research. it is important to recognise that there is a growing concern not to produce more embryos than are used per fertility treatment. new ivf techniques are increasing the possibilities for this. finally, in addition to the funding of embryonic stem cell research, there is another undesirable aspect in the proposal from the committee on industry, namely the revision clause. the committee on industry wants to review the exclusion from the european commission's proposal midway through the framework programme. i am thinking, for example, of so-called reproductive cloning and the creation of human embryos for research purposes only, which are not currently eligible for european funding. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. but we must now make a good choice, the choice to invest in the alternative forms of research without ethical problems, because this does not kill embryos. the dignity of life must not be compromised. after all, life is entrusted to us by god himself and we must not take it into our own hands!
the research and technology programme is a real step forward. it is one of the key building blocks for global competitiveness and is a response to globalisation. this european scale and the emphasis on excellence provide essential added value to this european commitment. top-level research at european level, but also a large and balanced effort from the member states and the regions, is urgently needed. as shadow spokesman for capacity - which is part of the research infrastructure - i have looked very closely with the rapporteur, mr buzek, at possible synergies with the structural funds. this will help to build high-quality structures and research infrastructure, and support innovation and production. i am glad that this emphasis will be reinforced again next month when the vote on the 2007-2013 structural funds is taken. in short, less money in asphalt, concrete and roads, more money in research infrastructure and training our people. that is the commitment for the coming years that is important for europe. this will allow a more specific research structure to be created and built up. in fact, with limited resources, in relation to the large research and the large structural funds, we can double the budget. that's a different sound. it can be. i have a question for commissioner potočnik. will we be jointly assessing the structural funds policy and its results and this policy in the future mid-term review? i see little incentive to do so. i say this with a view to the period 2013-2020. finally, i am pleased that some of my amendments have been accepted, concerning medical research, healthy food, aviation, logistics, etc. i shall be voting for a cautious and careful line on stem cell research. i shall support the amendment tabled with mrs niebler.
president. - i should like to begin by congratulating mr buzek and all the shadow rapporteurs. innovation is not the european union's strong point. despite the lisbon agenda and the knowledge economy, it is not easy to show strength, but there is a bright spot and that is the seventh framework programme for research. the programme offers great opportunities for innovation, especially for smes. i expect a lot from the energy research programme. there are opportunities for energy efficiency and renewable energy. not only research but also demonstration projects are important. for example, global warming is possible, as i saw last week in the dutch horticultural sector. but the financial and user sectors are not taking any action. the health programme is also important. health is too important to be left to private sector researchers. the ethical limits are, in my view, well defined. if we look at european industry as a whole, we may be too competitive. cooperation is just as important. we need to move towards a new approach, open innovation, sharing knowledge and sharing knowledge. i hope that the seventh framework programme can contribute to this.

cooperation between the member states. i welcome the commissioner's reference to this, because a recent report in the medical journal the lancet shows that while member states are already well it is unreasonable for us - if we consider ourselves as a parliament - to take note of this and to contact one another to see how we can solve it, without, of course, jeopardizing the freedom of the i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr adamou, for his excellent report. avian influenza remains a major threat to human and animal health in europe and beyond. this clearly requires european coordination. if there is a gap in the measures against avian influenza anywhere in the union, it can have very serious consequences for all of us. but it would be foolish not to look beyond our eu. the virus will not be able to hold on to the external borders. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the 80 million euros currently allocated by the eu to third countries is therefore a little too much. it's not a subject to be cut back on. perhaps it is an idea to abolish the monthly move of parliament from a security point of view to prevent the spread of the virus and to put the 200 million euros that this brings into the fight against the virus. furthermore, prevention is better than cure, so ensuring adequate responses to an outbreak is important. the commission's proposal is to be adopted by the council at the next meeting of the european council in brussels. i believe that the best prevention method is still the vaccination of poultry in europe. not only does this reduce the chance of the virus spreading, but it can also prevent us from killing millions of animals unnecessarily and prevent psychological damage to farmers and aid workers involved.

member of the commission. mr president, it is clear that the situation in tunisia as regards human rights continues to be very serious, not least where freedom of expression is concerned. both international and local human rights organisations, unfortunately, report an increasing number of cases of concern. this must be the starting point for our assessment and our policy, where human rights are taken as the key priority. the commission will continue closely to monitor developments and will use all the means at its disposal to improve the situation. for instance, i hope that the eu-tunisia sub-committee on human rights and democracy will soon be up and running. tunisia has already informally stated that individual cases will be raised, notably in the framework of its political dialogue meetings with the european union. it is evident that a dialogue on human rights must also include individual cases. i have some experience on this as regards the candidate countries, where it is linked to our rigorous conditionality. the application of conditionality is essential for the use of the ‘soft’ power that the european union has at its disposal. i hope that conditions can be established for a fair and smooth implementation of the meda project on modernisation of the judiciary, to which there was a reference, a project for which we have a budget of eur 22 million. that would be an important step forward in enhancing reform and modernisation of the judiciary through the meda programme.
the president of the council. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. relations with russia are - as the chancellor herself, the foreign minister and i have said at this point - of particular strategic importance. it is therefore also understandable that the austrian presidency has given particular priority to the 17th eu-russia summit in sochi on the black sea on 25 may. the summit focused on key issues in our relations with russia, such as the implementation of the joint roadmaps, the situation in the eu and the russian federation, and future treaty relations; he has also dealt with international issues. the debate was, however, focused on the important subject of energy, for obvious reasons. as you all know, at the beginning of the presidency, after only a few hours, there was something which the chancellor called a 'wake-up call' and which led to increased attention on the part of the european union on this subject. i would also like to recall the conclusions of the european council in march. the question of energy relations, in particular with russia, has also been the subject of a thorough internal discussion within the european union. the summit therefore provided a good first opportunity to talk to president putin about the russian-ukrainian gas conflict, which was the starting point of this crisis in january. president putin has strongly defended and tried to explain russia's actions; the european union has a long history of supporting the peace process in the middle east. the discussions in russia were also influenced by the european council in march, which for the first time envisaged the development of a european energy policy. the european council at the end of this week will be discussing the external aspects of the european union's energy policy on the basis of a paper by the high representative and the commission. the european union is also committed to the development of a common foreign and security policy. the energy talks at the summit will also be taken into account in the g8 discussions in st petersburg in july. as you know, the main topic of the russian g8 presidency is the security of energy supply. we have taken this opportunity to have a comprehensive, honest, open energy dialogue. the european union has stated that russia must be, has been and is a reliable partner in the energy sector. we nevertheless expressed our concern about the impact of the russian-ukrainian gas conflict in january on the eu and stressed that our relations in this area must be based on the principle of reciprocity, both in terms of market access and in terms of infrastructure and investment. we also stressed the importance of the principles of transparency and openness to competition and strongly urged russia to ratify the energy charter treaty. in response, president putin assured that russia would remain a reliable energy supplier, while at the same time not ruling out russia's ratification of the energy charter treaty in the long term. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the basis of article j.3 of the treaty on european union. he pointed out that there is already significant foreign investment in the russian energy sector, which is much larger than in many other energy producing countries. he said that reciprocity would only make sense if the european union was also willing to give russia access to resources that were of similar importance to the eu as energy resources were to russia. it has become clear that the european union and russia also have very different views on how to develop cooperation in the energy sector. on the positive side, however, the eu-russia summit addressed the issue in an open, positive and productive manner. the two sides agreed to continue, expand and intensify the energy dialogue already begun. above all, president putin has made it clear that he wants to bridge differences and work towards an agreement with the eu. the two sides stressed at the summit the mutual dependence on energy and the need for the eu and russia to define their relations in this crucial area by mutual agreement. of course, other issues were also discussed at the summit. one topic was the question of russia's accession to the wto. russia is determined to conclude the negotiations this year so that it can join in 2007. we reaffirmed our support and reiterated our readiness to subsequently work out a 'free trade agreement plus' with russia under a future comprehensive agreement to replace the existing but expiring partnership and cooperation agreement. russia reiterated its desire to move forward swiftly with the work on such a new eu-russia treaty. it was agreed that the treaty should be as comprehensive and as long-term as possible, reflecting the needs and dynamics of the relationship. until such a new treaty enters into force, the existing agreement should continue to apply in order to avoid a vacuum. we were particularly pleased that the summit was able to sign agreements on visa facilitation and readmission on the basis of the agreement reached under the british presidency at the end of last year. these agreements will greatly facilitate both business and people-to-people contacts. the conclusion of a readmission agreement with russia is a great success and of great significance for the european union. this agreement also shows that eu-russia relations are productive and bring real benefits to our citizens. in this context, we have once again called on russia to take steps to ratify the border agreements with estonia and latvia. the summit naturally also raised human rights issues. we discussed the situation in chechnya. we welcomed the agreement reached with the european commission on the modalities of implementing a eur 20 million programme to support socioeconomic recovery in the north caucasus. we also referred to the human rights consultations, the third round of which took place in vienna in march and which have now become an essential part of our relations. finally, i should like to mention that the discussions also focused on international issues, and in particular on the situation in iran. we also discussed the situation in belarus and reaffirmed our willingness to work with russia to resolve the 'frozen conflicts' in moldova and georgia. finally, the summit agreed on the establishment of an eu-russia jointly funded institute for european studies in moscow. overall, it was a good summit with frank and open discussions in a friendly atmosphere, with both sides willing to bridge differences and find solutions to common problems.
member of the commission. mr president, honourable members, i am grateful for this opportunity to report, on behalf of the commission, president barroso and my colleague ms ferrero-waldner, on the eu-russia summit which took place in sochi on 25 may. the summit took place one year after the adoption of the four roadmaps for the common spaces and thus provided a good opportunity to review the progress made since then. the summit was the occasion for the signature of the visa facilitation and readmission agreements. the visa facilitation agreement will help encourage contacts between the peoples of the eu and russia. the readmission agreement will help tackle illegal immigration, an issue of growing concern to both the eu and russia. at the summit, there was a very open, frank and substantive discussion with president putin on energy. it was never our expectation that we would find complete agreement in sochi, but the summit did clarify our respective positions. that was extremely helpful as we prepare for the european council discussions on the external aspects of eu energy policy on the basis of the joint paper by the commission and the high representative. the eu and russia are and certainly will remain interdependent in the energy sector and there is a genuine opportunity to integrate eu and russian energy markets in a mutually beneficial, reciprocal, transparent and non-discriminatory manner. in that regard, we look forward to continuing our dialogue with russia both bilaterally and multilaterally at next month’s g8 summit. as for the future framework for eu-russia relations, the summit agreed that our aim should be to replace the partnership and cooperation agreement with a new, durable, comprehensive and legally-binding agreement which should be capable of promoting the further development of eu-russia relations. the pca would remain in place until a new agreement comes into force. the commission is currently preparing draft negotiating directives to that effect. if the council is in a position to adopt them by the next eu-russia summit, negotiations could begin by the end of this year. for both the eu and russia, russia’s wto accession remains a key priority. this will lay the foundations for bringing eu-russia trade and economic relations to a qualitatively new level in the context of the pca successor agreement, taking into account the existing pca objective of a free trade area. in that context, let me report to parliament that we are making good progress on agreeing a new framework document for the renovated northern dimension policy. the commission is negotiating the text with russia, norway and iceland. i am pleased to say that russia shows every interest in confirming its joint ownership of the northern dimension policy. we hope to have an agreement in time for the senior officials’ meeting in finland in september. we will keep parliament informed and seek to ensure that the parliamentary dimension is included in the new policy. international issues were discussed in depth at the summit. the dialogue which has developed with russia over recent months, in particular on iran and the middle east, is a sound example of the eu and russia working to put the concept of effective multilateralism into practice. the eu also emphasised the need to work together with russia on issues in our common neighbourhood. we remain firmly of the view that the resolution of the frozen conflicts and the development of democracy and market economy in the countries of the region represent the best route to ensuring a stable, peaceful and prosperous neighbourhood for both the eu and russia. a number of other sensitive issues were discussed in a constructive spirit at the summit. the eu stressed the importance of russia ratifying the border agreement with estonia and signing and ratifying the border agreement with latvia. in that context, let me welcome the recent meeting between prime ministers kalvitis and fradkov and hope that this represents the start of an intensified bilateral dialogue. the eu also raised its continuing concerns with regard to the situation in chechnya, notably in the light of the most recent visit by the un high representative for human rights, louise arbour. the regular eu-russia human rights consultations continue to provide a useful forum for addressing these issues. in conclusion, the summit demonstrated the breadth and depth of our common policy agenda with russia. as the eu-russia strategic partnership develops, the need for eu coherence becomes all the more essential. the dialogue the commission has with the european parliament on russia is therefore a very important element in this regard in enhancing coherence, coexistence and the effectiveness of eu policy vis-à-vis the russian federation.
