madam president, there was not sufficient time for both gentlemen to reply to my very precise questions and i would like them to provide this house with a written reply if possible.
i am somewhat surprised, mr president, by the interpretation given by the european commission to the court's ruling. in 2002, i supported the oomen-ruijten report and also the challenge to the framework decision, but then the european commission repeatedly told us that the directive and the challenge to the decision should not be seen as a first step towards harmonising criminal law in other areas. it was only because of that guarantee that i could at the time agree to the challenge to the framework decision. it now appears that the european commission has its own interpretation of the judgment and wants to extend it much further and also wants to see harmonization in other areas. that is contrary to what we were told at the time. if i had known that in 2002, i would have voted against the oomen-ruijten report. the gargani report does contain some positive points, such as paragraphs 8 and 13, but as long as paragraphs 4 and 5 are also part of it, i cannot but vote against the motion for a resolution, together with my group.
the european people's party attaches great importance to common guarantees for the protection of privacy at union level. these will not only contribute to mutual trust between member states in the provision of data, but will also benefit the prosecution of criminal offences and the willingness to exchange data for the purpose of that prosecution. you are quite right to point to the availability of data, because it is important that we can make real progress with this for the safety of our citizens. before we can do this, this step should be taken to protect privacy. i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it has drawn a clear line with us on a number of points. we have not, for example, gone so far as to want to prohibit the exchange of information with third countries. we have collectively drawn a good line by not doing so. secondly, we have not and will not go along with those who want to include the secret services in this. thirdly, the rapporteur has not put pressure on the council to suspend the examination of the reports on fis and sis ii until this report has been completed. mrs roure has done a very good job with that. this is a balanced report, which strikes a good balance between protecting that privacy and creating the possibilities for better data exchange. the latter is our duty to our citizens if we really want the union to do its utmost to ensure security. i thank you again, mrs roure, for your cooperation.
on behalf of the pse group . – mr president, the work of the emcdda is, for me, one of the best examples of the eu having a common goal, a joint interest and an added value, because drugs and drug addiction have a damaging effect on society. they are a source not only of crime but also of social problems, instability and health hazards. the policies of various member states vis-à-vis these problems vary greatly. i believe that it is in the interests of our citizens, especially the young ones, that we europeans should be big enough to want to learn from one another, to want to ask, without any ideological bias, which policies are the most effective. questions that we could have debated in this discussion about the emcdda are, for instance: what about the option to provide heroin under medical supervision to the group that is most heavily addicted? is that something that helps? is cannabis really not dangerous to the health of our citizens? have we ever really investigated this? i am from a member state that has chosen a somewhat peculiar policy on both of those issues. i would be one of the first to welcome a very thorough inquiry. i would also be one of the first to want to do away with those policies if they are not effective. we have not discussed any of that, unfortunately. we have discussed other things, such as how much influence the member states will have on the scientific committee, how we are going to elect the chairperson of the management board, who gets to sit on the management board, and whether the european parliament will have a say on the executive committee – not to be confused with the management board, which is something completely different. i want to thank the rapporteur, because she has managed to engage in this institutional debate without jeopardising the goal of the centre, which is the benchmarking of various member states’ policies – still very much in this report. i praise the emcdda for the work it has done so far, and wish it the best of luck in its work in future.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i can be very brief, because most of what needed to be said has been said. i also wish to thank the rapporteur and express my admiration for all the work she has done on a dossier which has not been easy, for the reasons that have just been outlined by my colleague, mrs mastenbroek. this is an area which is very sensitive and very heavily charged with ideology and national traditions as well. the aim of the rapporteur to come to an agreement with the other institutions has our full support, and it has had the full support of all the groups from the very start. it has not been easy, because the text before us is a compromise. let us accept that. many people in my group would have liked to go much further, like many people in mrs mastenbroek’s group, but we have all had to swallow a little bit in order to achieve this compromise. one of the things we have achieved in this compromise that was very important to us was the independence of the monitoring centre and we all know that there were very strong pressures in order to put the scientific committee effectively under national control. however, we have managed to avoid that, and that is really an achievement worth supporting. i have been hesitating until the very end, but i can tell mrs brepoels that she has our support. however, in order to keep the compromise alive, we need the support of all the groups and, therefore, i am seeking clarification from the socialist group as to whether it will support the compromise, because it is essential to know that before we go to the vote. in any case, you can count on our support.
on behalf of the alde group . – mr president, i am very pleased that we are discussing the excellent report by mrs ždanoka tonight, but i should like to remind you that tomorrow, unfortunately, we will be discussing a resolution on rampant racism and homophobic violence in europe. between the ždanoka report and tomorrow’s resolution, we become painfully aware that there is still a lot to be achieved. therefore i should like to make a proposal to the commissioner. we have been talking a lot about anti-discrimination measures – and there are lots of very concrete proposals in the ždanoka report. when i was preparing my remarks tonight i thought back to march 2000, when the political leaders of europe launched a very ambitious strategy to make europe the strongest sustainable knowledge-based economy in the world. they were very ambitious. perhaps they over-reached a little bit, but ambition is a good thing. why can we not be equally ambitious in the fight against discrimination and in the promotion of fundamental rights and equality in the european union? those are, after all, our shared values. what we need here is not just one measure and then another, but a comprehensive and, most of all, very ambitious strategy. we need a roadmap and we need to put fundamental rights and equality at the heart of european policies. i would like to remind you that about a year and a half ago this house gave its confidence to president barroso and his commission, because he promised us that fundamental rights and the fight against discrimination would be top priority; it would be the cornerstone of the european commission’s policies. i know that the commissioner is personally very committed to that, but i have to say that, on the whole, the track record of the european union in the last couple of years has not been very impressive. i know that the biggest stumbling-block is the member states. the speed with which they adopt measures, security measures, for example, on data retention or pnr data, is remarkable. they manage to take decisions within three or four months. but when it comes to the framework decision against racism and xenophobia, they have still not reached agreement after five years! that is an absolute disgrace. i call on the commission to form an alliance with the european parliament and come forward with a roadmap for fundamental rights and equality, based on the proposals in the ždanoka report. we urgently need concrete measures. we need legislation. it is all very well to have awareness campaigns, but people who are discriminated against need a legal tool so that they can go to court. we need this framework decision. we need a mechanism to monitor the fundamental rights situation in the member state. all of that we would qualify as better lawmaking. that is also in mrs ždanoka’s report. better lawmaking is not just about repealing legislative proposals in the internal market policies; it is also about proposing things that are good for the citizen – something that parliament has been calling for consistently. the charter of fundamental rights needs to become legally binding if it is to have any meaning. with regard to the european year of equal opportunities, it is wonderful that we pay lip service to equal opportunities but, frankly, the budget allocated for the year is pathetic. if that is any indication of the importance that the member states give to equal opportunities, we still have a long way to go. i am counting on the commission’s cooperation with the european parliament.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0173/97). i would like to say a few things about romania and bulgaria, which are being given special attention today by a resolution which parliament is going to adopt later today. last month we debated here with commissioner rehn the accession of romania and bulgaria following the european commission's progress report on the preparations of these two countries. this week, the heads of government will also be discussing this at their meeting. our group believes that the european commission has reached a balanced conclusion. the commission shares our ambition to stick to the accession of both countries on 1 january 2007, provided they tackle the outstanding reforms with diligence. we are convinced, as is the commission, that this must be possible. our group has always been consistent in its support for the accession of romania and bulgaria on the date of 1 january 2007. now these two countries must concentrate on the last things they have to do to be ready for accession in time. i think that the political impulse to do so is present in both countries. in both countries - and that was also the reaction to commissioner rehn's report - the lesson has been well learned. the reactions from sofia and bucharest to his report were very businesslike. the line was: we have noted the problem and are working on it. this approach reinforces my optimism that romania and bulgaria will take their homework seriously. moreover, considerable progress has been made in both countries over the past year, and this is encouraging. speculation about postponing accession is therefore out of the question. the two candidate countries have shown their ability to act with determination in the past and that gives me and my group confidence that the preparations will be completed in time. we therefore have no fundamental objections at this stage to the commission's proposal to wait until the next report in october to finalise our assessment. we would welcome it if the council adopted the commission's approach. in this approach, each person has a role to play. the role of romania and bulgaria is clear. we have previously asked the commission to make an extra effort to support romania and bulgaria in their preparations and to be clear about what they still expect from the applicant countries. we're going to repeat that question now. the council also has the responsibility to make a balanced assessment, but also the responsibility to ensure that the eu countries complete the ratification of the accession treaty in time. as agreed with the european commission last year, parliament will also be involved in monitoring the accession process to the end and i am confident that this will lead to a positive conclusion.
mr president, if there were no european union, there would certainly be a need for a broad cooperation of all european states to contribute to improving the living conditions of people living in border areas, to transfrontier aspects of the environment, energy supply and consumer protection, and to protecting health against dangerous substances which can be traded internationally. the people want a europe which helps to remove obstacles to peace, security, public services and international solidarity. that is different from building a world power or increasing freedom for international corporations. the people want a europe that helps them solve their problems. not a europe that causes trouble. the people of europe no longer recognize such a europe in the present phase of the union. the voters in two countries have sent the signal that things must be different. if we want to continue with the european union, it will have to be fed from below, by our citizens and their organisations. continuing with top-down constructions, which are designed from the top down, solves nothing. we will have to use the enlargement and the increased involvement of our citizens creatively to enable a new and better start.
the european council must resolutely put an end to the confusion about the status of the european constitution. while one member state declares the document dead, another ratifies the constitution as if nothing had happened. i call on the heads of government to put an end to this confusion, otherwise the confidence of the citizens in the union will continue to decline. i support the extension of the period of reflection for one year, on condition that we start working on a completely new text of the treaty. it's a treaty text, not a constitution. a handy treaty that brings together the previous treaties, one in which the union demonstrates its added value in a defined area in relation to crossborder policy challenges. i call on the austrian presidency in particular to show leadership by finally putting an end to the confusion and giving a strong impetus to the emergence of a new treaty framework. this program will feature the sound of austria, which i've been waiting for for six months.
the treaty establishing the european union was full of ambitions which the union has not yet fulfilled. the eu is failing in its strategy to make europe the most competitive knowledge-based economy in the world, in its cooperation in the fight against avian influenza and in the implementation of a common foreign policy. why the ambition of a genuine constitution, if we cannot make good agreements in all those other areas, or violate the agreements that do exist, such as the stability pact, the europeans wonder, understandably. we have to be more modest. let us first carry out the institutional reforms that are really necessary. these changes, described in chapter 1 of the 2004 constitutional convention, then have the character of a normal convention and do not necessarily require a referendum in each member state. the charter of fundamental rights of the european union should eventually become a treaty text. it is precisely this that gives the constitutional weight to the current draft treaty, for which the union is not ready. i would have liked to see the direct election of the president of the european commission included. but at least we are solving the most urgent problems and strengthening both the european parliament and the national parliaments.
the strategy published by the commission is a positive sign. it shows that the commission is also concerned about a sustainable society. i can agree with the conclusion drawn by the commission in its document, namely that if we are to move towards a sustainable society, we must seize the opportunities that exist now. that should be the message to the council. if we are serious about sustainable development, there are many examples of actions we can take immediately. i would remind you that we do not own this earth, but we are commissioned to work it and to preserve it. so we really will have to change our way of life to ensure that our children and grandchildren can also build a life. that is why we need an ambitious package of measures and i would like to hear from the council and the commission how they intend to work with this parliament on this.

in the past, only differences in track gauge were a barrier to passing and cross-border trains. since electrification, the differences in grid voltage and safety systems have also contributed to the shortening of the railway network. a common electronic security system is badly needed. it is expected that within 20 to 30 years a number of variants of ertms will become the generally applicable standard for this purpose. the first investments in ertms have been made and many more will follow. the certainty of constant growth is enormously beneficial to industry. if everyone wants to apply this system already in the development phase, there will be shortages and high prices can be calculated. moreover, there is a good chance that in the not too distant future, at least the first version of the software will have to be replaced by better ones. the people working on the railways need a lot of preparatory training to be able to use ertms safely. for the time being, it is mainly useful as a second system alongside one of the twenty old proven systems. this has already contributed to delays in the commissioning of new railway lines in the netherlands. where ertms is used without this support by another system, this will initially lead to major delays and thus to irregular implementation of the timetable. in switzerland, the average train delay was 18 minutes during the first four months and only then did it gradually decrease. such start-up problems mean that an interested public will not see it as a success for the time being. therefore, to make it a success, we must make a sharp distinction between the experimental start-up phase, in which it can be selectively tested to gain practical experience, and a full-scale phase, in which we can rely on it completely. the european union funding should be directed to that experimental phase for the purpose of making the 2.3.0 and 3.0.0 variants reliable and for any further developments without unnecessarily burdening the industry. before the full introduction can take place, we must be vigilant against the neglect of old and proven safety systems and prevent further reductions in the railway network from becoming a means of rapidly achieving a high rate of ertms in statistics. the necessary financing is probably only possible through an additional levy on road freight transport as in switzerland. that is why the earlier decision on the eurovignette has been amended.
for the ind/dem group, by mr de gucht and others there are a number of factors which are hindering the competitiveness of the railways. the lack of a uniform safety and signalling system is one of them. given the role that railways are supposed to play in the logistics process, i am pleased with the attention that is being given to ertms. i agree with the substance of mr cramer's report, which covers virtually all the relevant aspects of ertms. i agree with him that ertms is a tool that can help the railways in the future. the present service provision has barriers which impede competition targeted security systems is undoubtedly part of this. this fragmentation is hampering the development and implementation of cross-border transport and is thus adversely affecting competition in rail freight transport. the introduction of ertms can counteract this negative aspect. it is therefore important to keep the transition period between the current systems and ertms as short as possible in order to minimise additional costs and functional inconvenience resulting from the use of dual systems. it is also important to have an adequately functioning ertms version. the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the system is economically sound and technically up to date. a major topic of discussion during our discussions was the financial aspect of the introduction of ertms. there i am not saying that we should not be concerned about the future of the european union. but what now? in 2004, there were some ten thousand irregularities in the spending of european funds, but these are of course only the known cases of fraud and incompetence. we can only guess how often european money is being misappropriated without our knowledge. i am afraid that this is only the tip of the iceberg on which the ship of the european union will eventually break. the european citizen is becoming increasingly critical of the eu and waste of money is one of the main reasons for this justified criticism. 2% of the structural funds are affected by fraud or some form of mismanagement. i think that, given the enormous sums involved, is a very worrying figure. we are talking about hundreds of millions of euros wasted, of which at least 20% is lost to direct fraud, according to the commission. self-enrichment at european, national and regional level. i think it would be desirable to have a 'competent authority' to cover the whole of the european union. this is in direct contradiction to the efficiency and transparency which the commission claims to pursue in its communication. it would european money, this should have an impact on the amount of subsidies they receive. this is also true of my own country, the netherlands, which refuses to name specific perpetrators in fraud cases. cases of fraud in this area. in addition to all the other moral objections i have to export subsidies, this seems to me to be an excellent reason for putting an end to this ridiculous and harmful form author. among journalists and scientists gathering knowledge about north korea, this country came to be regarded as the most curious piece of the world. in negotiations with other states, it behaves erratically and unpredictably. inland, the people seem to live in unimaginable unity and discipline with unbounded loyalty to the mysterious leader and unparalleled grief when that leader dies. despite its almost complete isolation from the outside world, the country has built up a metropolitan living environment and developed its own nuclear industry. some people outside that country are praising the remarkable achievements publicized by the regime. according to this view, these successes have been achieved thanks to a superior system and despite the opposition of the hostile outside world since the end of the war situation in 1953. yet, these successes convince almost no one. apart from a small number of admirers, virtually no european would want to live there. after the success stories, came the food shortages. nor is it any longer completely silent about how north korea ultimately deals with people who don't seem to fit that model. despite constitutional freedom of religion and despite ratification of the international covenant on civil and political rights, arbitrariness is being applied to anyone who may be a nuisance to the regime. the death penalty is also used as a political instrument. repression and hunger have led tens of thousands to flee to china. the resolution does not choose to repeat decades of cold war rhetoric, but makes sensible proposals to the government on human rights, the death penalty, the release of prisoners, freedom of expression, the adaptation of legislation and the authorization of international inspections. it also calls on neighbouring countries not to return refugees and supports food aid. my group thinks that is the right approach.
- a writer. as a historian, i have some knowledge from my studies of the horrors of totalitarian regimes in the 20th century. but what must it have been for the citizens of these dictatorships? mr posselt has already said: it is difficult for us in the free west to imagine. this afternoon we will reflect briefly on the communist reign of terror in north korea. the combination, mr president, of north korea with the word human rights, so often used by this parliament, is essentially shocking and should silence us. but that would leave the battered north korean people to fend for themselves. of course, dear leader kim jong il has every interest in our political passivity. besides, we don't have to expose him and his cronies. terrorist regimes, creepily enough, always do it themselves in the first place. even though the coverage of and from the most hermetically sealed pyongyang is very scarce. after all, the life stories of north korean refugees speak volumes. here's the urgent question: what is the european union to do in this oppressive situation in north korea? i believe that the council and the commission should, above all, encourage china to use its growing influence in neighbouring north korea to its advantage. the european union is not a party to the conflict, but a party to the peace process. after all, beijing is equally benefited. this may sound very pragmatic, but this message is merely intended to alleviate the material and spiritual suffering of north korean citizens. humans, co-creators of god, of the same movement as ourselves, cling to freedom of movement and not to forget, thought, speech and above all belief.
mr president, i would like to draw your attention to the following: on 10 july 2003, 21-year-old sibel yalvac, a physically handicapped woman from rotterdam, was arrested at the airport in the indonesian capital jakarta. on 6 april 2004, she was sentenced to 10 years in prison for drug trafficking. two independent dutch experts, a psychologist and a probation officer, examined sibel and they declare that she is less culpable. she can't tell cause and effect from each other, is gullible, has a lot of imagination and can't count to twelve. sibel has been abused by ruthless criminals for their drug trade and now faces years of hell. now every year in indonesia around the independence day of august 17th there is a chance to get amnesty. i have started a campaign with my national colleague, member of parliament harry van bommel, to ask the indonesian government to put its hand over its heart and grant sibel amnesty in view of her particular circumstances. the "help sibel" campaign, on behalf of sibel and her desperate family, asks for your support in this appeal.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, firstly i would like to thank ms wallis for her report. i agree that it is too early to draw conclusions; however, i would like to make a few observations. the first is about solvency. whether or not the company should be considered to have been solvent during the time leading up to the crisis has several consequences. that affects the policyholders’ possibilities of defending their rights and interests, but it also has an impact upon the responsibility of the uk authorities and, possibly, the commission. the uk authorities strongly emphasised that equitable life had never had any problems with solvency and always complied with eu law. the uk authorities also indicated that there are a number of different ways of valuing assurance companies. if there are several ways of calculating solvency, how is it possible to make a proper analysis? it is clear that the directive seeks to ensure that an assurance company is not managed in such a manner that it may jeopardise the undertaking’s solvency in the long term. it is also clear that the directive obliges member states to take such measures with regard to the undertaking, its directors or managers, or the persons who control as are appropriate and necessary in order to prevent or remedy any irregularities prejudicial to the interests of the assured persons. i doubt whether this has happened. the fact that the uk authorities always considered equitable life solvent also had repercussions on customers and authorities in other member states. it appears that both customers and authorities in ireland and germany were taken by surprise when the crisis broke out. another observation i have, which concerns the country of origin principle, is the question of monitoring and provision of information. the uk authorities, who were responsible for monitoring the activities of the company in other member states too, never informed those member states that there was a problem. maybe it was because they did not want to recognise this. frankly, in hindsight, it does not look as if the supervision has worked. the second problem with the home country of origin rule is the lack of redress for consumers, who have been sent back and forth by the national authorities. consumers who trusted that the european union would guarantee them an equivalent position and safeguards if they bought assurance on the internal market as they would if they had bought on the national market found themselves in a situation where nobody would accept responsibility for their efforts to obtain damages for their losses. from a consumer point of view, the home country of origin principle turned out to be a real ping-pong game. there seems to be a large vacuum in eu legislation here. the eu should take more measures to protect individual citizens.
the european parliament has already expressed its concern about the situation in the middle east. but commissioner mccreevy has, of the questions we have asked about developments in the stock exchanges, actually only answered the first part of question 2, namely whether we should infer from your silence on these developments that you really think that this is only a matter for the shareholders. apparently you do. and i want to say here explicitly that that is not my view. i think you absolutely cannot say that it is a purely shareholder affair. there are many other stakeholders with vested interests: the users of the exchanges, the companies listed on them, private and institutional investors such as pension funds investing through the exchanges. but financial institutions that offer alternative ways of investing also have their interest: the investment banks that trade in-house and provide alternative platforms, and the hedge funds. i do not know whether you are familiar with the book by mr seifert, the former ceo of the deutsche börse. he describes very well how hedge funds, acting on behalf of other interests, also asserted their shareholder rights in the struggle over the proposals made by deutsche börse at the time to take over the london stock exchange. i do not know whether you read german or whether there is a translation, but it would be good to take into account that someone who has been involved in the transformation of a stock exchange owned by the users into a listed company now sees the disadvantages of this purely shareholder approach. i think you will have to look more closely at what the public interest is that is at stake. it is our duty as politicians to look at that public interest and that is clearly a duty which lies at european level and not just at national level. i am therefore not in the least interested in hearing your views on the best combination and whether it should be a european or a transatlantic fair. but i do think that it is your task to indicate what the conditions are, what is required in the interests of those other stakeholders, of the users, of the european economy, that is the commission's task. on the transatlantic dimension, you have already said that the rules of the united states should not become our rules. on that point too, there are several experts - harvey pitt, callum mccarthy - who indicate that it is not so clear at all. so what we expect from the european commission is that we get clear answers to this, what the consequences are and how to prevent that american influence from spreading to possible constellations in europe. the commission has already made a number of proposals. i would like to emphasize our proposal to set up a committee of experts. i think it is really very necessary and very urgent that we establish a stronger european surveillance. the stock exchanges are an example of a sector where this is very much needed. the consolidation of financial institutions elsewhere also shows that we cannot leave this to the national level alone because other interests will be involved. it is clear that in this globalising economy we urgently need a european vision and a european oversight and it would be very good if a committee of wise men could advise you on this.
the economic importance of the maritime sector is great and it also has enormous growth potential. in particular, in this era of increasingly rapid globalisation, these global transport flows and their effective management are of great importance. european industry has a strong position in this area. it is a sector which is developing to a high degree into a competitive sector and it is actually the only sector at present which is exempted from the application of the treaty's competition rules. the time is therefore ripe to put an end to this exceptional situation. we therefore also support the proposal by commissioner mccreevy to repeal the present regulation 4056/86. however, legal uncertainty about the interpretation of competition rules is undesirable and i am glad that the commissioner also recognises this, because the commission must continue to take account of the specific characteristics of this internationally operating sector in the future. that is why we must clarify the rules of the game in the future. the european parliament therefore calls for clear guidelines to be drawn up for the sector before the regulation is repealed. i am glad that the commissioner has indicated his intention to do so before the end of 2007 for liner shipping. but for parliament it is an essential pillar of this proposal and we therefore think that it should also be explicitly mentioned in the legislative text. the sector must be guaranteed legal certainty as to what is and is not allowed under the current regulation. i am glad to hear that you are in intensive consultation with the sector. but parliament also asks you, and will do so again tomorrow, to consult us on the content of the guidelines. can you promise that parliament will be consulted on this? that is often a good practice in other areas as well. i am glad that tomorrow, as it seems, we will be able to send this strong signal to the commission by a large majority. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the health of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work is a welcome step towards the creation of a european health insurance system. that is a must for this sector, but of course it must always fit within the text of the treaty, within article 81. it is very important to make this cooperation work efficiently. president. - as regards cabotage and free-flowing traffic, the commission proposes that the enforcement of competition rules be brought to the european level. that is a logical step because the treaty is already in force. that is also a matter for the european level, and you can count on our support in that respect. some colleagues are proposing to exclude cabotage, but i will not support that. parliament wants guidelines to be drawn up for the tramp sector too, because this sector too has the right to know what is happening to it, just as the other sectors do. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of a fire. clearness in advance avoids all kinds of costly legal procedures that the industry really does not want to wait for. there are already examples of such cases in the tramway sector in relation to pool agreements. there have been complaints and it is unclear to the industry how the european commission is i am a member of both the committee on economic affairs and the committee on civil liberties and i must say that i am beginning to be very concerned, with both hats on. i wonder, from the point of view of civil liberties, where we are going with a kind of permanent and general surveillance of all citizens. that just throws the whole presumption of innocence out the window! as a member of the committee on economic and monetary affairs, i am concerned about the burden on business. i think it is remarkable that the commissioner for the internal market is giving an introduction here which is only about the fight against terrorism, because we should actually be talking about business here too. because companies, commercial companies that are just doing their commercial activities, are increasingly being tasked with collecting data for law enforcement and for judicial purposes. i would also like to refer to the data retention directive and the passenger name records as examples. the effectiveness of all these measures is questionable. we seem to have a kind of blind faith in technology, and if we just collect as much data as possible from civilians, we'll fish out a terrorist sooner or later. a recent report in the united states, however, strongly suggests that terrorists do not use regular money transfers to raise their criminal funds, but have all kinds of other channels for doing so. if we adopt these kinds of measures, i wonder, frankly, whether we are still driving those money flows underground. i hope that we have at least made the proposal a little more workable for the companies that have to work with it. i am glad, for example, that the clauses on the thresholds of eur 1000 and usd 1000 are in it. i wonder if the 150 euro threshold for donations to charities is a bit low. and i would also like to emphasize in this context the importance of what we call purpose limitation, which is that the data is really only used for the purpose for which it was collected. finally, i join those who have already expressed their concern in strong terms about the recent swift scandal and i think it is really time for a much more fundamental debate about how we want to combat terrorism effectively here in europe without undermining fundamental rights.
i would also like to congratulate mr szejna on his report. in the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy we had no difficulty in adding a lot of things to that. we have confined ourselves to a few points, particularly those which were also indicated by mr lehne, and i should like to thank him for his willingness to compromise. but i think, like mrs berès, that what we add to the amendments for the plenary does not contradict that compromise. i'll get back to you on that in a moment. mr szejna has taken a broad approach in his report, and that appeals to me. unlike mrs cederschiöld, i think it is important - and it was also discussed earlier in the debate this evening - that companies are not simply owned by shareholders. shareholders have shares, but companies are more than just a piece of paper, an investment object. companies operate in the real economy and there are interests beyond those of shareholders. i would therefore like to stress in particular recital f and general point 3, the role of employee participation in the decision-making process of companies and the view expressed in this report that this should also be an integral part of corporate governance and company law. i hope that mr mccreevy will agree with parliament on that point and will ensure that it does indeed play an important part in the future of the files. it is very important that this cannot be circumvented by means of, for example, the european company and the change of establishment, and we have therefore tabled a number of amendments on this point. i hope that mr lehne will look at it again - even if he is not listening at the moment - to see if he can support it.
the committee on transport and tourism has adopted a number of opinions. president, first of all, i would like to thank commissioner dimas for his realistic contribution just now. i very much support i would like to thank mrs lucas for her very clear report. i am in favour of the commission's proposal to put a stop to the rapidly increasing contribution of aviation to climate change. the report by mrs caroline lucas is a good complement to the proposal and we agree with her that the inclusion of the aviation sector in emissions trading should not be the only instrument but should be part of a package of measures such as the introduction of kerosene tax, vat on airline tickets and congestion tax for airports. i agree with the rapporteur that the emission allowances to be issued should be auctioned to avoid the large companies getting rich off free allowances without any benefit to the environment. although my group considers the commission proposal to be a step in the right direction, we should not blindly focus on emissions trading, the environmental benefits of which are not clear. and co2 is not the only problem in aviation. the emission of nitrogen oxides (nox) is also of great importance, not only for the climate, but also for air quality. that is why we are in favour of including nox in emissions trading, as is already being done in the netherlands. i am not sure that the commissioner has been able to give us a clear answer to this question. a system that runs on co2 alone doesn't achieve maximum effect. it is better to tackle the nox at the same time. we agree with the rapporteur that research into cleaner engines is desirable, but this should not lead to the postponement of targeted measures against these emissions. if nox is not included in emissions trading as it now appears, airports should be encouraged to implement local emission levies as part of the package of measures mentioned by the rapporteur. my party supports the commission's intention to end the tax advantages enjoyed by the international aviation industry. this has for years been leading to unfair competition between the different modes of transport. we therefore support the rapporteur's proposal that the eu itself should start by introducing a kerosene tax on all domestic flights. this could be a good brake on the tremendous growth of a number of low-cost flights with which the railways can hardly compete. the low-cost airlines already account for a quarter of european air traffic, which has led to the growth of regional airports. it is important that member states provide insight into subsidies that go to these airports to bring in airlines. we must get rid of the idea that the increase in air traffic within the eu is an engine of regional growth. for many of these medium-distance routes, rail links are a much more environmentally friendly alternative, but they must be fairly competitive. the railways have been paying a much higher price for electricity since the introduction of emissions trading, but the airlines still do not pay a penny of excise duty despite their constant complaints about high fuel prices.
president. - the consequences of our climate change are becoming more acute every year. the winters are coming later and milder, and the summers are coming earlier and warmer. is this a process that is inevitable? i hope not. but we must do everything we can to ensure that change is slowed down. that is why i agree with the european commission's communication. if we are to take the fight against climate change seriously, all sectors must contribute. the aviation sector in particular, which emits at high altitudes and therefore has an extraordinary impact. i can support the plan to introduce an emissions trading system for aviation too. however, this must satisfy a number of conditions. firstly, it must cover as many airlines and routes as possible to prevent unfair competition, i.e. all flights to and from all airports in the union. secondly, account should be taken of the efforts already made by airlines to reduce their emissions. i would therefore prefer to allocate the rights by auction. thirdly, it should be possible to include substances other than c02 if scientific evidence is available. i would also like to point out that the vat exemption for kerosene and air tickets should be mentioned, but that should be done in a comprehensive plan which also takes account of the vat exemptions for other forms of transport. the commission has promised, in the context of the eurovignette debate, to carry out a study on the internalisation of external costs for all modes of transport. i would like to call for its rapid completion so that the results are available as soon as possible and we can adopt legislation which takes account of the outcome.
president. - i would like to begin by congratulating caroline lucas. it has produced an excellent report which well reflects the problems of aviation and the dilemmas involved. what are the problems of aviation? firstly, aviation is a major contributor to climate change: the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the efficiency of air transport. the environmental burden is certainly high for short distances, and it is precisely at short distances that alternatives exist and are now underused. the second problem is that few people are aware of the large environmental burden of aviation. that is why it would be good if the co2 emissions per kilometre were shown on the tickets and on the cars. commissioner, i would like to see some initiatives on this. it would be useful for us as parliamentarians to know exactly what the co2 emissions are when we travel by car, train or plane to brussels or strasbourg. the third problem is that the environmental burden is not reflected in the cost in any way. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's air transport sector is not subject to vat. this is to the detriment of the more environmentally friendly mode of transport such as trains or buses. this can and must end soon. however, it is of the utmost importance that aviation be brought under a system of tradable allowances as soon as possible. that puts a price tag on co2 and that encourages innovation, especially more efficient engines and more fuel-efficient and better fuel economy. i think it is obvious that we should try to join the existing system of emissions trading, but we must avoid the mistakes of this system. not free allocation, but auction. no open end, but a clear ceiling. and of course we have to tax kerosene. the fourth problem is that it is not easy to tackle the aviation sector. aviation is a source of employment and a source of pleasure for the consumer and the tourist. it is therefore important to act in a european manner and at the same time to invest in comfortable hsl connections between european cities. this will also ensure public support for an ambitious policy.
- i'm the reporter. mr president, the time has finally come. on 3 may 2006, more than two and a half years after the commission presented a proposal for the revision of the battery directive, the council and parliament reached a mediated agreement on the final text. first of all, i would like to thank the austrian presidency for the constructive way in which this dossier has been brought to a successful conclusion. the presidency, and consequently the council, has given parliament a very good welcome in the 23 amendments adopted at second reading. i also thank all my colleagues, especially those who were in the delegation and the conciliation secretariat, for their commitment and support. this is part of the reason why we have achieved a good result. i am also grateful to the european commission for thinking of compromises and proposing new texts. this has been a very good help in the process of reaching an agreement. what have we accomplished? i would like to mention a few important points. first of all: the directive will make it easier for consumers to choose the most cost-effective battery. the cheapest battery is not always the most profitable. the directive requires manufacturers to indicate the capacity of batteries, which allows consumers to assess the price/quality ratio and choose the most advantageous battery for each application. secondly: the commission has also agreed that batteries should be easy to remove from appliances. replacing the battery prevents consumers from having to throw away a good device because the battery is dead. thirdly: the manufacturer must take back the batteries after use and ensure that the battery is recycled. this also applies to the historical stock. fourthly: the directive requires all member states to establish an efficient system of inward processing. this is a significant improvement as currently only six member states have a national battery collection system. the differences in collection rates between these member states are still very high, ranging from 59% in belgium to 16% in france. fifth: all producers of batteries in the european union are registered, so that all producers are responsible for financing the collection and treatment of waste batteries. finally, i would like to mention the most important part of the directive on which we had already reached agreement at first reading, namely the ban on cadmium in portable batteries, which will come into force in 2008. this will eventually remove this harmful metal from household waste. this is of course good for the environment and good for public health. in addition, the alternatives to nickel-cadmium batteries are found to be much better. mr president, in view of the good result of the negotiations, i can warmly recommend to my colleagues in parliament that they approve the new battery directive tomorrow. i would ask the council to do the same as soon as possible so that the new legislation can be published. the commission has also proposed that the commission should be given the task of drawing up a report on the implementation of the directive. i hope and wish that this will be started quickly, so that we do not have to make all sorts of compromises at the last minute. i think that in the future there will be a need for a review of the applications which are exempted from the cadmium ban. i assume that the european commission is also aware of this and will come to parliament with a proposal in due course.
president. - the unemployment rate in the eurozone is lower than ever, but still at 8.8%. that is why the structural funds and also the social fund must be used to improve the employability of our people in europe. i am grateful for the amendments, and i also thank the rapporteur, mr josé silva peneda, who has been very active and very energetic in the debate. the social fund is now focusing on globalisation and improving people's employability. the fund can help to promote access to employment, prevent unemployment, prolong working life and increase the participation of women and older people in particular.

i am very much in favour of the rapid integration of the aviation sector into the eu ets, but i cannot support the final version of this resolution, particularly because of the paragraphs which refer to equal treatment of the different modes of transport and the introduction of the kerosene tax. i would therefore ask that my name be withdrawn as rapporteur for an opinion on this resolution.
the commission's vice-president mr president, madam rapporteur, whose heroism i commend to you for being here at this hour, ladies and gentlemen, i am pleased that there is a broad consensus today on the commission's proposal, thanks to the political will of all parties and the cooperation of technical experts. i want to thank you, mrs sommer, for your personal and important contribution to the establishment of this consensus. community legislation harmonizing the technical requirements for inland waterway vessels dates back to 1982. this legislation establishes a certificate with which an inland waterway vessel may operate on all community waterways except the rhine. two thirds of europe's water transport is carried on the rhine! our proposal, which dates from 1997, therefore has three objectives: firstly, to update technical requirements to adapt the safety of ships to technological developments; secondly, to allow the recognition of the community certificate for rhine navigation in the future; thirdly, to establish a comitology procedure to be adapted in the future, on a regular basis, in line with technological developments and the development of the rules in force on the rhine. this matter has been blocked for a long time in the council because of the question of access to the rhine waterways for vessels with community certificates. but once this point was resolved, the council was able to reach a political agreement. the adoption of this directive will be a real step forward in the harmonization of the rules, as we want in the naiade action plan. it will make it possible to establish a single system for all the waterways of the european union. the technical requirements will be harmonised to a high level of safety, particularly for passenger boats. the future validity of the community certificate on the rhine will promote competition and create a genuine level playing field for inland waterway transport within the internal market. and we will maintain this level in future, in cooperation with the central commission for navigation on the rhine. as regards the amendment adopted by the committee on transport, we support it and i am glad that a compromise has been reached between the three institutions. i would like to take this opportunity to assure you that the commission is aware of the concerns expressed by some owners of large pleasure craft in some member states. the text you adopt will take account of most of these concerns. unlike directive 94/25/ec, which concerns market access for pleasure craft in the community, the new directive on technical requirements for inland waterway vessels is concerned with the safety of navigation on all waterways. a vessel of 20 metres in length requires additional requirements to those required for placing on the market. moreover, the exclusion of pleasure boats, with a length of less than 24 metres and not 20 metres from the scope of the directive, would make the rules applicable to the rhine different from those applicable to other community waterways. this could create difficulties for the recognition of the community certificate for navigation on the rhine and could create an obstacle to the prospect of having a single certificate for vessels in the community, namely the community certificate. finally, the directive allows a number of derogations for existing pleasure boats. i am replying to mr graham watson, who drew my attention to the fact that existing pleasure boats can continue to operate as long as their failure to meet the requirements does not represent a clear danger. i would add that article 7 allows member states whose inland waterways are not connected to the waterways of other member states to derogate from all or part of this directive. ladies and gentlemen, i can assure you that the commission will, in any event, take care in the comitology procedure to pay particular attention to the chapter on pleasure boats, either to avoid any contradiction or incompatibility with directive 94/25/ec or to clarify and clarify any perceived inaccuracies.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the internal market is not distorted. we in parliament should applaud this substantial contribution to sustainable, safe and competitive transport across europe. however, such transport is not a matter of course. it is important to create the right conditions for this. the report by mr sommer is one of the building blocks of this, because harmonized technical rules for inland navigation mean a level playing field for everyone, with the result that the fleet is clean, reliable and safe. this report is all the more welcome because it marks the completion of a process which has lasted over 10 years. i am particularly pleased that the institutional relations in the inland waterway sector have not been an obstacle to this agreement. i believe that this apparently fruitful cooperation will continue in other areas as well. we shall discuss that at a later stage. as far as the content is concerned, i am satisfied with the level at which the requirements for vessels have been set. although the sector would have to make the necessary efforts to meet these requirements, it does mean that the quality of the fleet is beyond doubt. given the potential of water transport, this is a very welcome and essential element. i am also satisfied with the procedure for further adaptation and improvement of the directive. surely it is not wrong to leave such a technical subject to specialists. i understand that there are a number of amendments on the table at the moment. i hope that the specialists will also find a solution to the possible discrepancy between this directive and directive 94/25/ec. i wholeheartedly support the amendment tabled to that effect. finally, i would like to thank mrs sommer for her efforts. i hope that this step will make a positive contribution to the quality of inland navigation.
i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr seppänen, for his excellent report. it rightly emphasizes that member states have a responsibility in the first place when it comes to the storage or treatment of nuclear waste. that certainly applies to the right to refuse outside waste. this fundamental right must not be undermined by european legislation, but in my view the responsibility of individual countries goes further. the producer of nuclear waste has a continuing responsibility to ensure that it is properly processed and stored, even if this happens in another country. the way in which eu countries now ship their rubbish to, say, russia, where it is then stored under miserable conditions, is absolutely outrageous. the local population and the environment are the victims of european nuclear laxity. if countries choose to use a dangerous and environmentally unfriendly energy source as nuclear energy, they should clean up their own mess instead of saddling the population of poor countries outside the eu with it. but if we really want to tackle the problem of nuclear waste in europe, we must first of all address the source, otherwise we will be left with the faucet on. it is particularly disturbing to see nuclear energy, after having been rightly marginalized for some time in the energy debate, slipping back onto the european agenda. but nuclear energy is not an environmentally friendly, safe, cheap and therefore acceptable alternative. the waste problem is a good example of this.
the debate on the future of europe calls for an ambitious and effective presidency in the second half of 2006. however, your presentation this morning gives me the worst suspicion. i'll start with ambition, or at least the lack of it. i blame you for clinging to the rejected european constitution. in doing so, you, as the finnish presidency, are blocking a new ambitious debate on the future of the european union. but that's not all. the finnish presidency, which is committed to transparency, maintains europe's painful spaceship. while you know that the text of the twice rejected constitution must at least be amended, you intend to ratify this european constitution during your presidency. how can this be explained to the citizen, to the dutch citizen? i'm also concerned about your lack of agency. are you really going to be on the shop floor alone waiting for chancellor merkel? how effective is a presidency which, through its foreign minister, has already indicated in an interview with the süddeutsche zeitung of 1 july that it does not expect much from the round of consultations with the member states. i do not often have the pleasure of agreeing with the analysis of the chairman of our committee on constitutional affairs, mr leinen. i do not think that the council is going to be able to do that. the finnish presidency must make a clear choice between the rejected constitution and a new treaty framework. i prefer the more ambitious second option. i therefore call on the finnish presidency to show the necessary ambition and real action.

rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on transport and tourism . mr president, i think we are discussing an important subject today. a significant proportion of the co2 we all blow into the air comes from the exhaust pipes of our cars. road transport is responsible for around 25% of european co2 emissions, half of which comes from passenger cars. the reduction of co2 emissions from our fleet can therefore make a significant contribution to the kyoto objectives to which the european union has committed itself. last year, in its report on climate change, the european parliament highlighted the need to make major improvements in energy efficiency in the transport sector. in addition, technological innovation is crucial to achieving our climate goals. fiscal measures can be particularly effective in this respect. by differentiating taxes according to their environmental impact, we can discourage harmful practices, promote economy and stimulate technological innovation. we therefore welcome the commission's proposal to differentiate the annual road tax according to co2 emissions. part of the annual road tax is then calculated on the basis of the amount of co2 emitted per kilometre by the vehicle. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not undermined by the fact that the community is not able to meet its own needs. it is important, however, that we apply a progressive basis of calculation. this means that heavier engines, which use more fuel and contribute more to climate change, are relatively more heavily taxed. in this way there are no high barriers to driving in modest models, whereas there is a price to be paid for touring in the most expensive and largest cars, which cause the most damage to the environment. we do not support the proposal of the greens to bring the directive into force sooner. we believe that member states should be given time to prepare properly. a second and, i think, most controversial part of the proposal is to abolish the registration tax on new cars in parallel with the differentiation of the car tax. while this will solve some internal market problems, the commission believes it will also speed up the renewal of the fleet, so that old and environmentally unfriendly cars will be replaced more quickly by new and clean cars. i am not sure that the commissioner has been able to give us a clear answer to this question, but i am sure that he will be able to give us a clear answer to this question. i think that, and i look at the experience in my own country, that registration tax can also be used to promote the use of environmentally friendly cars. in the netherlands, for example, these cars are no longer taxed, and this has given a huge boost to the sale of such cars. my group will therefore be voting on this in a variety of ways, but it is important for the commissioner to know that there may be different views on this. finally, the commissioner said something about the council and mixed reactions. i wish him a lot of strength, because in part this is an important debate, but we also know that it is a bit of a debate that is enjoyable in parliament, but in the council a proposal like this will probably quickly be vetoed by one of the member states, which absolutely do not want to talk about taxation at european level, but i hope that the commissioner will manage to achieve a breakthrough in principle on that point. a lot of strength.
the number of cars has increased alarmingly in recent decades, while the use of trams, trains, metros and buses has lagged behind the growth in mobility. the result is a huge amount of space for parking lots and motorways, with noise pollution, air pollution and unsafe traffic. the car is the most widely used means of transport and the road infrastructure has become very extensive, complicated and expensive, so we cannot avoid the question of who pays for it. the tax on passenger cars is now being increased in several member states to take account of the pollution they cause. unfortunately, the proposal for a directive has become a half-hearted one, because it seems to be pursuing other aims. without contributing to reducing pollution, the aim is to harmonise taxes in the member states and to open up new growth opportunities for the car market by reducing prices from 2016. the draft directive, which aims to gradually and partially offset car emissions in the motor vehicle tax, would have to have a quarter of the tax based on co2 emissions by the end of 2008 and half by 2010, but why are we stopping at half? why has the commission not proposed that nox and particulate matter emissions and fuel efficiency be taken as the basis for the motor vehicle tax, rather than waiting for amendments? why has the commission not supported greenpeace germany's initiative to develop a car that uses half as much petrol in ten years? instead, the commission wants to phase out the registration tax on cars across the european union, making cars cheaper in 16 member states. so far, taxation and its variations have been a matter of national autonomy and national considerations. if the union goes beyond the fight against tax havens and other forms of large-scale tax evasion, it is overstepping its powers. the aim should be to reduce harmful emissions rather than to provide the car industry with customers who want to buy the latest model car every year even after the market is saturated. my group rejects a ban on registration taxes. meanwhile, car emissions have increased by 30% over the past decade. despite the kyoto protocol and despite technical improvements. the increase in the number of cars and the average mileage of those vehicles, in turn, has largely offset the technological gains. that is why my group does not think that half-time is an option any longer. in our view, the tax on cars is not based on half of their co2 emissions, but entirely on the pollution they cause. the easiest way to do this is to convert the motor vehicle tax into a fuel tax, depending on emissions. this is the way the polluter pays, not the car owner. new cars will have to meet strict fuel efficiency requirements and soot filters will soon be mandatory for diesel cars. in addition, the number of car kilometres driven will have to be reduced, and this can only be achieved by strong, healthy and affordable public transport. the european union has been supporting the sector's austerity and privatization efforts, but so far the opposite has been the case.
mr president, the tax on car purchases is being evaded on a massive scale in the netherlands. a dutch newspaper reported this summer. escaping is particularly lucrative because the burden is high and the chance of capture is small. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat this evasion, not so much by abolishing the levy as by taking action on the part of the competent authorities. in the debate of 5 november 2003, i called for a transparent system of charges with clear objectives. linking the level of charges to environmental impacts is a transparent and clear objective. further research and discussion on the environmental effects of car taxes therefore deserve all support. the member states can then, on the basis of the results of the investigations, adjust their charges to take account of environmental damage. but to impose this measure from brussels is going too far. the commission proposal falls outside the scope of article 93 of the treaty. registration and motor vehicle taxes are not included in the harmonised taxes. it is not the differences in, for example, motor vehicle taxation that are hampering the functioning of the internal market, but above all the inadequate system of reimbursement of taxes on the import and export of cars. the commission proposal is therefore contrary to the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality. i shall therefore withhold my support for this report and the committee's proposal.
the suspension of the negotiations on the doha development agenda is a blow not only to the wto but also to all the negotiating partners. the wto has lost some of its credibility, while global trade has benefited from the creation and enforcement of international trade rules. i fear that the developing countries will have to pay the price for this suspension. i call on commissioner mandelson to take account of the interests of these countries both multilaterally and bilaterally. the absolute lack of economic prospects in africa, for example, is forcing more and more people to seek safety in europe. i personally have my questions on the g20's composition. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. i also voted in favour of the call by the us trade envoy, susan schwab, for china to show this new responsibility. in your interview with the german magazine internationale politik, commissioner, you said that you wanted to continue working to get doha back on track. i would ask you to clarify the basis for your optimism, especially since you yourself say that you do not expect the negotiations to be concluded before the end of the year. do you have any concrete reason to believe that the americans will move? i would also like to ask you once again to give serious thought to the european agenda after doha, which may be more desirable than we think.
