president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) amending regulation (eec) no 2052/88 on the common organization of the market in bananas. it was agreed that all export subsidies for agricultural products would be abolished in 2013 and that, after the eu, the us and japan would also guarantee free market access for 97% of all products to the least developed countries, there was agreement on a comprehensive package of aid for trade and finally the americans had said that they wanted to start to reduce their support for the cotton sector. after five years of hard work, that was the result. but of course the signature is missing and in my view, ladies and gentlemen, as i have said here, the poorest countries should not be the victims. implementation of these measures is absolutely essential to achieve the mdgs by 2015. now the commissioner has said, in response to a written question from me, that he is prepared to propose a frontloading of this package to the poorest countries. commissioner, can you confirm that again in this debate? can you also confirm that the eu is prepared to unilaterally end its export subsidies in 2013 if necessary? at the same time, do you not think that such a frontloading approach for the least developed countries could help to get things moving again? i am convinced that the european union must be able to take action in the field of human rights and democracy, and that the european union must be able to take action in the field of human rights. you yourself have said that several times. you are right to say: they will pay the price for that, and that should not happen, so we have to commit to that multilateral trade agreement. let the fight against poverty not become the victim of deadlocked negotiations. if the world trade round cannot be saved, at least save the millennium goals.
first of all, mr president, i should like to congratulate mrs gröner on her report. it contains a number of very positive proposals. and by that i mean, first of all, the removal of all drug-related subjects. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat drug abuse. the emphasis on combating trafficking in human beings in this programme is, in my view, the other major point of gain. i would like to take this opportunity to stress the importance of real action. the efforts of many to take measures against forced prostitution and human trafficking around the world cup have not been rewarded with constructive action by those concerned. i hope that the coalition of parliament and the commission, member states, ngos and citizens can mobilize and perhaps force respect for the rights of fellow human beings, and thus reduce abuses. i am less positive about the attitudes towards the division of responsibilities. i am firmly convinced that it is not the role of the government to interfere in the private sphere of individual people without good reason. only when criminal law has been violated is there a reason to do so. these limits are set by member state and it is therefore up to the member states to ensure that they are respected. there is no justification for an active role for europe in this respect, which is why my group has asked for separate votes on a number of such amendments. and when those elements are removed, i will support them.
the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the regulation to include the transport of dangerous chemicals and pesticides. in the parliament there was a large majority in favour of changing the legal basis, and in the council there was even unanimity. the fact that the commission has challenged this amendment before the court of justice has created years of uncertainty for the sector and, moreover, the court's ruling has only complicated matters. the legal basis for article 175 was the european parliament and the council. the commission wanted article 133. the court has decided to recognise both articles as the legal basis, to the surprise of all parties concerned. mr president, this is not an isolated incident. in several cases there is a battle between the commission, parliament and the council over the choice of the legal basis. finally, the matter of the transport of waste, for which i am also rapporteur. i would urge that in future the three parties should not fight these kinds of choices in the court but should come to a solution together. the most important thing is that there should be a clear policy on the choice of legal bases. we have now had three similar cases before the court, all of which have led to different rulings. that is not conducive to the coherence of legislation and also leads to a whole procedure of reparation legislation, such as that which will be voted on shortly. i would therefore call on the commission and the council to agree with this house on a clear procedure for the choice of legal bases. that can only improve the quality of legislation.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on women 's rights and gender equality . president, i thank mr de rossa and mr silva peneda for their work on the report on the european social model. i am glad that the input of the committee on women's rights has been taken seriously and is also reflected in the report. women are indispensable to the social model, just as they are indispensable to the labour market. it is not for nothing that we in europe have set out to increase the participation of women in the labour force. yet women are still far too often missing from the labour market and from the social model. women are often the first victims of a malfunctioning social system. when there is no money for shelters or for sick, old or disabled people, it is often the women, wives and mothers who stay at home to take care of them. that is why i am in favour of a model which takes women into account. i am in favour of a model where the government provides help where it is needed, in an affordable way. i am not in favour of a model devised by brussels which should be introduced throughout europe as such. i am in favour of social solidarity in europe under the motto 'one for all and all for one'. that is what i find in the report we are discussing today. i therefore support the report by mr de rossa and mr silva peneda.
the european parliament has been asked to take a decision on the future of the european union, and i am sure that the commission will be able to do so. the negative effects of demographic change are apparent, and this is making people very reluctant, also in the debate on the european social model. they wonder how sustainable everything we have right now is. can we maintain that pension, that unemployment benefit, if necessary, can we maintain that level of welfare at the present level? i mean, that reluctance. the central question is whether the social security systems, which are very diverse but all based on certain values, can deliver what was promised when they were set up. i am very pleased that the two colleagues, mr de rossa and mr silva peneda, have managed to produce a good report from various political disciplines, which can provide a basis for future discussion. i thank them sincerely for that. the european social model is essentially about building the economy and expanding the economy, otherwise we cannot provide and guarantee social security. we want to guarantee people basic social needs, but of course we have to earn some things first. this means that we need a continuous process of reforming social security so that it gives the citizen what he is entitled to expect. there are different models, and our model is different because it is based on a social market economy and is in fact also based on christian social doctrine. i thank the president for the many good recommendations in this report.
i am glad that the commissioner has taken the initiative to put forward this report. i am also pleased to hear that the system of tradable emission allowances is working well in the eyes of the commissioner and the council. our group has given its wholehearted support to the drafting of this directive and it is therefore important that things work well. i have a few comments, however, and, like mrs doyle, i have some doubts. first of all, it is regrettable that only nine countries have now submitted their plans. it is good to know that the commission is behind this, but it is disappointing that only nine countries are in time. secondly, i think that the allocations made by the member states in the past have been too generous and that the directive on tradable emission allowances has had too little effect. another politically salient problem is the windfall profits made by the electricity companies. the electricity companies have been given free allowances and have been able to sell them on to the energy-intensive industry, but also to consumers, and that is of course unacceptable at a time when the profits of the electricity companies are already increasing. i would like to see a full evaluation of this directive, commissioner, and i think we should also ask ourselves, given the generous allocation and the windfall profits of the electricity companies, whether it is not much better to move to an auction system instead of the grandfathering system we have now.
i would like to express my concern about the problems of counterfeit medicines. counterfeit medicines are manufactured without any form of supervision and are illegally traded. their effects are uncontrollable and therefore pose a potential danger to public health. this is why we must fight against piracy. mr cornillet has already raised this, the commissioner has also spoken about it and i welcome any action the commission is taking to combat this problem. the problems are particularly in the developing countries, the poor countries. i therefore foresee the greatest difficulties in dealing with this piracy efficiently and adequately. in these countries, the supply of reliable, legal medicines is often less well organised than in europe. this gives dealers in counterfeit medicines a relatively wide margin for marketing their medicines, the effects of which cannot be controlled and can even be dangerous to public health. in addition, there is often a lack of legal framework in developing countries. there is simply not enough legislation to deal with this problem effectively. and even if these laws are in place, they leave a lot to be desired. in the developing countries there is a need for affordable, effective and, of course, legal medicines, and i would therefore like to refer to the possibilities offered by generic medicines. they do not eliminate the problem, but they can alleviate the need that exists. unfortunately, it is still little used. maybe there's more to it. the commissioner says that an international convention or standard is not adequate. i imagine that a convention or a standard can do something, and if this is not adequate enough, there must be more. i think that a clear and universal approach is extremely important. europe will have to help, and i am thinking here of the transfer of knowledge, to tackle counterfeit medicines more effectively. i would like to ask the commissioner, but he has already said something about this in his explanatory statement: what are the new plans to further tackle counterfeit medicines and the organisation in this area, particularly where criminal activities are concerned?
president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, europe has little power in the field of public health and it must remain so, because health is first and foremost a responsibility of the member states. europe has a very important complementary role: a common research policy with a strong emphasis on health and a public health action programme aimed at improving the quality of care and treatment methods, by exchanging data, pooling expertise and encouraging countries to learn from each other. it is precisely in the field of mental health that this approach shows promise, and that is why, commissioner, i am also pleased with the green paper. i also thank john bowis for his commitment and involvement in this. he gave an excellent report. ladies and gentlemen, as has been said several times this evening, one in four europeans, or 100 million europeans, will experience at least one period of serious health problems in their lifetime. that is tragic and on top of that it also costs 3 to 4% of the gnp. mental health problems can be anything: eating disorders in girls or young women, alcoholism, drug addiction, behavioural problems, depression or any other psychiatric condition. successful treatment methods for these conditions are difficult to develop. take drug addiction, for example. what is the best approach to recovering from addiction? medication, methadone, talk therapy or a narconon approach with saunas and vitamins? a serious comparison of the success of the different methods of withdrawal would be useful. or take eating disorders and depression: there are success stories, but there are just as many failures. what i'm interested in is bringing expertise together, that therapists learn from each other, that patients or addicts gain insight into successful treatment methods. it is also useful to set up a european coordination group of experts. involve patients, addicts and health care providers. make it a kind of coordination point with a website where one in four europeans and their family circle can go if they have mental health problems. then, mr president, european cooperation is a very valuable thing.
europe is facing major threats to public health: i've been told that there's a lot of things that can happen to you. however, mental illness also belongs in this category. after all, why are people with mental illness treated differently from people with heart disease, aids, or cancer? in an age when patients are increasingly seen as consumers, better informed and more involved in decision-making, a change in attitudes towards mental health is also appropriate. at present, the eu shares responsibility for public health and healthcare with the member states. member states are responsible for national health services and systems. the eu is only given a complementary role. i am in favour of a greater role for the eu in public health matters, precisely because they are so important for citizens. in order to promote and support cooperation between member states in the field of mental health and to eliminate disparities between member states, it is appropriate for the union to take action in this area, for example through cooperation, the promotion of cohesion and the creation of a platform. in this way, the eu can be of service to citizens and change the negative image of bureaucracy and unnecessary rules that many citizens have.

president. - everyone expects that within a year the independence long sought by the vast majority of the people of kosovo will be recognised by many states. the discussions on this are about the conditions for independence and how the minorities of serbs, roma and others are protected. the question of the northern part of the city, which wants to return to serbia, and the possibility of creating new municipalities specifically for serbs are still uncertain. my group has bad memories of the european union's attitude to kosovo between 1989 and 1999. nobody seemed interested in the non-violent mass resistance led by the later president ibrahim rugova, who boycotted all serbian state institutions and organized their own administration and education in albanian. the war in 1999 was not aimed at the liberation of kosovo, but at the subjugation of serbia to meet other european and american desires. the now inevitable independence of kosovo is unfortunately not a choice motivated by a desire for democracy and equality but the result of an industrial accident. after seven years, it is clear to almost everyone that continued occupation will not solve anything, while return to serbia would lead to guerrilla war and huge flows of refugees. i agree with the questioners from four other groups that the european union is now obliged to play a positive role in the peaceful and correct separation of serbia and the building of democracy and economy in kosovo.

on behalf of the pse group. - madam president, i would like to thank our rapporteur, mr toomas ilves, for his work, also on behalf of my group. albania is a difficult, complicated country and i think that the analysis and conclusions he draws with regard to albania are correct. we too welcome the signing of the stabilisation and association treaty and will vote in favour of it in parliament today. the treaty reflects the achievements of the past few years and opens the way to candidate membership of the european union on the basis of the agreements reached in thessaloniki. our group will continue to support albania in the steps it still has to take on the road to european integration. until the early 1990s, albania was the most closed country on the european continent. with this signature, it joins several other countries in the balkans which have taken bold steps in the past year. croatia is now negotiating eu membership, macedonia has been granted candidate status and bosnia-herzegovina has also made clear progress. we as a group have for a moment considered asking for a postponement of the vote on this agreement, because of the upcoming local elections in albania, to put a little pressure on the authorities there to ensure that the elections are conducted in an orderly manner, but we think that you can also turn the argument around, by saying that the signing of this agreement, the approval of this parliament, is a positive incentive for all parties in albania to behave decently and to continue on the road of political and economic reforms, whereby we can work with the criteria that we have used in other countries working on a future within the european union, in particular, of course, the first copenhagen criterion. at the same time, however, we must not lose sight of the fact that we still have a long way to go. this signature does not mean that the albanian government, which has presented the conclusion of this treaty as a major victory, can rest on its laurels, and i hope that this is being recognized in albania. if we look at the copenhagen criteria, the ultimate benchmark for eu membership, there are a number of warnings to be addressed to the albanian government. i've already said something about the local elections. we expect everyone to do their best to ensure that it is free and fair. we will be watching this and we hope that the commission and the council will also be watching how things are going. a second point i would like to make is the situation in the media. free and independent media are essential for a transparent and robust democracy. the country's government clearly has a responsibility to provide this, but we must note that the media are under pressure in part, including through the proposed amendment to the law on electronic media. the independence of the judiciary also raises questions. these are all issues which the european union will have to keep a close eye on, even after the ratification of the stabilisation and association agreement. finally, we see a degree of political polarization in the country that is of concern to us. that's not new, but we still don't see the end. we therefore call on the government and the opposition to act in a pragmatic and constructive manner, because only then can albania fully exploit the opportunities offered by this agreement.
the albanian part of europe is the most forgotten part of europe. the albanians were backward farmers and herders whom no one wanted to take into account. when the ottoman empire collapsed nearly a century ago, both serbia and italy tried to gain a lasting hold on the albanian territory. it is a miracle that in the most inhospitable part of the albanian residential area an independent state could still be formed in 1913. that state has not been a success so far. after a right-wing dictatorship, a foreign occupation and a left-wing dictatorship, what is particularly striking is the chaos and intolerance between the political parties. the north of the country in particular is unsafe and backward. the millions of albanians living around albania on the southern edge of montenegro, in most of kosovo and in northwestern macedonia, do aspire to language rights and self-government, but expect little positive from joining albania as it is. the public opinion in the european union also prefers to keep albania out of the way. the country is now known for child trafficking, pyramid schemes, energy waste, mistreatment of prisoners, and international crime. the problems seem intractable. the preparatory discussions have shown that other groups are more optimistic about the situation in albania than my group and that they have less need to invite albania to change. despite this difference of opinion, my group also believes that continued isolation and exclusion from european cooperation will not solve albania's enormous problems. the promise of a prospect of accession to the european union must be fulfilled. in the long run, before it can get that far, it needs support for democracy, education, the environment and the economy. i regret that the proposed decision seems to be more concerned with guarantees for foreign companies than with the environment, food safety and good governance. we believe that the neighbourhood policy and association agreements should not serve the interests of the present eu member states unilaterally, but should above all help the potential future members of the union to progress.
the german press reports today that there are serious objections within the bundeswehr to the conditions imposed by the lebanese government on foreign aid to protect its 225 km long maritime border against illegal arms transfers. beirut itself would like to control a six-nautical-mile strip of the coast and beyond that, the control of the navies of various eu member states is desired, including my country, the netherlands. the president of the bundeswehr association does not accept these conditions, and i think he is right, mr president, because what navy of an eu member state would want to watch helplessly as hezbollah is being rearmed? the decision on this specific mandate now rests with the united nations. i would like to hear from this council and commission what action they are taking on this issue at the un, secretary-general annan, and how they are reacting to it, what position they are taking on this controversy, because it has a direct bearing on european involvement in stabilizing the situation around lebanon. it is significant, mr president, that even the two representatives of hezbollah in the lebanese government voted against this dangerous compromise proposal. it is logical, in short, that israel is maintaining its naval blockade against lebanon for the time being.

hizbollah has been bombing haifa and natanya for years now. how are we supposed to trust europe if you are not even willing to call that terrorism?’ our resolution speaks of root causes that need to be addressed. let me try to define these root causes in the words of daniel levy, co-author of the geneva peace initiative. he said that the palestinian cause, the injustice of occupation, the hypocrisy of the united states and the west are a grievance for millions worldwide. some abuse and use that and will continue to do so with effect until this conflict is resolved. in order to be effective in the region we need the trust of ordinary citizens. we can earn this trust only by consistently addressing these root causes, but we can earn my friend renan’s trust only if we are willing to say out loud that hizbollah is a radical militant group that abuses the palestinian cause to strengthen its position as a threat to democracy in lebanon and as a threat to ordinary israelis.

why beijing's doctrine of peaceful reunification, especially the ominous one-child policy, which the socialist group is particularly concerned about, currently has no chance with the population: "we its authoritarian rule, and it is not likely to do so in the foreseeable future". i think that this is a satisfactory answer to the socialist amendments, which, curiously and unofficially, in view of the usual use of words in eu documents, refer to two systems in relation to china and taiwan. it's a matter of self-disclosure. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, with this report the european rapporteur. – mr president, today we are discussing the pnr agreement with the united states. however, we are all fully aware of the fact that this issue can only be debated in a wider context, particularly in the light of the information received last night to the effect that president bush has finally admitted that secret cia detention camps actually exist. we should look at this issue in the wider context of the methods used to combat terrorism – the methods that we in europe want to use in the fight against terrorism and the methods that are used by our friend and ally, the united states. the case of the cia detention camps and rendition flights and the case of the pnr agreement demonstrate very clearly the urgent need for europe to speak with a single voice. we urgently need a single european policy in this area. i welcome what commissioner frattini has said, because in my view a common eu policy is not one designed by a handful of ministers that meet behind closed doors, in informal meetings. an eu policy is one made following a proper democratic procedure that involves parliamentary scrutiny, i.e. in codecision with parliament. you therefore have my wholehearted support for the proposal on the bridging clause. i hope that the governments too will now realise how urgent that is. with regard to the pnr issue itself, we should distinguish three phases. one is the short-term renegotiation of the current agreement, which is very urgent and needs to be concluded by the end of the month. i have heard rumours which seem to indicate that the us is in no great hurry to conclude the agreement and might actually ultimately prefer bilateral agreements. it is therefore very important that all european countries and the eu institutions close ranks and adopt a united stance. i very much welcome the much more forthcoming attitude adopted by the council and commission this week. that is the best way forward. were there to be no agreement it would be absolutely catastrophic for the european union and for the protection of our citizens’ personal data. with regard to this agreement, we all seem to agree that the substance of the agreement should preferably be left untouched. we would have preferred to improve the agreement, but the us would like to move in another direction. i therefore feel that the best we can achieve at the moment is to have the same agreement but on a new legal basis. during the negotiations, however, you should insist that the undertakings made in the current agreement be implemented without delay, because assessments have shown that even if there is an agreement it is not being fully and correctly implemented. i would notably point to: the switch from the ‘pull’ to the ‘push’ system, which is long overdue; information to passengers, which is key but which is apparently still not happening; and purpose limitation – which is a more difficult issue, but an absolutely crucial one. i hope you can see to it that the undertakings are integrated into the agreement rather than being a sort of appendix that is not legally binding. with regard to the medium and long term – i.e. the post-2007 review – we very much hope that by then the passerelle clause will have been adopted, that parliament will have secured codecision and that everything will be dealt with as part of a proper democratic procedure. at that point, parliament will insist on proportionality, which is to say that there should be data-sharing, because we all want a safer world and to fight the scourge of terrorism, but that this should be proportional. we should not share more data than is strictly needed to achieve our purpose. it goes without saying that there should be adequate protection of personal data and procedural safeguards. that is quite clear. one of the proposals in the report, which fortunately received the support of the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs, is that we start a parliamentary transatlantic dialogue. i feel that this rift with the us needs to be healed. we must fight terrorism together. it cannot be that while we call the americans ‘cowboys’ they call us ‘cowards’, and we are deeply divided. at the same time, there are proposals circulating on a european pnr policy. i am rather unhappy that we only learned about this through the media. we have heard that this idea was presented at an informal meeting in london a couple of weeks ago. that is not the way forward. if there is to be such a policy then we would prefer that it were presented here in parliament. finally, i should like to make a very urgent appeal to the council to adopt proper arrangements for data protection under the third pillar, along the lines set out by our rapporteur, mrs roure. (applause)

author. - mr president, after decades of war and intensive norwegian mediation, successive sri lankan governments have declared their intention to reach an agreement with the rebellious tamil tigers. it could even be a confederation in which the central government cannot interfere with the democratically elected administration of the autonomous north-east of javna. unfortunately, this good promise has become the subject of political struggle within the sinhalese population, some of whom feel that the tamil rebels should settle for less. this division within the majority group undermines confidence in a peaceful solution within the minority. as long as the tamil minority is not yet certain of political agreement within the sinhalese majority group or of previous promises, we cannot expect them to wait in good faith for an offer. meanwhile, both sides have resumed violence. europe must not consistently take sides but do everything possible to promote peace and harmony. the ltte rebel group is not banned in sri lanka, but unfortunately has been listed as a terrorist organisation in the european union since may 2006. that makes negotiations difficult and invites endless violence. the fact that an organisation is put on the terrorist list means that no negotiations can be held with that organisation and that all attention is focused on its destruction. we have known for many years that this organization cannot be destroyed and shut down, even if we wanted to. that's why it's been talked about for many years. we need the ltte to achieve harmony and peace. we must therefore do everything we can to put an end to the despair that has led her to resort to violent means again.
author. mr president, over 60 years of isolation has developed in north korea a model of government and a way of life that is very different from anything else you find anywhere else in the world. moreover, it seems that more attention is being given to missiles and atomic bombs than to adequate food production. people who feel uncomfortable living in such conditions are trapped. the border with south korea is still closed. neighboring china is not a solution either, as long as it sends some refugees back to a country that is likely to punish deviants severely. the people who are sent back, we never hear from them again. therefore, an alternative must be provided for those who wish to flee. these people are now being sent to poor countries like vietnam and cambodia, but increasingly to the much more prosperous thailand. thailand could easily accommodate this relatively small number of people for the time being. although the united nations refugee agency recognizes them as refugees, the thai government unfortunately sees them primarily as illegal border crossers. this means that after all the misery they have already suffered, they are sentenced to another 30 days in prison plus a fine that is high by their standards. instead, thailand should welcome them or allow them to travel on to south korea or another country that welcomes them if they so wish. we hope that thailand will also adopt the standards which have been common in europe, which are not followed very consistently today, but which were a tradition. if thailand is to be a democracy, with problems attached to it, it should treat these refugees in the same way.

- i'm the reporter. president. - i think that generosity also applies to the second speaker of the house today, but i will try to keep it short. i would also like to begin by thanking the shadow rapporteurs on this subject, holger krahmer, anja weisgerber and anders wijkman, and all the other shadow rapporteurs who contributed to a good result. air quality is a major health problem, and when i started looking seriously at air quality problems more than six months ago, i was shocked by the seriousness of it. europeans die about nine months earlier because of air quality. 350,000 people die ten years earlier because of air quality. millions of people struggle with asthmatic conditions. not everyone is equally affected by poor air quality. the people who live in the big cities, along the highways, children and the elderly are the hardest hit. air pollution and the acid rain it causes are also a major environmental problem in large parts of europe, especially in scandinavia. we therefore expect a firm policy to reduce air pollution, commissioner, and we are therefore disappointed in the proposals you have put forward. the reports by krahmer and myself are first and foremost an indictment of the indifferent attitude towards air pollution. we are asking for three political changes. firstly, stricter standards, secondly, more attention to implementation and thirdly, source measures to tackle air pollution. i'll start with the standards. the commission's report on the environment calls for a higher level of ambition in both reports, and this is badly needed. the thematic strategy shall choose between the a, b and c levels of ambition and the maximum feasible scenario. all impact assessment studies indicate that even the maximum scenario is still cost-effective and that the optimal scenario is between b and c. the commission nevertheless opts for the a-plus level of ambition, without any further explanation. in the interests of public health, this is an incomprehensible choice. a higher level of ambition can be achieved - as the united states has shown - and is also very cost-effective, as is shown by all the studies which underpin it. the proposals in my report are a few billion more expensive, but they bring substantial health benefits. they are also a strong driver of innovation. the directive sets a new standard for the smaller particulate matter, pm2.5. the smallest particles are the most harmful to health, and the standard is also more precise because the smallest particles contain fewer natural resources such as sea salt and desert sand. pm2.5 is therefore a better measure, but the commission's interpretation is very disappointing. parliament calls for a target and limit value of 20 μg/m3 for pm2.5. this is considerably better than the 25 μg that the commission and the council want, but it is still worse than the 15 μg in the united states and the 10 μg recommended by the world health organization. i am also very curious to see how the commission will incorporate the level of ambition chosen by parliament in the strategy into future legislation and the nec directive. can you comment on that, commissioner? do you now also follow parliament's example and opt for a level of ambition between b and c? i said that we need better standards, but that is only one of the three pillars. our second pillar is focus on implementation. so far, the efforts of the member states have not been very convincing. only four member states now fully comply with the limit values as set out in the current directive. the commission has a duty to ensure that this is done properly, and it needs to do so, because air pollution is a transnational problem. half of the air pollution in the netherlands comes from abroad. the netherlands cannot therefore clean up the air on its own and must rely on the governments in belgium, england and germany to make every effort to tackle air pollution. but the netherlands exports a lot of pollution and therefore the scandinavian countries, germany and belgium too must be able to count on the netherlands to do everything possible to clean up the air. so far the commission has not responded very convincingly to member states that exceed the standards. the committee on the environment wants to see new provisions in the directive to encourage the commission to take a more active stance. the member states will be clearly obliged to make an effort. some countries need more time to reach the limit values. i can agree with that as long as they make every effort, but without effort, no exception. adequate efforts are the condition for exception. exceptions are not, as in the commission proposal, a premium for doing nothing, and amendment no 66 makes that clear once again. the third pillar, ladies and gentlemen, is an ambitious resource policy. without a source policy, without a policy to make cars, freight traffic, shipping and industry run cleaner, it is very difficult for member states, and especially for the densely populated member states, to make the air cleaner. the only way to improve air quality is to stop traffic and industry. these are draconian measures and at the same time ineffective. the bill is being passed unilaterally to the lower levels of government, and that is of course unacceptable. a serious resource policy is good for the air, for the environment and for public health, but ultimately it is also good for energy savings and for the climate because of the reduced co2 emissions. and it's also good for innovation: for example, cleaner and more fuel-efficient cars sell better. the commission is therefore proposing to include article 30a in the directive, which calls for a source policy for 2010. the strategy therefore proposes an interinstitutional agreement to establish a source policy. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not distorted. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat air pollution, and it is not surprising that the commission has taken a number of measures to combat it. i think that the environment committee has laid a solid foundation with these three pillars. we have made a new compromise which has made things a little bit more clear and i thank once again all those who have contributed to this, and in particular holger krahmer, anja weisgerber and anders wijkman. we have made some important political changes with stricter standards, more attention to implementation and source policy. this approach is a new europe, close to the people, with a view to the implementation of legislation by national and local authorities and focused on innovation.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the environment is protected, and i am sure that the commission will take the necessary steps to ensure that this is done. the purpose of the legislation - and i am concentrating particularly on the draft directive - is therefore very good and i particularly support the efforts of rapporteur holger krahmer to make the legislation as workable as possible. we must ask ourselves whether we are on the right track here as the european union and whether we are not in the process of creating symbol legislation. why do i say that? we want to impose strict requirements on member states when they are already unable to meet the old air quality standards. since the entry into force of the air quality directive in 2005, which sets limits for pm10 particles, 10 member states have failed to comply with the requirements. and already in the run-up to 2005, it should have been clear to the commission that member states would not meet the limit values without a systematic source policy, i.e. limiting exhaust gases from cars, lorries and ships. between 2002 and 2004, around 96% of cities exceeded daily standards and annual standards were exceeded in several areas in southern europe, in the benelux and in germany, as well as in central and eastern europe, totalling around 73% of cities. without source measures, which have often been announced by the commission years ago, a little more or less stringent values for pm10 or pm2.5 are of no use, let alone nitrogen. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we are trying to get all these resources in, but they are not there yet. the opposition to this policy in the commission (and indeed in this parliament) is enormous. local short-term measures are ineffective. effective policies must focus on long-term measures and thus also on resource policies. that's not the case here. the aim of the legislation is good, but the route chosen to achieve it is questionable.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the air quality in the member states is not affected by pollution. my analysis is that the problems that exist in the netherlands must also exist in almost all the other member states, especially in urban areas. the agreement now reached between the political groups therefore very rightly gives a possibility of delay before the limit values for pm10 become applicable. i shall advise my group to accept this agreement. i would also like to draw particular attention to amendment no 76, because in my view the position of nitrous oxide has been wrongly excluded from the agreement. i would ask members to support that amendment. i would like to address commissioner dimas. we have heard you say, commissioner, that the commission cannot accept that any delay should be linked to the commission proposing new measures. you will see tomorrow that you have a large majority against you. you have no doubt also read the report drawn up by the european environmental policy institute last week. it says that such a link is an extraordinary good thing. you really don't see any way to meet us? finally, of course, a word of thanks to the two rapporteurs. i know it can be very difficult to sift through such a sensitive file between the rocks. i think you have done a very good job, and for that we should thank you both. jonas sjöstedt, thank you for the good collegial cooperation, you're doing well.
i would like to thank mrs anja weisgerber in particular. - why? - why? because she's been so understanding, so listening, and also worked on a good compromise with holger krahmer and also with dorette corbey. i thank them all. mrs corbey, you are in favour of a strict and new source policy. in order to achieve the air quality standards, mrs breyer and mrs hassi, we can put strict standards on paper. but if we don't have the ability to achieve it technically, then it's not going to do you any good. no, moreover, the netherlands is being condemned for wanting to speed up the introduction of soot filters. now, if all that is not allowed, then we shouldn't set strict standards either, because we can't meet them. i also know, and i say this again to mrs hassi - because mr blokland and mr maaten have already said many things with which i fully agree - i also know why the netherlands voted for this legislation. they did so because it gave them the opportunity to get some relief on a number of points. that is precisely why they voted for it. but what do you see when you compare the council's position with that of parliament? the environment ministers can sleep well at night, because they have set strict standards. but then they come home and they're confronted with an investigation by the environmental protection agency that says that everything they've put on that patient paper is not going to make it home anyway. this policy must be stopped. i can't explain that at home anymore. that is why i support the compromise reached here in parliament on all points. we in parliament say 'strictly' but in a feasible way.
in the past, the countryside dominated and the cities were the exception. this has changed considerably over the last century, as a result of population growth and the emergence of urban occupations, with the vast majority of europeans now living in real cities, in urban suburbs and in former rural areas heavily urbanised by industry, ports or mining. in the third world, but also in parts of europe, we see that such urban areas can become totally uninhabitable through traffic chaos, squatting, pollution and land speculation. lacking clean drinking water, a dense network of frequent public transportation, good waste disposal, and affordable, good housing, or lacking health, welfare, and education, the city becomes hell. once the city is in trouble, people with high incomes and high education can move away from the area, but workers and migrants have no choice but to stay. national and european policies are still based on the growth of the number of cars and on cuts in public services. it threatens the future of our cities. the european union, which was originally focused mainly on agricultural production and the countryside, has rightly shifted its attention in recent years to the cities. unfortunately, that was also the case in a negative sense. if the european commission's 2000 proposal for a public procurement obligation for urban transport had been adopted unchanged, it would have slowed down the expansion of tram networks, free public transport and the maintenance of a dense and frequent transport network. urban transport, urban planning, urban planning, green areas and cycle paths should be managed by the city and the region as much as possible. the european union can contribute to research, exchange and transfer of knowledge. this will help to combat risks and abuses, to promote initiatives for improvement and to strengthen the development and enforcement of better environmental standards. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area. i support the rapporteur, mr hegyi, in his efforts to promote clean transport, the protection of historic urban centres, careful water management, energy conservation and the protection of natural areas.

the european parliament's discharge for 2004 certainly took an unusual course. if it were not for a newspaper article just before the discharge date which drew attention to the fact that the purchase of two new buildings in strasbourg had involved a compensation amount, we would probably have simply discharged in april. once that message was received, the administration responded, and rightly so, by canceling the purchase of those two buildings. i should also like to say that the committee on budgets has responded very adequately by immediately setting up an internal committee of inquiry to examine all aspects of the matter. i can only say that mr ferber has played a brilliant role in this process and in that committee of inquiry. he deserves all the credit for this. the committee of inquiry itself has also done a good job and has done it quickly. the conclusion for me is also - on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, i wish to begin by paying tribute to the rapporteur for a magnificent job. even if we did not always agree, he was extremely patient and open to cooperation, for which i am very grateful. it is very important that we make our position clear once and for all. i am concerned that the report we adopted in committee has been interpreted in so many different ways. there have been jubilant press releases stating that the european parliament has finally called for a framework directive, whereas others state that the european parliament has finally rejected the framework directive once and for all. we need to get away from a situation in which we have watered-down compromises that can be interpreted in various ways. i can state, on behalf of my group, that we do not want a framework directive. we do not want a one-size-fits-all solution for europe, precisely because we want subsidiarity. but what is subsidiarity? subsidiarity means that the national and local authorities can define their services of general interest and decide on how they are organised and financed. subsidiarity does not mean automatic exemption from the market rules. we are therefore talking about two kinds of subsidiarity here. i listened very carefully to what mr barroso said and again he left some room for interpretation. i would like to interpret his remarks as meaning that he also wants targeted and concrete solutions for real problems. because another recommendation i should like to make to this house is: if it ain’t broke, don’t fix it! in that case, it is not legislation we need, but solutions to real problems. i have worked for a local authority, so i know what the real problems are. local authorities have very legitimate concerns. however, we have to acknowledge that there is also a tendency towards protectionism. we will have to find the right balance here. we also need to get away from the ideological debate, or at least be honest and admit that there is a certain degree of ideology involved. some people very legitimately feel that the public authority should also be the provider of services. i agree with my colleague, mr hökmark, that the market can provide excellent services. some sectors have been liberalised and that did not mean that more people were excluded from access to those services, but quite the contrary: many services have come within reach of many more people, which is extremely social. a market is not a jungle; it has rules, because if there are no rules it is not a market. any market – even the simplest market on the market square – has rules and that is exactly what we mean by the social market economy we have in europe. we need to recognise the diversity of the member states and allow them to decide for themselves what they call ‘services of general interest’. finally, we should not try to define what services of general interest and services of general economic interest are, because the word ‘economic’ applies not to the nature of the service itself but to the way in which it is provided. to take an example: everybody will recognise that water is of general interest, but it may be provided in a purely commercial way. so we must not lose ourselves in a senseless debate on the differences between ‘of general interest’ and ‘general economic interest’. the only interest we should be looking at here is the interest of our citizens and consumers.
the exclusion of services of general interest from the scope of the controversial services directive was seen as a victory at the time. the public sector would be spared bolkestein, but that may have been a pyrrhic victory. the definition of services of general interest is only now being defined. the fight for a directive on such services should be a battle over what is and is not marketable and, above all, who can best judge that. we will defend tooth and nail what we understand by the public interest against liberalization. market effects in e.g. education, health care, culture: we have experience and we've had enough! we know europe as a 'never enough' place when it comes to liberalizing things that do not belong to the market but actually belong to society. the european parliament has been a very active participant in the debate on the future of the community. the latter is already happening with a new onslaught on health care by the commission. we believe that this directive will be a means for citizens, pupils, patients and consumers to defend themselves against the 'never enough' of the union which is becoming more and more liberal. the directive should make clear that it is the member states and their citizens who determine what services are in the public interest, and that brings politics closer to the citizen, where it belongs.
mr president, in the 19th century the government took on many tasks which were not originally governmental. education, access to health care and the regulation of working hours are examples of tasks which were not previously primarily its responsibility. it was and is necessary for the government to regulate tasks where the market fails. the distinction between services of general interest and services of general economic interest is theoretical. the purpose of this distinction is therefore to free services from the market and to limit government intervention. the limitation of government intervention is a good aim in areas where citizens can be expected to be self-sufficient and responsible. however, making this distinction should not mean restricting access for citizens. i therefore call for restraint in the release of services of general economic interest to the market. society is served by a government that gives justice to the weak and ensures access to services essential to daily life.
mr president, as the coordinator of the pse members of the committee on economic and monetary affairs i asked to be the last speaker on the pse group side, precisely in order to remove some of the prejudices and misunderstandings about this debate. so i hope i can eliminate some of those misunderstandings. firstly, i want to thank mr barroso for his speech and to say that we in the pse group fully understand his four principles and his view that it is necessary to create more legal certainty for the service providers, whether they are public or private or something in between, for the member states and the decentralised public authorities, and for the citizens. as mr harbour rightly said, that is the crucial element. i think mr barroso has correctly interpreted the report that mr rapkay prepared on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and that we voted for by a large majority in the committee. we have not expressed a preference for any particular form of legal instrument, but have made it clear that legislative initiatives are needed and that we should have a full political process of decision-making on this, in which parliament and the council can participate fully. codecision is the right way to deal with these political issues. that is the message of the report. the report does not say that there should be a horizontal instrument or sector-specific instruments only; it leaves the options open and says that both methods can be used in parallel. it will be clear from the debates that preferences differ, but also that there are a lot of prejudices about the options and preferences we promote. in my political group, i chaired a group of legal experts who drafted a text. unfortunately i have the impression that many did not read this text, because if mr harbour had read it he would see that it is not a political statement but a clear attempt to reconcile the rules of the market, of competition, the subsidiarity that we want to guarantee for local authorities, the quality aspects and the importance of public service and services of general interest and economic interest for the citizens of europe. this is not ideological; it is a concrete attempt to reconcile these. the draft is open for debate and we hope you will participate in discussing it. but it is up to the commission to come up with real proposals and then we can set to work on the legislative methods and formulations. for the vote on this report, again i state that we have agreed to disagree on the issue of which instruments would be preferable. there are amendments now from both sides, from the alde group and from the verts/ale group, to try again to force a decision on the preference. i do not think that is wise. i agree fully with mrs thyssen that it is wiser to stick to the agreement that we had and to leave this issue open. it is the commission that has to decide and if one of those amendments is adopted the only result will be that whole report would fall. that would be a pity because, as everyone has said, mr rapkay and the committee on economic and monetary affairs have produced a good report.
the text of the resolution, mr president, strikes a good balance between the freedom which member states must retain in the future in organising and defining their public tasks and the respect for the rules of the internal market. the focus of europe - and the text of the resolution is clear on this too - must be on legal clarification and a sectoral approach where there are problems and uncertainties. we are asking for legal certainty, but legal certainty does not mean restricting competition and protectionism. we also ask for legal certainty to help local and regional authorities, but we must be well aware that these same local authorities want to retain their own margin of choice and do not want additional rules imposed. we must above all make room for diversity and not freeze it in a horizontal regulatory framework. the dynamic must be maintained for a europe that is capable of fighting. president barroso, i very much appreciate your presence in person in this debate. we ask you for clarity, but you can expect clarity from us. the epp will therefore vote against the amendment by the socialists calling for a framework law. mr van den burg spoke of the prejudices and misunderstandings of the socialist group's intentions. but your group's chairman, mr schulz, is even feeding those prejudices, because he says that we must guarantee social standards at european level, that we must also guarantee quality standards and the quality of service at european level. i 'm not going to do that . the commission has no framework law and no horizontal regulatory framework.
in writing. this report is more than just the approval of the 2004 accounts. without amendment no 9 it would also mean an authorization for the bureau of this parliament to spend the reserve on the acquisition of buildings in strasbourg. we do not object to the calculation that shows that in the long run buying is more advantageous than continuing a lease. if that were the only way, we would vote in favour. more importantly, it is an important contribution to the sustainability of the monthly strasbourg weeks. they cost €200,000,000 a year or more now. in september 1999 the dutch parliament unanimously voted against the continuation of the european parliament's meetings in two different cities. the dutch representatives in all eu bodies were therefore called upon to put an end to this as soon as possible. this is in line with what the media and public opinion in the netherlands have been saying for many years. the purchase of buildings in strasbourg is seen in the netherlands as one of the most important decisions of this parliament, but it is not discussed separately after the purchase authorization hidden in this item. we shall therefore vote against.
in 2004, romania and bulgaria were considered less mature than the ten new member states. there was no doubt that with some delay they would receive equal treatment. the group of the european united left has always supported this approach. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for approval, and the council will be consulted on the matter. we emphasize in our opinion human rights, proper democratic governance and good environmental policy. when i voted in 2005, i voted in principle for the future accession of bulgaria and romania. the question then remained open as to when and how this enlargement could take place in a good way. this parliament has provided for the right to decide on the postponement of the vote in respect of romania, while the application of this option in respect of bulgaria has been left entirely to the council. this was at a time when the concern about the shortcomings in romania was the greatest and bulgaria was expected to cause the least problems. the european union is not a party to the treaty of rome. in both countries, the situation of the roma population is worse than in most other new member states. in a number of cases, the place where they have long resided is declared illegal and they are expelled. the hungarian minority in romania and the turkish minority in bulgaria are also facing problems, although they are less severe than in the recent past. it is no longer a deliberate governmental disadvantage but a laxity in the face of the discrimination that ultra-nationalists exercise against these groups. anti-semitism has not disappeared either. in previous debates on this subject, my group has put forward proposals to demand improvements, including in relation to the illegal payments being demanded in bulgaria from transit european union citizens of turkish origin. unfortunately, a majority of this parliament has not supported our demands. this was the case with rosia montana, where mining is prepared with the aid of dangerous poison. a communication circulated by romania on 23 september reports that an environmental impact assessment has been launched and that consultations are under way with neighbouring hungary on the danger of toxic waste water flowing into the country. unfortunately, as there is no final result yet, it cannot be involved in the final judgment of this parliament on romanian membership on 11 october. my group fears that after accession many problems will have to be solved and that, in the event of setbacks, further enlargement of the european union will become increasingly controversial in public opinion in the present member states. nevertheless, after the preparatory debate, it seems that a majority of my group will vote in favour of accession on 1 january 2007. a major argument for this is that other member states have joined earlier despite their unresolved problems and that a year's delay will not bring about significant improvements. finally, i should also like to inform you that my party, the socialist party, has spoken out against the 2007 accession date together with the christian democrats, on the basis of the many problems in the dutch parliament. we think that there are still a lot of problems to be solved, but the real vote will take place on 11 october.

rapporteur. – mr president, we are bringing to the final debate this report on turkey at a special moment. this report is preceding the european commission’s progress report, and therefore provides a unique opportunity to influence and enrich the stance of the european commission. this does not mean, however, that there would be so much need to influence the commission on this point, because the report as i have presented it to the committee on foreign affairs, and as i present it now here in this sitting, has been prepared in very good cooperation with the european commission and with commissioner rehn himself. i also think of mr rehn’s words when i presented the report in the committee on foreign affairs, words of welcome which were very clear. the committee on foreign affairs adopted the report by a large majority: 54 votes to 6. the report has been described as fair but tough: fair because it is based on facts and is constructive; tough because, unfortunately, there is reason for criticism of at least a strong push from parliament. the basic conclusion of the report is that we, as the european parliament, regret that there is a slowdown in reforms. we had expected at the start of the negotiations in 2004, when we made a decision, that the impressive reforms of 2002-2004 would continue. unfortunately they have slowed down. that is not to say that nothing has happened. i compliment the turkish politicians on their ninth package of legislative reforms and the many proposals that were adopted by parliament. i also compliment the turkish officials for their professional work in the negotiations so that the first chapter could be provisionally closed. so why then is there criticism? because we would have liked to have seen more progress in the mainstream of further political reforms. i will mention a few vital areas. firstly, on freedom of expression, we as a parliament have already criticised some parts of the new penal code. although it was a big improvement, some articles raised doubts – article 301, first and foremost. some people have been set free. however, the reconfirmation of the sentence of mr hrant dink proves, as ali birand, the famous journalist, also stated so clearly, that article 301 has to be changed or repealed. i ask the turkish government and the turkish people to see the need for this. it is important for the freedom of the turkish people, it is important for the image of turkey in europe and the wider world. second, a lot needs to be done as regards freedom of religion. i was in turkey a few weeks ago. until recently, monasteries and churches have been taken away, and the new proposed law on foundation, as the commissioner said, is not good enough. so please let this stop. give back the properties to the religious minorities and change the proposal of the law on foundation so that if they cannot be given back, then at least there is a good compensation for the minority churches. also, the training of clergy must resume. since 1971 seminaries are closed, and for a country that wants to be european, it is very important that it should be as easy to build a christian church in turkey as it is to build a turkish mosque in the rest of the european union. we said we want priority for these reforms because that is the european spirit. we ask it as a parliament, and turkey signed, in the revised accession partnership, a commitment to achieve the short-term priorities before the end of 2007, and then we as a parliament asked turkey to do what was agreed. a third situation that worries us is the situation in the south-east. we strongly condemn the pkk and any terrorism. we strongly condemn it – there is never any excuse for terrorism; but at the same time we ask turkey to try to find peaceful interlocutors, try to find a political way and invest in the social and economic development of the south-east. a last major point: cyprus. it was agreed at the start of negotiations that there would be no formal recognition ceremony by turkey of cyprus but that there would be an ankara protocol as a kind of normalisation of relations. the protocol was signed, the council asked for it to be implemented before the end of 2006, and it is now a matter of credibility – also for the credibility of our institutions – that turkey fulfils its commitments and implements a protocol before the end of 2006. as a parliament, we also make it very clear that we want to see more efforts in the northern part of the island in terms of trade regulation. but once again, turkey cannot just sign up to this: it should do what it promised to do. i shall finish with one last point – i shall try to make it short. there was a vast majority in the committee for the basic line of the report and i thank the members for it because with it we are giving an important signal. there was unease, however, because of an amendment on the armenian genocide. allow me in two sentences to make my position clear. in paragraph 50 of the report, as rapporteur i tried to put forward a position that is both strong but also realistic. it says that recognition as such is formally not a criterion, but for a country on its road to europe it is indispensable to come to terms with its past and therefore we want committees, research, open discussion. i think that is a line that is strong but fair and beyond criticism. i know that, with an amendment from mrs de keyser, another text has been voted in as well. it is of course the responsibility of every member of parliament to vote as he or she pleases, but if you ask for my opinion, the text in paragraph 50 is the best text and the one that takes us the furthest. i have been speaking for too long. i want to thank members once again for their support up till now and i just want to say that i hope, as the rapporteur for turkish accession, that this signal – a tough but fair signal – will be perceived by turkish politicians and the turkish public as an encouragement to restart the reform process, to vigorously support those people who want it, so that i as rapporteur can be more positive in my next report than i have been here today. (applause)

that turkey has already done. it is negative, not only in content but also in tone. this is the way to thwart reforms in turkey, by undermining public support for them and giving turkish eurosceptics a powerful weapon in the upcoming turkish elections. what we really want is for the criticisms in that report to be translated into action, and that will be good for everyone. there 's another way . i have a great problem with the eurlings report as it stands. the problem is not to be found in the many critical paragraphs calling on the turkish government to do more and to speed up reforms. the turkish government is rightly called upon to abolish the infamous article 301, which has already led to dozens of trials against writers and journalists, as soon as possible. my problem with the report is that there are a few paragraphs in the report which are completely out of line, in particular the paragraph which mentions the recognition of the armenian genocide as a kind of condition for accession and the cyprus paragraphs which make it seem as if turkey is the only one to blame for the current problem. the original report, as presented by the rapporteur, was critical but fair and good agreements were made during our discussion in the committee on foreign affairs to improve the report by many amendments. the mistake was that, during that debate, wording was added to the report at the last minute by means of all kinds of oral amendments, which threw the balance of the report out of balance. that was a great success for a number of lobby groups, but it has worsened the report drastically. and what we want, what my group wants by introducing a number of amendments, is actually to go back to the original eurlings report because we thought it was both critical and fair. i am therefore pleased to hear today, and also to read in the interviews of the rapporteur in the turkish press, that under the motto 'better half-turned than completely lost' he is prepared to support the efforts to restore the report to its original form. i therefore count on his support in the votes tomorrow. why is it so important that the report be critical but at the same time fair? i would very much like the european parliament to continue to play a role in the reform debate in turkey by supporting those groups and those people who, like us, are fighting for the same thing in their daily work. it is precisely these people who have approached us in the last few weeks and said: this report in its present form is not helping us. it is only giving wind in the back to those in turkey who are against the reforms and i do not want the european parliament to help those who do not want to help turkey to move forward. if that report does not change, if it remains in its present form, then that means that we are putting ourselves out of the debate. i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this, but i am saying that it should be involved in the work of the european parliament. that is why the changes are necessary, and i count on your support, i count on the support of the rapporteur. i really hope that the majority of this house is prepared to go that way: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament.

the informal summit in tampere in recent weeks has made one thing clear: the member states are fundamentally divided on how to deal with illegal immigration. the need for a solution is clear, but there is disagreement about the means. the member states are therefore eager to bring the immigration problem to the european conference tables. and with that, history repeats itself. in the late 1990s, the netherlands and germany had major problems with the influx of asylum seekers. they called for solidarity and burden-sharing. however, france, spain, italy and portugal blocked any solution at european level. now the northern member states do not respond to the call for help. the solution, however, must be found at european level. a common market with a shared area in which citizens can move freely requires consistent security of the external borders and regulated access to that area. the european policy on illegal immigration is necessary, but it must be such that countries do not continue to act independently. the legalization of 700 000 illegal immigrants by the spanish authorities without the knowledge of other member states cannot be reconciled with the desire for help from other member states. finally, president bush has admitted that there were secret cia prisons. the countries in which these prisons were located and whether there were any in the european union are still unclear. i would like to hear what the council and commission are doing to put an end to this ambiguity.
the immigration problem is one of the greatest challenges in 2006 and you would think that the urgency of this problem has now been felt in all the member states, but nothing could be further from the truth! the report on the tampere meeting last week was a toothache. the lack of agency was rampant. a real humanitarian tragedy is taking place at the external borders of the union, thanks in part to the failed policies of the european member states, and i have to tell you, i am truly ashamed. if there is any focus, it is on strengthening the external borders. frontex is the key word. but frontex depends on the resources and manpower provided by the member states, and these resources remain extremely limited to this day. it is an illusion to think that the flow of migrants can be contained by strengthening the external borders alone. we shouldn't want that either. the reasons why migrants leave their countries en masse must be examined. the link between migration and development is of paramount importance. large and strategic investments in the countries of origin are necessary. call it what you will, a modern marshall plan. mr president-in-office of the council, take the example of commissioner frattini, who has repeatedly called for a comprehensive package of measures. only then will the union be able to have any effect. the commission's proposals are not only a means of achieving the objectives of the treaty, but also a means of ensuring that the community's objectives are met. i would like to thank the rapporteur for her report. provide for such cooperation agreements with the countries of origin and transit. ensure the early establishment of a european return policy in which everyone is entitled to respectful treatment. you're gonna have to make do with those information campaigns. get that european green card. take on your own black market. advice, to stay in your metaphor, i'd ask you to ride as fast as you can.
