mr president, there is a great need for reflection on nanotechnologies. the design of materials at the molecular level creates new properties whose effects on human health and the environment are still unknown. the european commission is proposing an action plan for the period up to 2009. the action plan expresses high expectations of benefits for the economy and society. however, the commission sees ethical considerations and public concerns as obstacles in the first place. in this respect, the action plan is certainly unbalanced, commissioner. unfortunately, this is also true of the ransdorf report, although it does offer some counterbalance. what's missing? first, openness to concerns other than safety risks, including the desirability of new technologies and philosophical questions. the benefits and possible harmful effects must first be identified. this should prevent choices being made early in the development of technology solely on the basis of technical-economic value judgments. secondly, we need to focus more on international coordination in the european union, not only in the area of toxicity testing and life cycle risk assessment, but also in legislation on standardisation, labelling and liability. the person who places the nanoparticles on the market is liable for any damage. finally, the desirability of patenting nanotechnologies and materials, particularly basic and general engineering and materials with a large number of different applications. can the commission provide a critical analysis of this? it is therefore necessary to consider the extent to which patents and licences are putting poor countries further behind and how this can be prevented.
mr president, innovation accounts for some 30% of competitiveness in the highly developed countries, according to the latest survey by the world economic forum. this is particularly true of nanotechnology. for example, nanomedicine is creating opportunities for better treatment of cancer, heart disease, diabetes, alzheimer's and parkinson's. in sum, these diseases account for two thirds of european mortality. european research institutions and companies are among the world leaders in this field. in the netherlands alone, the nano-industry has a turnover of 20 billion euros. in my country, for example, the government is investing a record amount in the centre for molecular medicine in eindhoven. companies are also adding a lot to that, and that's an example of an offensive strategy. the world's development, competition, demands such an attitude. the commission's proposal is to make choices, invest and support europe's core infrastructure, and that is now well expressed in the ransdorf report. it is then the task of the european commission, the member states, regional authorities, industry and smes: i am very pleased that the commission has taken the initiative to put forward a proposal for a regulation on the protection of the environment in the event of a natural disaster. the seventh framework programme for research and development, the european technology platforms and the knowledge regions have provided a good basis, together with the structural funds, also in terms of finances. and then there are the risks. of course, the risks must be considered, especially on a global scale within unesco/oecd. and finally, this autumn, commissioner potočnik is launching a roadmap for research infrastructure. it will then be possible to identify areas, regions or clusters which have the real potential to compete globally. investing is not a global brain drain, but it must become a brain drain in this area, a win for the european economy and the well-being of our citizens.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on international trade . mr president, first of all i would like to thank mr hutchinson for this important report. effective development assistance requires coherence between the eu's aid approach and the eu's trade approach. if we are to achieve the mdgs by 2015, aid and trade can and must complement each other. unfortunately, the reality is often different. aid and trade policies, including agriculture, still too often work in tandem. in this way, it will certainly take another hundred years in africa before we make poverty history. at the moment there is little coherence in our european policy. the trade negotiators of commissioner mandelson preach free trade with global rules, the developers of commissioner michel work on the millennium development goals and the agricultural subsidy policy is against the objectives of both camps. i would like to compare the different policy areas to 'ships that pass in the night'. unfortunately, the development side is all too often compromised. for example, in the economic partnership agreements, the famous or sometimes notorious epas, the commercial side is far too dominant. the development side is too often referred to as the existing aid funds. a truly integrated approach, with real extra money and new development plans, is often far too little. this is the case, in particular, where the aid effectiveness could be increased by the aid provided by the epas, thanks to the increased local cooperation which they provide. we must assume that the weak parties to the negotiations will of course be given the necessary facilities in the area of development, that fair agreements and a realistic timetable will emerge from the epas. the replacement of fiscal transition revenues with a reformed tax system, for example, and the institutional strengthening of public services and social sectors, education, health care, are prerequisites for a real trade take-off. the current lack of coordination and coherence is not only ineffective but also contrary to article 178 of the treaty and therefore unacceptable. one of the reasons is that the trade experts, or the developers, often lack knowledge and expertise of each other's fields of work. the lack of willingness to put the real costs and benefits of integrated development on a par with each other and to seek new funds jointly - and therefore also jointly in the council - also plays a role. in fact, various european policies - agriculture, trade, development and others - often run directly counter to each other. agriculture is dumping its products in north africa with export subsidies because unemployment is rising there. migration complains about the flow of economic refugees from that region and then sends them back with aid money instead of working on good agreements on the regional labour market. only a european agenda for coherence by the commission and the council would substantially improve the situation. global trade is important and very useful in getting developing countries out of their vicious circle of poverty. that process should not stand in the way of their development and therefore there is much to be gained from the right combination of aid and trade. this is the way to achieve the millennium development goals. i call for the european agenda of coherence. let the new development cooperation instrument provide the new framework for this.

the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. southern europe considers the measures insufficient to protect their footwear sector from unfair competition. the eu is growing more divided and mistrustful. dumping is different from fair competition or lower costs. dumping is unfair competition, whether because the dumping companies receive state aid or because they use child labour to reduce their wage costs. if it is a question of dumping, we must close ranks. we cannot simply wipe out unfair competition if the benefits to the whole community seem a little bit bigger for once. unfortunately, my dutch government also uses this argument against the adoption of anti-dumping measures. what a randomness! but who are the benefits for? not for the european consumer. last year, import prices of shoes from outside the eu fell by 25%, while consumer prices remained stable. does the commissioner agree with me that we must work towards a new anti-dumping policy which excludes arbitrariness and brings unity to the eu? we must protect ourselves against unfair competition. we must not only protect our producers, but also our consumers. they don't want to buy products made by children, do they? clean clothes, clean shoes, clean hands.
president, the accession of newcomers to the european union is best achieved when democracy, human rights, environmental protection and prosperity are at about the same level as in the other member states. the european agency for reconstruction can help to overcome some of the differences that are still delaying the arrival of seven states in the western balkans. in recent years, there has been disagreement about the emphasis that this agency should place on education and infrastructure, which are in great need in those countries, or on reforms. the reforms in the areas of administration, justice, police and the market are mainly desired from outside. on the other hand, budgetary support from within is expected for small projects which can be carried out independently without much bureaucracy, supervision and control, and for the repair of railways, bridges, dams and drinking water basins damaged by war or neglect. the transfer of the tasks of this office to commission delegations and offices in 2009 is a radical move. i ask the commission whether it is only helping to simplify the intervention of the european union itself or also to make it easier to accommodate local needs.

the european parliament has been a party to the european convention on human rights since the beginning of the century, and it is not the council that has been the party to the convention. the assembly of the council of europe also has serious reservations. the president of that parliamentary assembly called for a review of the proposal last week. there are two main arguments against the creation of a fundamental rights office. unfortunately, the compromise reached by mr gál does not sufficiently refute these arguments. that is why i am tabled an amendment to reject the proposal which is being put to the vote today. the proposed office will unnecessarily duplicate the work of the council of europe, the court of human rights and the osce. it also makes an unwanted distinction between the 25 eu member states and the other 21 european countries. secondly, the fundamental rights office is hindering a good relationship with the countries around us on this issue. the dialogue and the continuing dialogue between the european union and third countries take place in the context of international relations. in these relations there are more points of discussion than just criticism of fundamental rights. very recently the dutch senate unanimously prohibited the government from voting in favour of this proposal. a dutch veto can only be avoided if the criticisms mentioned are taken into account. so it is obvious that the vote will be postponed.
mr president, human rights are a worldwide issue. despite our huge deficits, we are ahead of europe. the council of europe has reached agreement on the minimum level of human rights to be guaranteed in europe. the european convention on human rights binds 47 member states, 20 more than the 27 that will be part of the european union from 2007. the 2000 charter of fundamental rights is the main common denominator of existing provisions from national constitutions and that european treaty. this charter was later incorporated as chapter ii in the draft constitution. the european union is not a party to the convention on human rights, but it is a party to the convention on the rights of the child. it would be nice if the proposed human rights office could serve as a signal that the european union gives more importance to human rights than to the common market. some in my group see this proposal as a very different signal, namely the attempt to compete with the council of europe and to promote the constitutional text rejected in two referendums. we do not agree with that.

president. - mr president, we are today commemorating in the european parliament a dramatic political event that took place in hungary 50 years ago. the uprising that erupted then and was brutally crushed by soviet troops a few days later has opened the eyes of many in western europe to the dangers of communist ideology as dictated by moscow. the historical significance of budapest 1956 thus goes far beyond the local or national level. as a 13-year-old schoolboy, i only realized that my mother's beloved homeland was fighting a heroic battle for the freedom so passionately desired. the images of prime minister imre nagy and general pál maléter and the fight against the russian tanks are etched on my retina. what we were hoping for in the west was support against all odds. what we lived with the victims and with the refugees. my parents taught me then that hungary was not an eastern european country but a central european country with strong religious and cultural ties to western europe. fifty years later, the soviet bloc has been dismantled and eight, and soon 10, of its former satellite states have joined the european union. what did we do with that freedom we had? national socialist ideology was defeated in 1945 and communist ideology 45 years later. but did anything good come of it? can we build on the ruins of these ideologies a society of tolerance, responsibility and love of neighbor? patience and perseverance are needed. 5 years of occupation in the netherlands required at least 15 years of reconstruction. a period of 50 years of oppression may take several generations to heal all the wounds inflicted. may the hungarian people have the courage and strength to overcome the past together and work together for a future of peace and freedom.
president. - ladies and gentlemen, commissioners, it has already been said: breast cancer is a major and growing health problem. after lung cancer, breast cancer is the most common cancer. in lung cancer, some of the causes are known. smoking and asbestos are major causes, and air pollution is a risk factor. there is something you can do to prevent lung cancer. quitting smoking, for example. breast cancer is actually a little known disease, but there are statistics. the netherlands has the highest relative incidence of breast cancer. there are also statistical links, and i thank the commissioner for the comprehensive and concise answer to my written question. commissioner, you're arguing that there's a link between obesity and breast cancer. there's a link between having children late in life and breast cancer. there is a socioeconomic factor: low-income groups are less likely to participate in screening and therefore are at greater risk of mortality. and finally, and perhaps most importantly, hormones, particularly estrogen, are considered by the commission to be a determinant of breast cancer risk. it could be estrogens from different sources. chemical pollution, i.e. exposure to environmental factors, but also drug or pill use. commissioner, this is important information. in all my contacts with women's groups, these two factors come up again and again. many breast cancer patients themselves suspect that their disease is related to the environment, to medication, or to the use of the pill. can you therefore give us an incentive to continue the investigation as a matter of urgency? in writing. the basques are in an exceptional position compared to most other european peoples. at the beginning of the 19th century, europe was divided between a small number of large multinational states. the congress of vienna in 1815 thought it could fix that state structure and its boundaries forever. since 1830, two thirds of the present european states have arisen violently or peacefully, as a result of the democratic struggle for governance and education in their own language. of the remaining multilingual states, spain and belgium have adopted a federal form of government that recognises linguistic and cultural diversity. unfortunately, within spain, the basque country is still divided between the three main basque provinces, which together form a region, and the navarra region, of which only the western half has a basque majority. the basque people's wishes are similar to those of catalonia, which have now been largely granted. the answer to their demands must not be military and judicial repression, as applied by previous spanish governments and now advocated by the present right-wing opposition. we welcome the fact that the independence movement and the spanish state are at last working together to find a peaceful solution, and that most of the groups in this parliament support this.

on behalf of the ind/dem group mr president, i agree with all the previous speakers. what happened in the ivory coast with the waste from the ship proba koala is terrible and should never have happened. it is quite clear that this was an illegal waste shipment. the basel convention expressly prohibits the shipment of hazardous waste to non-oecd countries and this prohibition has not been upheld. it has long been known that the current legislation on shipments of waste is seriously flawed. samples taken in england and france show that between 75% and 100% of waste shipments are not compliant. for the european parliament, good enforcement and stricter controls were a sticking point in the negotiations during the creation of the new waste shipment regulation. and we've only partially gotten what we wanted. at the moment we do not need new rules or a european minimum penalty for infringement as commissioner dimas suggests. what we need first is enforcement of existing laws. it would be a great honour for the commission and the member states to finally put this to work.
president. - the export of waste to developing countries is prohibited. that's very clear. only normal ship waste and rinsing water are not covered by the ban. but it's also clear that the proba koala's rinsing water was not normal. the deaths, the thousands of wounded and the enormous upheaval in côte d'ivoire are proof of this. but earlier in amsterdam it had already been shown that the waste water was not normal. someone fainted and there was an unbearable and unusual smell. it is clear that the ship with this contents should never have left amsterdam without warning the authorities of the next european port, namely estonia. the netherlands is therefore rightly conducting a criminal investigation to ascertain how the poison ship was able to leave the netherlands and whether the port authorities and inspections made any mistakes. but we need to focus now on how to prevent these disasters in the future. commissioner, i have three questions in this connection. in estonia you said that dumping poison in the ivory coast was the tip of the iceberg. on what basis did you make this determination? secondly, do you agree that ships leaving the eu should be obliged to discharge their waste water and waste at the last eu port? finally, what measures can and will the commission take to strengthen controls on the export of hazardous waste?

president. - the disaster in côte d'ivoire calls for a rapid european response. the victims and the damage to the environment must be compensated, and the polluter must of course be held responsible for the costs incurred. if europe wants to move forward, it will have to make clear to its citizens where its added value lies. the incident in the ivory coast is a matter in which europe clearly has a responsibility. shipping is notoriously complex in terms of ownership and management and is almost by definition a cross-border business. in the course of two months, the ship proba koala called at the ports of gibraltar, algeciras, amsterdam, skaw, paldiski, dover and venkspits. the vessel is also flying the flag of panama, is owned by a greek company, is chartered by trafigura bv of the united kingdom and has a russian crew. trafigura bv has a post office box in the netherlands. this degree of complexity requires clarity and unambiguity in regulation. if the european union is to act effectively, it must meet these conditions quickly. only by acting decisively can europe demonstrate why further european integration is important to us all.

member of the commission. mr president, the twelfth conference of the parties to the united nations framework convention on climate change in nairobi is the first such conference to be held in sub-saharan africa, the poorest region of the world with which the european union has special political relations. it is therefore natural that the conference should focus on the needs of developing countries. the european union will emphasise that the fight against climate change can be combined with rapid economic growth. the commission also stressed the need to integrate emission reduction policies into decision-making processes in key sectors. the development of the economies of asian, african and south american countries, security of energy supply and improvements in air quality are, for example, important parallel benefits of action to combat climate change. i believe that better international understanding of the dangerous effects of climate change, and a greater interest in addressing the needs of developing countries, will increase the chances of reaching an agreement for the period after 2012. the european union has four specific priorities in nairobi. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the fund will be financed by contributions from the clean growth mechanism and is estimated to yield more than eur 350 million over the period 2008-2012. secondly, to reach agreement on the list of activities under the five-year work programme on adaptation. the programme contains a number of activities aimed at improving our resilience, such as further improving our knowledge of the negative consequences of climate change, its socio-economic impacts and the vulnerabilities of our economies and societies to climate change. thirdly, to facilitate greater access to the clean development mechanism by the poor countries of africa and other parts of the world. we must address the issue of fair distribution of the clean development mechanism projects, because only a small percentage of all projects are allocated to africa. fourthly, the debate on the period after 2012, which will continue, but will not end in nairobi. i do believe that we have a lot of work to do. there are many opinions on what should be done. developing countries are not comfortable with the prospect of having to step up their efforts to combat climate change. at the same time, many developed countries are still reluctant to take the necessary action to reduce the greenhouse gas emissions needed to help limit the average global temperature increase by 2 degrees celsius. in nairobi, the european union will continue to make a strong case that the fight against climate change and the millennium development goals are not only compatible but also interdependent. in nairobi we want to ensure, on the one hand, a clear, concrete and balanced approach and steady progress in the ad hoc working group on article 3 (9) in the dialogue taking place within the convention. the second is to reach agreement on how to revise the kyoto protocol, on the basis of article 9. but we must look beyond nairobi, as europe needs to step up its efforts to convince its partners around the world to continue their efforts to reduce greenhouse gas emissions beyond 2012. it is very positive, ladies and gentlemen, that 10 members of parliament are part of the community delegation and i personally look forward - as has been the case over the last two years - to working closely with them. i will see that they are fully informed and that all available information is forwarded to them immediately. we will be addressing the media together and i hope that the members of parliament will have contact and dialogue with representatives of other countries during the conference. in this way we will be able to convey to a global audience our ambition and our shared vision for a sustainable future for humanity.
president. - mr president, i have been following the debate on the report by mr al gore for some time. the climate is changing fast, faster than expected. ice sheets are disappearing, sea levels are rising, and the point of no return will soon be reached. then you expect a response from politicians. not just mourning al gore, but the beginning of a solution. nairobi is an opportunity to take up that challenge with strength. when we talk about the energy crisis and the climate problem, we must not be held hostage to pessimism. because solutions are possible. where can we find these solutions? first of all, in confidence in our own measures. i would like to reiterate my support for the emissions trading system. it's not perfect. and i am now looking particularly at the commission, which should ensure that the national allocation plans are credible and that over-allocation is a thing of the past. in the slightly longer term, we should think about auctioning emissions allowances. we are therefore avoiding windfall profits for the electricity companies and the all too generous allocation of free emission allowances. secondly, i am in favour of large-scale investment in the development of a new clean energy source. there are many innovative opportunities in addition to the existing initiatives. blue energy from the mix of salt and fresh water can provide energy. the ladder windmills with high-altitude kites that work like a windmill and generate energy. energy-producing greenhouses and houses, geothermal, solar mirrors, there's a lot of potential. we must seize these opportunities through technological innovation. money must come from governments, but eventually also from the auctioning of emission allowances. thirdly, we must take forward cooperation with countries like china. every month, a new power plant opens in china. china needs clean coal technology, and we have to provide it. technology transfer can persuade china to commit after 2012. and if china joins, the united states' participation will also be closer. fourthly, we must invest generously in the adjustment fund. europe has a historic responsibility for climate change and it is our the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. now every political movement is cheering the return of freight transport by water. this consensus is based on different visions, as was shown in this parliament in the debate on the transport white paper 2010 in february 2003. for my group and for some others, water transport is an alternative to ever more and ever wider motorways. it's a way of saving space and the environment. another view, on the other hand, is that we should have more of everything, including an ever-increasing number of motorways. the creation of excess capacity gives operators greater freedom to choose the most profitable mode of transport at any given time within that excess capacity. an example of overcapacity is the construction of the expensive betuwelijn, a rail freight ten project in the netherlands, alongside the navigable rivers waal and lek, downstream of the rhine. more important than such projects, which are likely to be underused, is the widening of the narrow canals in northern france linking belgium and the netherlands and the restoration of the danube transport, which has been severely reduced since the 1999 bombings, between hungary and the black sea. my group opposes overcapacity, even on waterways, at the expense of space, nature and the environment. river adaptation has implications for surface values, biodiversity, drinking water supply and flooding of surrounding lowland. water transport does not eliminate all the risks of pollutants and hazardous substances. their transport poses dangers not only to the river itself but also to the downstream habitats and natural areas. it takes more than cleaner engines and low sulphur fuel. together with the green group, we are proposing infrastructure charges to promote fair competition between different forms of transport, better environmental impact assessment of european development plans, tightening up legislation on the transport of dangerous goods and combating the flagging of inland vessels, which has negative effects on social, safety and environmental standards. only if our water supply meets such quality requirements will its growth be a step forward.

i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in the preparation of the report, but that it should be a matter of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy to decide on the matter. i see a clear parallel with the discussion we had on services of general economic interest, on which we decided at the last plenary session. in both cases, the issue was how to provide greater legal certainty to local authorities, service providers and citizens. that can conflict at times. on the one hand, the rules of the internal market, on the other, the general interest that these authorities should serve their citizens. now it is often the case that the court of justice, the judge, has to intervene or that the commission gives interpretations. the important message in both these dossiers has been that we really want to cut political knots on this through the codecision procedure and that it is also important that we ask the commission and really insist that it come up with legislative initiatives that we can decide on in codecision with this parliament and with the member states and their parliaments. that is the main message i would like to convey to the commission again with this report.
i have negotiated with mrs handzlik on this matter on behalf of the pse in the committee on the internal market, and it is a pity that she is no longer really participating in the debate, but i think i should say again that we were very much aware of the problems raised by her and by many colleagues in my group from the new member states, and that we of course recognize that unnecessary barriers, unnecessary bureaucratic obstacles to the free movement of services and the free movement of workers must be removed. on the other hand, i have also had enough experience of what is happening in the labour market legally, illegally, semi-legally to know that people are indeed being exploited there and that cross-border possibilities of posting are also being used to evade regulations. i would therefore like to reiterate very clearly that we should not throw away the child with the bath water, but that we should look for a good balance; i would like to point out once again to mr clark, who does not seem to know how these arrangements work, that there is a principle of subsidiarity, that the labour law is governed by the rules of the country of employment and that member states are completely autonomous in this respect and that the posting directive is only intended as an exception to that rule, to regulate situations in which workers are posted across the border and are allowed to work temporarily on their own terms, but with some minimum protections. it is precisely the control of this, and the fact that it is so complex, that makes it necessary to have rules to have possibilities for control, better possibilities for labour inspectorates to cooperate, and that also makes it necessary for us to have that balance. but i would also like to stress, in the context of the services directive, that there is no hierarchy of rules governing that labour law, that member states are autonomous in this respect and that the rules on the free movement of services do not override this.

i think it's unique that you do, too. yet much of the european central bank's decision-making takes place in a dark room. now, of course, we do not want the whole european parliament and the bbc to be present at the meetings of the ecb's executive board, but we do want to know what arguments are in favour of or against a decision taken and whether or not that decision was taken unanimously, so that the market can better assess monetary policy. finally, mr president, we are calling for a reform of the procedure for appointing the members of the executive board of the ecb. we believe that the current de facto distribution of ecb seats based on the size of the member states should be abandoned. it doesn't have to be a big public debate, but we really think that choosing from multiple candidates is a better system.

in writing. the grech and elles rapporteurs are right to call for greater efficiency in the handling of the 2007 budget. the setting of priorities is also a necessary and sensible element in the approach of the rapporteur, mr elles. this does not automatically mean that more money should go to the priority budget lines. the non-priority budget lines deserve rather a more critical approach. unfortunately, most of the amendments are still in favour of increasing the budget. i would also like to make a specific comment on multilingualism. it is good that attention is also being paid to improving efficiency. however, the focus on cost reduction must not be allowed to jeopardize multilingualism. all in all, it is good that, following the council's position, more attention has been paid to efficient and more economical farming. that is why i support mr grech's resolution.
in writing. europe must keep its distance from syria. not because the us president bush says that this country is part of the axis of evil, but because it oppresses its own people and creates unnecessary hardship for others. syria has been a dictatorship for many years under the leadership of father and son el assad. many of the country's inhabitants were forced to flee abroad, for otherwise they would be imprisoned or be completely unaccounted for. opposition cannot organize internally. not only political opponents but also kurds and christians feel threatened. in neighbouring lebanon, syria has been responsible for years of military occupation, political assassinations and the arming of hezbollah. in the neighbouring country of israel, syria is seen as the most unreasonable neighbour who never wants peace and reconciliation, and one can abuse syria's behaviour as one of the arguments for not working towards a peace agreement with a neighbouring palestinian state. syria recently secretly extradited a dutchman of iranian origin to iran. the eu's position on syria is still too focused on good relations with all the mediterranean countries and too little on democracy and human rights.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the situation in tibet is not getting better, it's getting worse. maybe that's our own fault, too. before 1951, european countries accepted that tibet should be part of china, even though it was not on maps. this continued when, in the 1950s, the people's republic of china effectively took control of this inhospitable area by military means and the dalai lama fled to india. tibet is now more easily accessible from china via the world's highest railway. foreign climbers have recently witnessed the brazen shooting of residents trying to flee the country. the government and probably the vast majority of the chinese public view tibet as a largely unoccupied territory to be colonized by chinese from the most densely populated areas. the chinese economy is now more intertwined with the european economy than ever before, and this gives europe a powerful lever. the question is whether we want to use that drug. without pressure, the oppression will continue.
president. - i should like to begin by congratulating mrs lienemann on her excellent report. she has written a really excellent report. it is not easy for fish to survive in the seas and oceans surrounding the european union. overfishing and pollution have taken their toll. that's it. so we need to take drastic measures now to prevent fish becoming extinct by 2040. this prediction of scientists is an important warning. the marine strategy is a good initiative, but i think the directive is still too weak. first of all, fisheries reserves must be established. the establishment of reserves at spawning grounds enables fish to reproduce undisturbed. it's been proven to work in new zealand and australia. if we introduce sustainable fishing methods in addition to reserves, we can secure the future of fisheries in the waters around the european union. it is also important to deal with the exotic pollutants and the problem of ballast water. the japanese oyster has found a pleasant climate in the north sea with no natural enemies, but this oyster is actually growing too well. ballast water can be easily treated and this is a joint task. the marine directive forces member states to think about how to improve the environmental status of the seas and how to work together better. it's in the best interest of all of us. i wholeheartedly support mr lienemann's report and i hope that you will all do the same.

i would like to thank mr freitas, the president, for the way he has presented the initiative of the european christian democrats, and i would like to thank the commissioner for opening the debate on the future of dairy. because let's face it, the situation has changed over the years. the cost of milk production has risen considerably as a result of the high cost of quotas, and at the same time european dairy policy is becoming increasingly stagnant. we have lost 60% of our exports to the world market, and the european union and the european dairy sector have lost 4% of world production. so with global demand for dairy increasing much faster than production, europe would do well to face the situation and consider what needs to happen after 2015. on the one hand, there are entrepreneurs who want to grow and cannot because of the high quota costs. on the other hand, there are areas in europe where the milk quota is already being exceeded. i am thinking, for example, of the united kingdom. so all the more reason to look now at how we can use a good road map after 2015 to come up with a better and more effective system. i would like to make a few points on this point. first of all, the milk quota must be made internationally tradable. the superlevy should be reduced when the quota is used but the sale is made virtually without support to the european union. we can also think of the harmonization of the milk quota at european level. if one country does not milk the quota and the other can use a little more, we can then balance the case just fine. the commission has the netherlands i would very much welcome the commissioner to follow that line. i must compliment her, also because some of these proposals have been put forward by her dairy director, mr rasmussen. the report by mr pimenta, which i am referring to, is a report which has been approved unanimously by the committee on agriculture. we thought that additional aid for rural development in portugal was justifiable, although of course it is not ideal. however, it was part of a whole package of measures agreed by the council in december. the european parliament did not consider it necessary to change the financial perspective during the negotiations. but again, it's not ideal and it has to be exceptional. however, something similar has been agreed for several countries. if i may, mr president, i would also like to say something about the goepel report. the european parliament's committee on the financial perspectives, which was subsequently approved by the whole of the european parliament, made it clear that the european parliament had reservations about this voluntary modulation. it is therefore surprising that the commission has come up with a proposal so quickly. it has simply been following the orders - or whatever you want to call it - of the council. i disagree with the commissioner that there is no danger of renationalising the european agricultural policy. i think that's a very dangerous trend. the most important thing for the european farmer is the single market of 500 million consumers, and we must try to preserve it as much as possible. nor has there been any examination of the effects of the policy - mr goepel has already said that. it is possible that by 2013 the average farmer in europe will receive a third less as a result of all sorts of measures than he was presented with in 2004. is that the kind of trustworthy government the european union wants to be? what are the consequences of that? can the farmers survive in certain regions and so on? we have not received an analysis of it and it seems to me that the commission is obliged to analyse what the implications are if a proposal is made. one of the important rights of this parliament is budgetary law - mr bösch will no doubt deal with this later. we have approved the financial perspective. the financial perspective contains precise figures. so much for this, so much for that and, among other things, so much for market measures in the framework of the european agricultural policy and so much for rural policy. if that voluntary modulation is carried out, it means a change in those figures. is that not a change in the financial perspective? in my opinion, yes. and a change in the financial perspective implies approval by the budgetary authority and that means approval by both the european parliament, the commission and the council. now i am not at all sure that the european parliament will agree to that. i shall therefore vote against the european commission's proposals on voluntary modulation.
president. - we are having a debate as we have for many years. some elements are always the same. again, no declaration of trust, much to the displeasure of anyone who has a warm heart for european cooperation. and again, it is not entirely clear to us why that declaration of assurance is not given. every year we ask the court of auditors for more precise information and every year there are again those typical phrases in the report - i will quote a few: 'the court of auditors concluded that, overall, cap expenditure still shows material errors.' the commission has already made a number of proposals. in writing. the dutch members of the pse group are naturally in favour of more modulation, of transferring funds from the first to the second pillar of the agricultural budget. modulation is an important improvement from a social democratic point of view, because the current income subsidies are unjust. the biggest farmers get disproportionately more than the others. they are also inefficient because they do not increase the farmer's purchasing power sufficiently. rural development offers more opportunities for all rural dwellers. young and old need good facilities, strong infrastructure and a diverse economy. rural development is also in the interest of the vast majority of farmers. voluntary modulation is less desirable than compulsory modulation because of the potential distortion of competition between european farmers. but the labour party can also agree to it, because it is a step in the right direction. the council has, however, taken a decision without following the most basic democratic path. the european parliament has not been consulted on this major budgetary intervention. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support them. we therefore abstained.

on behalf of the pse group. – mr president, this is a very important, historic debate in this house. i should like to thank the rapporteurs for all the work they have done, and commissioner rehn for the clever and efficient way in which he has prepared the process that will now lead to the accession of romania and bulgaria to the eu on 1 january 2007. this is also an opportunity to say a final word of welcome to both countries as members of the european union. it has been a long and arduous process, but it is all very rewarding in the end, especially for the two countries, which were not able to join in 2004. they can now join the other central and eastern european member states as full participants in the union. we asked a lot of these countries, and we can see the results of their efforts, especially over the past year. i should like to congratulate both countries for this result. throughout the process, our group has been very supportive of the accession of both countries on the earliest possible date. i would also like to welcome our bulgarian and romanian colleagues, who have been observers so far, but they will be full members of this house as of 1 january. i am convinced that they will continue their constructive work after that date. i personally am also happy for another reason: i have been participating in so many debates on romania and bulgaria, and i am happy that this burden will now shift to other committees in this parliament! this is the final debate prepared by the committee on foreign affairs on romania and bulgaria. that is also a good thing. nevertheless, parliament will remain involved on the basis of our reports, and on the basis of the progress report that the commission will have to present in future on the homework still to be done by the two countries. i am quite convinced, however, that both governments and parliaments are well aware of what has to be done. finally, as my last remark on romania and bulgaria, i should like to welcome all of you, and i am quite sure that both countries will play a constructive role in the future development of our european union.
the rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on budgets. president, i would like to thank the two rapporteurs, edward mcmillan-scott and hélène flautre, for the way in which they have taken over the opinions of the committee on budgets. the committee on budgets is concerned with two points. firstly, the accounting and financial framework 2007-2013. the budgetary committee wants us to be able to monitor the expenditure of the money. secondly, the rights of the european parliament in this area must be safeguarded, both in consultation with the ep and in the implementation of the policy, which, according to the commission's statement, must be carried out under proper democratic control. we are very pleased that the committee on foreign affairs and the rapporteurs have adopted this proposal. the committee on budgets also considers it very important that money should also be made available from other important instruments, for development, but also for partnerships with neighbouring countries and pre-accession. if that happens, if this instrument is used in that way, i am convinced that when we speak about this programme in the committee on budgetary control in five years' time, we will be able to give a discharge in this area.
mr president, tomorrow this parliament will vote on a proposal to liberalize the european fireworks market. no one in europe has asked for this except the fireworks industry, whose hand is clearly visible both in the commission document and in the report. there are major differences between the laws on fireworks in the european countries. in my own country, the netherlands, we have been particularly careful since the terrible disaster at a fireworks factory in enschede. we are not waiting for europe to impose harmonization on us, so that we can no longer impose our own stricter requirements on fireworks. the safety of citizens must, in my view, always be put before the profits of the fireworks industry. that is why i shall also be recommending to everyone tomorrow that they vote against this report.
the research programme is one of the main pillars of the lisbon strategy. so it's fine that more money is being spent. it's just a pity it's not nearly enough. it is important that a lot of money be spent on health and energy research. there is a great need for innovative research in these fields. energy has been allocated 2 billion, and at least half of that is going to renewable energy and energy efficiency. there are promising ideas that deserve further research and it is important that member states work together much more closely in this area. in health, it is important to address neglected diseases and antibiotic resistance. it's world health day this week, and it's a good time to raise money for health research. it is also good that 15% of research funding is reserved for small and mediumsized enterprises. but then, of course, the design of the research programmes must be made much less bureaucratic. so far, participation by smes has been limited because there was no way through. hopefully, this can change now. finally, commissioner, i should like to draw your attention to the following: fisheries research is of great importance to protect fish stocks and prevent overfishing. unfortunately, fisheries is not a separate theme in the seventh framework programme. what guarantees do you give that there will be sufficient attention and money for this important research work?
president, minister, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, this seventh framework programme is indeed a milestone, both in terms of the budget and in terms of overall policy. as the epp-ed shadow rapporteur on capacities, i note that we have succeeded in obtaining additional budgets, additional possibilities for the use of structural fund resources for research infrastructure at regional level. the budget has thus been increased by many billions with the rapporteur mr buzek, mr prodi, mrs hübner and the committee on regional policy of this parliament. it can be decentralized. and together with the risk-sharing facility at the eib, it can be invested heavily. the recently published research and research infrastructure roadmap sets the course for this. i see all over europe this kind of joint investment, high-tech campuses and open innovation emerging. we must, in the face of fierce global competition, achieve greater focus, greater mass and greater coherence between research and infrastructure. i'm closing up. and that way, we can get to the centers of excellence for nanotechnology, microelectronics, healthcare, and so on. not a brain drain, but a brain gain.
the european parliament must maintain and seek to strengthen its leading role in patient rights in europe. there is still much to be gained in the field of patient information. the european citizen has the right to be better informed, as the right to information for the patient is enshrined in the european charter of the rights of hospital patients. patients should be better informed about possible medicines and different treatments. why would the doctor have a monopoly on this information? the provision of more information to consumers is, if i may say so, a liberal wish in the best sense of the word. and in the field of medicine, important developments have taken place in recent years. the patient lineup has changed radically. more and more of them feel that they have a right to more information and more and more of them actively seek information about the treatment of their illness. patients want to be (co-) responsible for their own treatment. and they often seek out other patients who suffer from the same disease to share information. when citizens are fully informed, they may make different choices. the citizen has every right to do this.
the world health fund is already the largest fund in the world in the fight against these diseases. i am proud that 65% of the contributions to the eu fund come from the eu. president barroso has made a strong statement and commitment to the global fund. that is most commendable. europe has a responsibility to the rest of the world and this fund is fulfilling that responsibility. but, mr. chairman, i must point out to you and to those present a great scandal. next year barroso is in danger of not being able to honour his promise to the global fund. the money from the european development fund that was reserved for the global fund before 2007 has already been spent on other things. the commission now wants to compensate for this budgetary gap by taking money out of the general development budget. that means: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. mr president, it is not possible to explain this to all those people who rely on our support and our commitments. how is it possible that the commission is asking us to choose between the fight against deadly diseases and all the other things we stand for, which we have set out in the budget, on the one hand, and the contributions to achieving the millennium goals on the other? in matters of life and death, it should not be either/or, but and/or. if you can set aside 1 billion for infrastructure and 3 billion for good governance from the european development fund for the coming years, there may still be a little limitation of 100 million a year for the world health fund. president barroso must release that 100 million, not for education, child labour or women's care, but from the european development fund. then just a little less infrastructure, then just a little less on those other points. if you set your priorities right, you are doing well, and if you are not, you are doing well in a way that neglects good planning and thereby seriously harms business. i call on the commission to review this matter firmly.
in writing. last year, the idea was that bulgaria's accession to the eu would be the least problematic. on the other hand, romania's accession would cause or perpetuate major problems, which would have to be postponed until 2008. parliament reserved the right to co-decide on such a deferral for romania, but waived it for bulgaria. the problems in bulgaria have not diminished. with the knowledge we have now, parliament would have agreed the same right of deferral for bulgaria last year. although it was not at first obvious that the newcomers would be admitted on 1 january 2007, opposition has melted surprisingly quickly over the past months. the aim of completing the enlargement sought after the fall of the iron curtain is overcome by practical objections. a majority in this parliament decided today that bulgaria can be a member of the eu in 32 days. although no one is enthusiastic, the prevailing view is that another year of delay will not solve anything. we think that the voters underestimate how negatively this enlargement will be felt in member states which face greater burdens and how unfavourably it may affect the further admission of newcomers.
in writing. our group did not speak again yesterday on enlargement, because the outcome is already decided in advance. a majority in this parliament decides today that romania can be a member of the eu in 32 days. the delay, which prevented romania from joining the other 10 countries on 1 may 2004, does not appear to be sufficient to bring romania up to the original expectations. although no one is enthusiastic, the prevailing view is that another year of delay will not solve anything. we think that the voters underestimate how negatively this enlargement will be felt in member states which face greater burdens and how unfavourably it may affect the further admission of newcomers. public opinion increasingly associates enlargement with low wages and poor reception of migrant workers, or with crime, corruption, environmental pollution, roma discrimination, fascist movements, legal uncertainty, begging street children and financial scandals. that is an argument against the applicant countries, croatia and macedonia. the fact that these countries are likely to be less problematic than romania and bulgaria does not count. on 15 december 2004 we voted in favour of the principle that romania and bulgaria could be admitted in the future, but today we conclude that admission in 2007 is not justified.
in writing. i voted against the accession treaty for romania and bulgaria. the state of affairs in the field of corruption and the rule of law is such that the criteria are not met. the question was whether one year's deferral could be granted to one or both countries. the possibility of postponement was not used again, although objectively, according to the commission's report, there was every reason to do so. that is why i am voting against the moscovici and van orden reports again today. the voters have repeatedly asked for the criteria to be applied strictly and this has been promised time and again, but not done. that is why i am personally voting against today, although i am in favour of romania and bulgaria being admitted to the eu in the long term.
in writing. in addition to the vote on the framework programme for scientific research, the specific programmes will also be voted on. the council will vote on the compromise between the european parliament and the council on the framework programme. the specific programmes are also voted on as a whole. the exception is the specific programmes 'ideas', 'cooperation' and 'capacities', which have been further amended. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'common carrier' to cover all transport services, including the carriage of goods by road. 'certain research areas, such as activities aimed at reproductive cloning of humans or the modification of human genetic material, are not eligible for community funding. the commission also confirmed that it would continue to adhere to current practice and would not submit to the regulatory committee any proposals for projects involving research activities involving the destruction of human embryos, including stem cell production. although this stage of research is therefore excluded from funding, the community will finance the subsequent stages of research involving human embryonic stem cells.' the ppe-de therefore votes in favour of these amendments in 'ideas' and 'capacities'. however, for 'cooperation', the shadow rapporteur indicated free voting. we shall vote in favour of that.
