i am very pleased that the commission has taken this opportunity to make a statement on the subject of the directive on the protection of the environment. first of all, i would like to congratulate my colleague, mr guido sacconi, on the result achieved. he's talked a lot in the last few weeks about his dog, who he loved so much that he would go to reach a little less and stay home a little more. that dog, guido, must have been a terrier, witness the tenacity with which you handled this case; it wasn't always easy for the shadow reporters to get what they wanted. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the safety of the products concerned is not compromised. and that includes all substances produced in quantities of 1 tonne or more per year. others make us believe otherwise. reach also replaces a complex of very opaque legislation that has made the european market unworkable. mr president, what will have to happen is that we will make reach work. it's important enough, 1.3 million people work in the chemical industry, 27,000 mostly small, but also big companies are working with reach, are going to work with reach; the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud. it is therefore important that we put a good legislative package on the table. the compromise we have before us is, in my view, the best balance we could find after very long and very difficult negotiations which brought the extremes together in the middle. the compromise is very precarious, protecting on the one hand man, the environment and consumers, the limited animal testing, but it also offers an opportunity to create an optimal climate for european industry; we can tell the finnish minister that we are ahead, but we must make sure that we do not become deadbeats. reach transfers responsibility from the member states to the companies themselves, the most important improvement over the common position. we have better protection of confidential company data, registration is more in line with the nassauer/sacconi package - it is a pity that this has not been done - we minimise red tape, which is important for the epp; a good balance has also been struck in the area of authorization and substitution. it is now up to the european commission to ensure that it becomes a workable entity.
i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr sacconi, for his report. under his leadership, the european parliament has achieved the maximum possible result in the negotiations. it was difficult to negotiate with a divided parliament and a council that clung to its common position. the fact that after difficult negotiations on several fronts we have finally reached a result with which we can live is to be congratulated. the fact that both the environmental movement and the chemical industry are unhappy with the compromise is understandable, but in this case it is clear that the better is the enemy of the good. no compromise at second reading had led either to the adoption of a virtually unchanged common position or to a long conciliation process which might have resulted in the withdrawal or rejection of the whole proposal. in this case, half an egg is better than an empty shell. i would like to make one further point, namely that i believe that member states should be able to pursue a stricter environmental policy than is currently established; this is particularly true when countries such as sweden and denmark already have such stricter legislation. i shall therefore support my amendment on this subject. i am sorry, mr president, that i will not be able to attend the rest of the debate because of a vote in libe, and i would like to apologise to the commissioner and to mr sacconi for that.
mr president, after almost four years and thousands of amendments, reach is finally a reality. all credit, of course, goes to our rapporteur, mr guido sacconi, who has brought all this to a very good conclusion. the result may indeed be there. replacement has become one of the objectives of reach. reach is not only about knowledge of thousands of chemicals, but also about the substitution of 2,500 dangerous substances. it is high time that dangerous substances were finally disqualified. there is a great deal of concern about dangerous substances. the increase in cancer, allergies and fertility problems in humans and animals is linked to dangerous substances. the majority of the chemical industry is doing its utmost to handle dangerous substances as carefully as possible, but unfortunately careful handling of substances is not always taken for granted. of course, i understand the industry's view that replacement is not something that can be achieved in a hurry. it is good that the new agreement has built in a little more flexibility. the duration of a dangerous substance authorization is now being considered on a case-by-case basis. that's profit for producers. there must be a research plan to develop safe alternatives. that's good for the environment, but it's also good for innovation. the challenge of removing the most dangerous substances from the production process and from products is immense. reach is the catalyst for this innovation which benefits both our competitiveness and the environment. of course, everything now depends on proper implementation. it is important to ensure that smes can make the change. in seven years, there will be a first review. then we can see if it is still possible to get more substances under reach. then we can see if the hormone disruptors can still be put under replacement regimens. i hope that this will also address the duty of care and that the obligation to inform workers and consumers can be improved. until then we will have to work with this and i think a very good compromise has been reached.
i would also like to thank the rapporteur, and certainly his staff, for their commitment to this report. it has been shown that it is an interesting subject, but also a controversial one, and that the committee on agriculture has at least made a correction to the commission's proposal. this means that article 1 (1) (b) of the 2002 european directive should be deleted according to the proposal which was issued following a judgment of the european court of justice. mr president, it was clear from the discussion that there are quite different views on the labelling of compound feedingstuffs. it is not a question of the exact wording, but it is also a question of whether we really want to have a debate on the open declaration clauses in the legislation in question. i think that this open declaration should be condemned, since the current system is not workable and is still not transparent, because eventually labels will still be issued with 110% ingredients. in addition, too little consideration is given to the intellectual property of the feed industry, which is forced by current regulations to make its recipes public. that is bad for industry, but also bad for the farmer, because it also means that our competitors outside the european union know exactly what we are doing. in addition, the current regulations reduce the incentives for innovation in the feed industry. and farmers are not getting any benefit from this either. unfortunately, it turned out that only the deletion of article 1 (1) (b) was at issue and that the other articles in the legislation could not be amended according to the legal services of the european commission, the council and the european parliament. i must say that the coordinators of all the parties shared that view. the european people's party therefore calls for an early review of the whole of the feed legislation, not least because it will provide absolute clarity. mr president, in view of the amendments, i must say that the european people's party is surprised that the liberal group has tabled an amendment to another article, whereas their own coordinator for agriculture has declared this amendment inadmissible. what we want as the european people's party is a rapid revision and we welcome the council's commitment to this point, to complete a complete revision in the first half of 2007; i assume that the commissioner will follow this proposal from the commission to the council.
i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr president, for his absence, but i have the impression that, despite being a member of this parliament for many years, he is still as enthusiastic about certain subjects as he used to be. if we look at the past, the european union has seen a number of unprecedented livestock feeding scandals that have left a deep impression on politics. it is therefore obvious that we cannot be too cautious in the animal feed sector and there is every reason to monitor it closely. the big question is whether we will solve that by stating exactly the percentages or the quantities on the label. that is probably why it is not effective. it is changing every day and i know very few farmers who study every label before they buy feed. what is important for the average farmer, the average livestock farmer, is what the energy content of the feed is, what the protein content is and what the reputation of the company is. what is the role of government in all this? is it the government that should always have some kind of insight into what a particular manufacturer is doing and how to control that? so i think it is important that every feed manufacturer in europe has the data available at all times to show to anyone who is interested. that does not mean that all business secrets should suddenly be on the street. on the contrary, i do not think we can demand that. there are certain experiences which are good for a particular factory and which can be maintained, but i think the european commission should be able to say that it should always be able to check, as a public authority or as a national authority, what the composition of the feed is and what its possible impact on public health is. one of the interesting aspects of the court of justice's judgment was that the requirements of the directive as we know them were completely out of proportion to the main objective of the directive, which is to improve public health. so my big question is: the current commission is very much in favour of less regulation. how does that fit in with the commission's earlier demands - perhaps even now, if i look at the proposal carefully - of those precise percentages? it doesn't go together. less regulation and accurate percentages? i also have serious doubts that the national legal authorities in seven of the member states have already declared the directive inadmissible. so i think the whole directive as it stands is a legal illusion; i therefore welcome the commission's proposal to come forward with a new proposal as soon as possible.
president. - since the bse crisis and the dioxin scandals, farmers and consumers have needed more reliable information than ever before on the origin and content of feedingstuffs. there is no clear regulation on this. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the animal health standards are complied with. it is very possible to reach a compromise and i therefore urge the european commission to come forward with a new proposal in 2007. the indication of all ingredients should be mandatory, but the percentages are only essential for the substances that determine the name of the product. the exact percentages do not always need to be given. the rules for animal feed need not be stricter than those for human food, but the information must be on the product itself for the users, the farmers and the consumers and not be kept silent until something goes wrong, because then it will be too late. the commission proposal should provide such clarity on ingredients. we need a transparent market, and farmers and consumers have a right to it, and the animal feed industry has a right to it. the conservatives and liberals in this house have unfortunately allowed themselves to be too much influenced by industry with their amendments. the eu will have to make a rapid choice of rules in the interests of consumers and farmers. without undue delay, every farmer wants to know exactly what feed his animals are getting. preventing a new crisis requires maximum transparency, which must be achieved in 2007.
the new development instrument will finally give us a development law that puts the millennium development goals at the centre. with these millennium development goals, we made a promise to millions of children in africa. a promise of education and health care. unfortunately, the bitter reality is that in recent years the mdgs have been lost sight of in africa rather than being brought closer. that is precisely why we have been so vigorous in the debate on the financial instrument before us. we have spent a year and a half negotiating this new law intensively, and it was not for nothing. it was not in vain. for the next seven years we will have one clear european development law, with a clear thematic and geographical programme - together worth almost 70 billion - that will give us direction. that puts an end to 16 different schemes that are difficult to implement and control. the new law sets out two clear priorities, the millennium development goals and, within them, education and basic health care, in short, the areas that really matter. the campaigns we have waged to achieve this have not been in vain. we have been working with school education unions and many other education organisations, including education international, to make education more central to european development programmes. the same is true of our actions with health workers to double spending on basic health care. finally, the european commission's commitment to achieve a minimum doubling of spending in 2008 and to spend at least 20% of the geographical budget on education and basic health care is now in place. this includes the thematic part of over 2.5 billion euros. and with this new financial instrument, europe can start making a difference in africa, together with the member states. there are still more than 45 million children who cannot go to school and millions of patients, mostly girls, who lack basic health care. they will have access to the education and health services to which they are entitled in 2015. this puts an end to the struggle which i have been waging with my group since i entered the european parliament in 1999. i thank all the european citizens and organisations who have helped me to do this. from the stop aids alliance to bono, thank you! as of 1 january 2007, we will begin a new chapter, namely the implementation of this law and all its principles and promises. the committee on development and cooperation will devote a large part of its parliamentary time to this. the commission has also been involved in the preparation of the national policy plans and in monitoring their implementation. we do not want a free market, but a 100% commitment from all parties, and we do not accept that the programming for the period up to 2008 has already been completed. consultation of civil society organisations and parliaments there and ngos and the european parliament here, should take place in accordance with the new arrangements. the commission is legally obliged to do so on 1 january. i assume that the commissioner would agree with me without further ado and would be prepared to explain that here without further ado. ten days ago, the funding of the global fund threatened to jeopardize the agreements made on the thematic part of the 2007 law. fortunately, in its letter of 8 december, the commission said that it would still respect the thematic programming agreement for 2007. it would be good if the commissioner would confirm that as well, in order to reply to the 23 ngos who wrote to president barroso urgently this weekend. i would like to congratulate mr president, mr mitchell, the council and the commission on this success. i also thank them very much for their thorough cooperation; i do hope that this house will reject the annulment amendment, because i think we have said, no more amendments, this is the law, let's make this a success.
mr president, development cooperation has been an important area of european cooperation since its inception, with its own commissioner, its own budget, its own committee in the european parliament and the european parliament having codecision on this point. the european union rightly feels a responsibility to contribute to peace and prosperity in countries outside the union as well. the commission's attempt to improve the work is a good thing. we invest a lot and the results are far from satisfactory. look at africa, for example, where poverty actually seems to be increasing. the achievement of the millennium development goals requires a lot of work. the commission's original proposal, mr president, which was intended to improve the situation, was extremely disappointing and not very credible. the development cooperation budget and the european parliament's control were both in danger. the negotiations were not easy, and i congratulate mr mitchell on the excellent work he has done to ensure that there is at least a separate development instrument with codecision and only article 179 as the legal basis. there is agreement on the priorities of the millennium development goals and within them priority for education and basic health care. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it is wrong to say that mr mitchell's amendments are against reproductive health. he says - and he reads his reasons for that in the amendments - that it is too important to end it in two sentences. and that's different. the new financing instrument for development cooperation will change the work of parliament. we will have to concentrate more on monitoring and implementation, on the execution of programmes. i hope we can count on the commission's commitment to cooperation and openness in this.
i would like to congratulate mrs klaß on the result achieved. although the groundwater situation can be very different and there are not always transboundary effects, it is positive that we now have a good groundwater directive. it has even succeeded in raising the ambition that the quality of groundwater should not deteriorate. so instead of moping with the faucet open, we're actually turning the pollution faucet off. i am glad that the directive takes account of the specific protection measures that some member states already have. i am referring in particular to denmark, which has strict standards for pesticides because groundwater is used directly as drinking water. although it is already in the treaty, it is good that this directive explicitly confirms that member states may take further measures. finally, i would like to say that the possibility of repealing the current nitrates directive should be seriously considered. the target, a limit value for nitrate, has already been set. the measurement method is now well regulated. the nitrates directive now only concerns the means of achieving that objective. i believe that the means are secondary to the end. the mother can throw away the bath water with a feeling of relief, since the child has now been washed. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the quality of bathing water is not affected by the pollution caused by nitrates.
in writing. socialists have a long tradition of international solidarity. they are not only standing up for human rights, peace, democracy, livelihood, fair sharing, public services and a clean environment for the people in their own town, region or state, but also for the people in their neighbouring countries and in other parts of the world. human rights for others are more important than our own economic benefits from cheap imports from countries where human rights are trampled. we have supported boycotts against the dictatorial minority regimes in south africa, the former rhodesia, the former portuguese colonies and pinochet's chile. by joining forces with resistance movements in those countries, we have been able to contribute to radical change. that is why we, the sp delegates in the netherlands, support the flautre/mcmillan-scott report, which concerns human rights in international conventions and relations with dictatorships. we are aware that this report can be misused to take measures against venezuela or other states where the population has opted for radical steps towards human equality, and perhaps even as a justification for humanitarian military interventions. we regret that a section of our gue/ngl group now seems to be choosing the principle of non-interference out of fear of this, and thus inadvertently also to avoid unacceptable dictatorships.
madam president, moving images have a great attraction for people and that is one explanation for the fact that more and more time is spent consuming audiovisual products. it is well known that such attitudes have an effect on human behaviour patterns. the content of the products offered can be useful, but can also be so distressing that many citizens avoid such products for ethical reasons. it is the duty of governments to protect the weak in society. it can do so by providing security in a physical sense, but also in a mental sense. in that respect, i welcome two parts of this report. firstly, by a stricter provision on jurisdiction. i think it is right that member states should have more influence on the provision of audiovisual services broadcast on their territory. each member state has its own identity. audiovisual services offered should be included. compliance with the rules of the country of establishment of the provider is not sufficient. the second point concerns the position of minors and vulnerable people. these groups are relatively easy to seduce into behaviour harmful to themselves and sometimes to their environment. it is up to the legislator to minimize the chance of damage.
it is important to promote the free circulation of european television programmes and to ensure that the production of these programmes is not hindered by excessively strict rules on advertising. the liberal group is committed to a realistic view of product placement. information is good, but consumers do not wait for a warning signal during a television programme. a warning before and after the end of a programme is sufficient. the revenue from advertising and product placement is needed to help finance european productions. the alternative is more cheap american productions, which are crammed with product placement. programmes aimed at children should be subject to stricter standards. the problem of obesity is increasing. given the high impact of advertising of unhealthy foods on children's menu choices, such advertising targeting this target group should be limited. i therefore call for the development of an effective code of conduct on advertising, product placement and other marketing of unhealthy drinks and food to children. finally, i continue to advocate a strong country of origin principle. television producers should not be subject to additional laws and regulations if they wish to offer their programmes elsewhere in the european union. we must increase the market for european audiovisual products.

mr. speaker, gender equality is a recurring theme in this house. apart from the fact that it is probably about equal treatment of men and women rather than equality, i think it is too easy to model this subject in the right way with a few big blows. in the last few decades, there has been a lot of attention given to women's rights, both healthy and unhealthy attention. this is not to say that there is no need for special attention in certain areas and in certain regions. the reason for this is that different cultures have had different views of role patterns over the centuries. its traces can still be found in the cultures of countries, including those within the european union. in my view, the need to deal with these differences wisely is necessary to achieve equal treatment of men and women in all countries and member states. i believe that the individual member states have a role to play in this. they are best placed to assess the local situation and take appropriate action. if a member state is not taking enough initiative, there are plenty of national and international organisations to encourage or even force concrete action in this area. in short, mr president, i am very much in favour of equal treatment and equal opportunities for men and women, but i do not see a role for a new european institution at the moment.
on behalf of the pse group president, cars need to be much cleaner, because the air quality is downright bad in many european cities. you can, because the technology is there! tomorrow we will vote on the euro 5 standards which will make diesel cars in particular much cleaner. i thank the rapporteur, matthias groote, for his efforts. the compromise has made a number of important improvements. but i do have some concerns. first of all, i'm not very happy with the procedure. the choice has been made for an agreement at first reading, and that is a procedure with little transparency, with decisions being taken in the back rooms. that would be defensible in a time of great urgency. this was not the case with euro 5, as the standards are only applicable from september 2009. a second reading would therefore have been preferable. we would have had the opportunity to reach a position in plenary before speaking to the council. secondly, i also have mixed feelings about the content. as i have said, president. - ladies and gentlemen, i will not repeat here how successful enlargement has been so far. that has already been done by many colleagues and also by the commission and the council, and it seems to be a bit of an open door in this house. but it is not an unnecessary luxury in a european union where more and more people are questioning the usefulness and necessity of enlargement. that said, it is nevertheless necessary and, in my view, possible to learn from the way we have done it so far. the commissioner has already mentioned it, and those lessons are in his report and they are also in the reports of this parliament. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware of the fact that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. don't give out a date too early, because that's against us and against the process. do not postpone difficult issues until the end and focus more on the execution than on the promise. in the debate on these lessons, the debate on absorption capacity, now called integration capacity, has suddenly emerged in the last year. it is a reaction by some supporters of further enlargement - and i count myself among them - to the fear of participating in that debate, because many opponents of further enlargement have used the concept of integration capacity as an argument against further enlargement. colleagues, let me be clear: the european union has a duty to ensure that the european union is not left to its own devices. let me pick two elements. institutional reforms: i am again a strong supporter of further enlargement with the countries of the western balkans and with turkey, but i too am convinced that this enlargement cannot take place on the basis of the present treaties and that we therefore need fundamental institutional reform. i just think that we should not be arguing in this house about how exactly we should do that. but it has to be done! president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0179/97). europe, previously divided into three economic blocs, had to be rapidly united within the european union. the plan for a limited first enlargement - which included estonia, the czech republic and cyprus but not latvia, slovakia and malta - was then replaced by a large enlargement. romania and bulgaria have been left behind, but they will be joining in three weeks' time. after the promise to welcome all the balkan states which have not yet joined, after the delayed start of negotiations with croatia and turkey and after the short-lived enthusiasm about the possible rapid accession of ukraine, moldova and belarus, there has been a remarkable lull. the negotiations with the applicant country macedonia have not even started. it seems that newcomers are not so welcome these days. governments fear that without a constitution the european union will become increasingly rigid and will have to redistribute more and more money. the public sees that there is a labour migration taking place, with people coming far from home to do dirty and unsafe work and to be poorly paid and housed. the adoption of the services directive has raised fears that good social legislation and good collective agreements in the old member states will have to compete in the near future with poorer arrangements in new or future member states. both the neoliberal policies within the european union and the lack of progress on democratisation and human rights in turkey meanwhile make further enlargement extremely unpopular with public opinion in the current member states. these two accidents force a slowdown and temporary transitional measures. the danger is that states which are temporarily denied access will meanwhile become increasingly dependent on the european union. it can hinder their own choices and their own development. my group wants the equivalence of potential members to be respected during a transitional period and financial support to be given to those who fall behind, in order to facilitate their future accession. we are obliged to the many citizens of our member states who come from the former yugoslavia and turkey to take seriously the desire to join those territories.
president. - mr stubb's report is disappointing for more than one reason. firstly, the report does not contribute in any way to breaking the current institutional impasse in which the union finds itself. the only thing that is the latest position of this house when this report is adopted is the reaffirmation of support for the european constitution and a summary of that constitution in paragraph 9. the lack of vision on the enlargement process is equally disturbing. i come now to my second criticism. paragraph 11 emphasises the need to make efforts to increase public acceptance of enlargement. however, the underlying factors of the declining support for enlargement are completely ignored. this report also maintains the taboo on the finality of the european union. the very lack of political leadership to define the borders of the european union is causing dissatisfaction and uncertainty about the enlargement process. in this report on the institutional aspects of the european union's capacity to integrate new member states, i also lack concrete recommendations on the incorporation of the enlargement process into the treaty. i propose that the various stages of the accession process be laid down in a treaty so that any increase in the status of aspiring members is based on uniform criteria. this will make the enlargement process more transparent and will enable the european parliament to give real substance to its mandate of parliamentary control at all the decisive moments of the accession process.

in writing. unlike the other dutch members of the pse group, i am in favour of amendments nos 170, 156 and 177. these amendments seek to restrict advertising of unhealthy food to children. i have the following arguments for that: 1. within the eu, 1 in 4 children is overweight. the cause is a combination of poor diet and insufficient exercise. overweight children get off to a bad start, both in their social and physical development. research shows that advertising has a powerful influence on consumer and purchasing behavior. the second. there is broad public support for limiting advertising of unhealthy foods. the food centre, the heart foundation and the consumers' association all call for a ban on television advertising of unhealthy food aimed at young children; the results of the food centre and the ad (saturday 2 december) surveys show that almost half of parents are in favour of restricting or banning aggressive advertising. the third. the question is, of course, whether there should be a legal ban or self-regulation. i prefer to go for self-regulation, unless it turns out that codes of conduct are not effective. the question is also whether a restriction or a prohibition should be regulated at national or european level. in any case, i am in favour of the right to do this at national level. we must not allow this television directive to lead to a situation where member states no longer have any say in this matter. the television directive is now before the house for its first reading. it is not a final text at this stage. it is therefore better at this stage to support the amendments which are heading in the right direction and which also offer the best chance of preserving the right of the netherlands to restrict advertising messages. a political statement that puts the health of children above the commercial interests of the food industry.

president of the council. mr president, the council has never discussed this matter because it does not fall within the council's competence. the treaties of the european union do not confer on the community or on the union any competence to regulate abortion within the union. the member states are therefore competent to lay down the relevant rules and to ensure that the measures they adopt are complied with within their territory. in areas outside the european union's competence, the eu cannot intervene in cases of maladministration resulting from differences in the legislation of the member states.

president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on transport and tourism, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) amending regulation (eec) no 1408/71 on the application of social security schemes to employed persons and their families moving within the community (com (97) 0406 - c4-0179/97). one is driving licence tourism, which allows people who are not entitled to drive to have a driving licence. the other is the dangerous situation for riders of two-wheelers in the middle of heavy car traffic. we could solve the second problem at national level, but not the first. we have long known that driving lessons are taken during holidays and that afterwards in the middle east or the caribbean a driving licence is issued which does not meet the requirements we have at home. it is even worse when someone whose driving licence has been revoked as a punishment for a serious traffic offence or reckless driving is given the opportunity to take a new driving test in another member state of the european union. the mutual recognition of national driving licences may then allow him or her to regain the right to drive a car in his or her own country despite the earlier revocation. such persons abuse the existence of state borders and pose a serious threat to road safety. it is a key task of the european union to combat crossborder nuisance and miscommunication, and the situation with regard to driving licences is one of them. it is very important to make it clear that the member states of residence are no longer prevented in any way from countering this dangerous abuse. the text now proposed as a result of the second reading rightly offers that possibility. more controversial is the way in which attention has been paid to accidents involving motorcyclists and mopeds. often it is young people who are either the victims themselves or the cause of an accident for others. some new riders of these two-wheelers want to be able to proudly show others that they are capable of life-threatening stunts. it is right that we try to protect potential accident-makers from themselves and also to protect other road users from the reckless behaviour of a few. but there's another side to this case. motorists and motorcyclists are rightly suspected of wanting the road for themselves. and that's why they'd rather lose the bikers than be rich. motorcyclists feel that it is discriminatory to require them to meet stricter requirements than motorists and, in practice, to be older before they can make their first legal ride. it is only at the age of 24 that one can get the right to ride the heaviest motorcycles without intermediate steps. this leads to conflicts of interest between young and old and between armored four-wheelers and unarmed two-wheelers. my group would like to support the most vulnerable group, the two-wheelers, but in countries with aggressive motorists this could lead to unnecessarily more victims falling within that group. perhaps we could better manage the solution of this sensitive problem on a member state-by-member state basis, but in an age of increasing cross-border traffic, it is becoming difficult to enforce it. three years of discussion on the proposal to harmonise national driving licences has so far resulted in the greatest common denominator of wishes of different member states and interest groups. not all those involved, such as motorcycle riders' organisations, are satisfied with this. the practical experience we will gain from this directive and the possible problems it may cause will probably give rise to amendments. however, my group believes that this proposal is better than continuing the present situation. the alternative advocated by mr bradbourn may work well in the splendid isolation of britain, but not for member states with land borders running through densely populated areas.
mr president, i wish to begin by congratulating mr grosch on a good result. in the negotiations with the council, he succeeded in getting many points of parliament’s position included in the final compromise. thus, in addition to the euro – our european currency – we will get a european driving licence. this will finally end driving licence tourism: if your licence is taken away in one country, you will be unable to get a new one in another country. it is also very good that fraud will be reduced, as we have succeeded in getting a limited period of validity from the member states. it is a pity for mr jarzembowski and other german colleagues, but it is good for road safety in europe. a compromise always means you win some and you lose some. it would have been fairer on the motorcyclists if they could get access to heavy motorcycles at the age of 21 instead of 24. you can drive a big suv or a ferrari when you are only 18 years old but, under this proposal, you have to wait another six years to get direct access to heavy motorcycles. that is disproportionate. if the commissioner really wants to do something about the safety of motorcyclists, he should improve research in that area and take measures based on that research. it will become clear that, in addition to good driving education, we need to improve our infrastructure – with better crash-barriers, for example – in order to improve safety for motorcyclists. finally, i am pleased about the introduction of extra possibilities for drivers with a category ‘b’ licence to tow heavy trailers. therefore, i strongly support this new directive for a european driving licence.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0176/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on transport and tourism, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) amending regulation (eec) no 4064/89 on the common organization of the market in fruit and vegetables (com (97) 0406 - c4-0414/97-97/016 (cns)) (rapporteur: mr de gucht). the problems were underestimated and the costs of change were given the most attention. it took the oil tanker disasters and the widespread destruction of the marine and coastal environment to make europe realize that it must not be left behind but must be at the forefront. single-hull tankers for heavy oil are dangerous and must be phased out as soon as possible. it was only after an american ban and the threat that all old tankers would be moved from america to europe that a majority for such a policy of extinction was formed here. unfortunately, the 2002 regulation is not yet clear enough. it is still possible that single-hull tankers flying the european flag, in particular 20 ancient greek tankers, are causing disasters in other parts of the world. this hole needs to be filled. europe does not want to have single-hull tankers of other countries in its ports, but we should not want to send single-hull tankers to the ports of other countries either. the freedom for existing single-hull tankers to continue to carry heavy oil until 2015 could pose major problems. that problem is not going to be solved now, but it's good that we're tackling the other problem now. the objections raised by the shipowners' association intertanko and the greek member state should be subordinated to the protection of our marine, coastal and port environment. the confederal group of the european united left/nordic green left supports this proposal also at second reading.
president. - i would like to congratulate mrs eluned morgan on her courageous and determined approach. i think it is good that we have three things to say today. firstly, that the climate problem deserves the highest priority. secondly, we are now seeing that the liberalization has not brought much good to consumers, that compliance with european rules is lacking and that the directive on tradable emission allowances needs to be amended in a number of respects. before we can ever speak of a common energy policy, compliance and implementation must be greatly improved. this is a task for the commission, but also for ourselves. we will have to constantly challenge the commission on the enforcement of european rules. thirdly, we must invest heavily in efficiency, renewable energy and innovative research in the coming years. we must put an end to subsidies for polluting energy. and renewable energy must be supported where necessary. so far, billions are still going to dirty energy, especially coal and nuclear, and we really need to stop that. i also welcome the report by mr werner langen on biofuels. it is important that europe should opt for mandatory blending for both petrol and diesel. targeted intervention is needed to ensure that biofuels do not lead to the clearing of rainforests and the loss of biodiversity or compete with the food supply.
in writing. the epp-ed group is keen that the council not only adopts a framework decision on data protection which takes due account of the opinion of the european parliament, but that it does so soon, as it undertook to do at the time of the adoption of both the sis ii package and the eu-pnr agreement. we believe strongly that a strong framework decision will greatly facilitate the adoption of the vis package. however, while we consider it highly desirable that a framework decision be adopted soon, we agree with the statement of the rapporteur that the adoption of the decision cannot be construed as a sine qua non for future work. the epp-ed group will do everything in its power to prove responsible and loyal partners in the legislative process.
author. president, countries ruled by rulers who derive their power not from their voters but from their machinery of violence can be extremely attractive to foreign investors. they are taking advantage of low wages, poor working conditions and a population that has no opportunity to protest. moreover, there can be no free movement to defend nature and the environment against short-sighted decisions that can increase the short-term profits of the company. if their main objective is to keep the cost of their business low and to take advantage of that low cost in the international market, they have been in the right place in burma for many years. such international support has enabled the military regime to defy domestic protests in its early years. since a number of major companies have pulled out of burma under pressure from protests in their home country, it has been quiet. the regime accepts the low living standards and criticism from outside as inevitable, without changing. the outcome of the elections continues to be ignored, the opposition leader is being detained and minority peoples are being oppressed and driven away as usual. international aid agencies are being opposed. this regime has not even managed to build its own political support and now serves no purpose other than its own preservation. without the isolation of burma by its neighbours, without a halt to arms supplies and without strong compliance with economic sanctions in the european union, this regime will be able to survive for an unnecessary long time. the resolution supports the measures which my group has been calling for for years and we are therefore happy to support it.
president. - i thank the finnish presidency for the good cooperation it has shown on the development instrument. we talked about reach, and we talked about the framework programme, a 17 billion dollar bill for the next few years around the millennium development goals; i'm very grateful for the way we've done that to each other. i have two questions. firstly, darfur, as my colleagues crowley and watson have also pointed out, it is somewhere intolerable that we in darfur find that these human rights violations are getting worse and worse and that we are unable to act effectively. i ask you once again to look at a special un initiative and to promote such an initiative. secondly, the enlargement: you say yes to enlargement, but you also make demands on the treaty. be clear about this. say, we need to be more democratic and effective before we can expand. we'd like to expand, but we need those requirements. be clear and show leadership in that, because otherwise it becomes and remains a creep-through-creep-through method, and that's not effective.
president. - i would also like to thank the finnish presidency. i have a question about relations with russia. trade is currently facing major problems, especially in the agricultural sector, because of veterinary or phytosanitary concerns. does the commission think that there are technical reasons for this or is there a political reason behind it, and if so, what is the commission going to do about it?
